Gravitational instantons and harmonic maps Thanks: * Department of Mathematics, University of California, Berkeley; mingyang_li@berkeley.edu Thanks: † Institute for Advanced Study in Mathematics, Zhejiang University; songsun@zju.edu.cn
Mingyang Li *, Song Sun †

Abstract
. We study the interaction between toric Ricci-flat metrics in dimension 4 and axisymmetric harmonic maps from the 3-dimensional Euclidean space into the hyperbolic plane. Applications include • The construction of complete Ricci-flat 4-manifolds that are non-spin, simply-connected, and with arbitrary b 2 b_{2} . Our method is non-perturbative and is based on ruling out conical singularities arising from axisymmetric harmonic maps. When b 2 b_{2} is at most 2 2 , these recover the Euclidean Kerr metrics and the Chen-Teo metrics; when b 2 b_{2} is bigger than 2 2 , these spaces are not locally Hermitian in any orientation and they provide infinitely many new diffeomorphism types of complete Ricci-flat 4-manifolds. They also give systematic counterexamples to various versions of the Riemannian black hole uniqueness conjecture. • A PDE classification result for axisymmetric harmonic maps of degree at most 1 1 , via the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz and the LeBrun-Tod ansatz, in terms of axisymmetric harmonic functions. This is motivated by the study of hyperkähler and conformally Kähler gravitational instantons.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, a
gravitational instanton
is defined as a complete oriented Ricci-flat 4-dimensional manifold
(M,g)
that satisfies the
finite energy
condition

(1.1)

\int_{M}|\Rm_{g}|^{2}\dvol_{g}<\infty.

This notion was first introduced by Hawking
[
Haw77
]
to study Euclidean quantum gravity. Mathematically gravitational instantons provide the most basic singularity models for Einstein metrics in Riemannian geometry. It is a fundamental question to construct and classify gravitational instantons.

In general, it is difficult to directly tackle the fully non-linear PDE system governing gravitational instantons. Almost all known gravitational instantons, up to orientation reversal, exhibit
special
geometry, i.e., they are locally
Hermitian
(indeed, hyperkähler or conformally Kähler) with respect to some complex structure. These special gravitational instantons are closely related to complex geometry and mathematical physics. They have been widely studied, and by now we have a satisfactory classification theory. However, little is known previously about gravitational instantons without special geometry.

The main goal of this paper is to construct gravitational instantons on infinitely many new diffeomorphism types of 4-manifolds. These new metrics are not locally Hermitian. The underlying oriented smooth 4-manifolds are

(1.2)

X_{n}:=\begin{cases}(S^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})\#k{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}\#k\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}},&\text{if }n=2k+1;\\
(S^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})\#k{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}\#(k+1)\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}},&\text{if }n=2k+2.\end{cases}

The metrics we construct are asymptotic to a flat quotient
(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{1})/\langle q_{\beta}\rangle
(for any
\beta\in(0,1)
), where
q_{\beta}
acts as a rotation by angle
2\pi\beta
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a translation by
1
on
\mathbb{R}^{1}
. These asymptotic models are referred to as the
\AFa
(asymptotically flat with rotation angle
2\pi\beta
; for a precise definition, see Definition
4.30
) models. Such models have cubic volume growth but the asymptotic cones at infinity are more complicated: when
\beta
is rational, the asymptotic cone is a 3-dimensional flat cone; when
\beta
is irrational, the asymptotic cone is the 2-dimensional half-plane.

Theorem 1.1
.

For each
n\geq 1
and
\beta\in(0,1)
, there is an
\AFa
gravitational instanton on
X_{n}
.

Some remarks are in order.

(1). Since
X_{n}
is non-spin, there is no hyperkähler gravitational instanton on
X_{n}
, with respect to either orientation. The metrics constructed above are Hermitian if and only if
n\in\{1,2\}
. In these cases, the metrics coincide with the known ones: a Euclidean Kerr metric (when
n=1
) or a Chen-Teo metric
[
CT11
]
(when
n=2
). Our method for proving Theorem
1.1
recovers them in these special cases and provides a geometric explanation for the existence of these metrics. When
n\geq 3
, previous classification results by the first author
[
Li23a
]
show that
X_{n}
cannot support a Hermitian gravitational instanton (for both orientations). Therefore, the metrics constructed in Theorem
1.1
provide new diffeomorphism types of smooth 4-manifolds that support gravitational instantons.

(2). Theorem
1.1
implies that with a fixed asymptotics at infinity, there is no uniform bound on the topological type of gravitational instantons. This is new and different from the hyperkähler and the Hermitian setting.

(3). Theorem
1.1
is related to questions of Gibbons
[
Gib79
]
, Lapedes
[
Lap82
]
and Yau (
[
Yau82
]
, Problem 116) regarding the Riemannian version of the black hole uniqueness conjecture. In general relativity, the black hole uniqueness conjecture states that the only asymptotically flat stationary vacuum Einstein space-time is given by the Kerr space-time. The naive Riemannian version of the conjecture was disproved by Chen-Teo
[
CT11
]
, who wrote down explicit families of asymptotically flat toric gravitational instantons on
X_{1}
. More recently, it was discovered by Aksteiner-Andersson
[
AA24
]
that the Chen-Teo spaces are Hermitian (or one-sided Petrov Type D, in the language of relativity); subsequently Biquard-Gauduchon
[
BG23
]
fits the Chen-Teo spaces in the framework of toric complex geometry via the LeBrun-Tod ansatz. In
[
AAD+23
]
a modified uniqueness conjecture was proposed, which says that asymptotically flat gravitational instantons must be Hermitian. Theorem
1.1
showed this is still too optimistic. In Section
8
we will discuss versions of the uniqueness conjecture which are more likely to hold.

(4). Our construction is non-perturbative and non-explicit. It remains an interesting question to understand the geometry of these metrics. When the parameters degenerate (i.e. as
\beta\rightarrow 0
or
\beta\rightarrow 1
), one can show that the metrics split in the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff sense to a finite union of Taub-NUT, Schwarzschild and Taub-Bolt metrics (under suitable orientations). See the discussion in Section
8
. For the Euclidean Kerr spaces, with some computation one can explicitly see the splitting into the union of a Taub-NUT space and an anti-Taub-NUT space (i.e., a negatively oriented Taub-NUT space), See Example
3.20
.

(5). In this paper we focus on constructing the special family of examples in Theorem
1.1
. It is possible that similar techniques can be exploited to construct
\AFa
gravitational instantons on non-compact 4-manifolds with other topologies. One may also attempt to apply the same strategy to construct gravitational instantons of other Asymptotic Types, for example, the
\ALF
or
\ALE
asymptotics. See Section
8
for more discussion.

To prove Theorem
1.1
, we study gravitational instantons with toric symmetry, using harmonic maps. In the Lorentzian setting there is a well-known reduction of the axisymmetric stationary vacuum Einstein equation to an axisymmetric harmonic map from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
into the hyperbolic plane
\mathcal{H}

[
MM67
,
Ern68
,
Car73
,
BZ78
,
WS92
]
. Similar reductions also exist in the Riemannian setting (see, for example,
[
Lot20
,
KL21
]
). In both settings, the topology of the 4-dimensional space is encoded in the data called a
rod structure
(see Definition
3.1
for the precise definition in our specific setting), and we are interested in axisymmetric harmonic maps which are
strongly tamed
by a rod structure (see Definition
4.10
). Using general theory of harmonic maps (see, for example,
[
Wei20
,
KL21
]
), we know the existence and uniqueness of axisymmetric harmonic maps which are tamed by a given rod structure and with desired asymptotics at infinity (see Theorem
4.24
). Together with a choice of an enhancement (see Definition
3.3
) we obtain a 4-dimensional toric Ricci-flat metric, which however may have
conical singularities
(see Definition
4.19
) along a union of codimension 2 submanifolds. In the Lorentzian setting, the axisymmetric stationary case of the black hole uniqueness conjecture is equivalent to the statement that conical singularities must be present unless the spacetime is a Kerr space-time. The difficulty in proving this conjecture lies in the lack of explicit knowledge relating the cone angles to the rod structure.

In our Riemannian setting, by contrast, we will show that solutions without conical singularities exist in abundance, which give rise to toric gravitational instantons. More precisely, for each
n\geq 1
we will design a specific rod structure which gives rise to the smooth 4-manifold
X_{n}
and such that, by adjusting the rod lengths, we can achieve that the cone angles along the rods are all equal to
2\pi
, so conical singularities are ruled out. Our argument is based on a topological fixed point theorem. The key observation is that, although there is in general no explicit knowledge on the cone angles, it is still possible in our setting to read out the behavior of cone angles when the rods degenerate to zero or infinity. To achieve the latter, we need to perform a bubbling analysis of axisymmetric harmonic maps. We show that the bubble limits are, up to bounded hyperbolic distance, modeled on four types of explicit axisymmetric harmonic maps (see Section
4.2
for the definitions):
\Ae
(Asymptotically Euclidean),
\AF_{0}
(Asymptotically Flat),
\ALF^{+}
(positively oriented Asymptotically Locally Flat),
\ALF^{-}
(negatively oriented Asymptotically Locally Flat). These are the harmonic maps generalizing those associated to the standard gravitational instantons, given respectively by the flat
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, the flat product
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the Taub-NUT space and the anti-Taub-NUT space. Heuristically, these model spaces serve as the fundamental building blocks for constructing the gravitational instantons in Theorem
1.1
. To realize the above strategy, there are a few subtleties

•

In general, there are topological constraints on the rod structure of a toric gravitational instanton (see for example
[
Nil24
]
). This means that we need to design the correct rod structure to start with (see Section
6.2
).

•

In general, it is not true that for an arbitrarily given rod structure one can always tune the cone angle by varying the rod lengths: for example, in the hyperkähler case (i.e. the case of degree
0
, see Remark
7.8
) the cone angles stay
constant
when we vary the rod lengths. Our observation is that in suitable settings the cone angles can be shown to diverge to infinity or go to a number smaller than
2\pi
, due to the degeneration of the enhancement in the process of the bubbling convergence, see Proposition
5.13
, Proposition
5.14
, Proposition
5.15
, Proposition
5.17
. Clearly, this can only occur in the non-hyperkähler case.

•

In the scale when the enhancement does not degenerate, there is again no precise information that can be said on the limit cone angles. Here the new ingredient is that there is an
angle comparison theorem
(see Proposition
4.42
), which is motivated by the Bishop-Gromov volume comparison for Riemannian metrics. It provides some key inequalities in our argument.

In the reverse direction, we will prove a PDE classification result for axisymmetric harmonic maps, motivated by the theory of gravitational instantons.
Locally, an axisymmetric harmonic map
\Phi
into the hyperbolic plane defines a 4-dimensional toric Ricci-flat metric
g
. If
g
has special geometry (i.e., locally Hermitian), then
\Phi
can be described explicitly in terms of an axisymmetric harmonic function: this is given by the
Gibbons-Hawking ansatz
if
g
is hyperkähler and by the
LeBrun-Tod ansatz
if
g
is Hermitian non-Kähler. In this paper, we give a characterization of these special axisymmetric harmonic maps in terms of a topological invariant of the rod structure. Namely, we define a notion of
degree
for a rod structure (see Definition
3.11
), which takes value in the set of non-negative integers and distinguishes different connected components of the set of all rod structures of a given Asymptotic Type. We prove the following

Theorem 1.2
.

Given an axisymmetric harmonic map
\Phi
which is strongly tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
(see Definition
4.10
), then

•

\Phi
defines a locally hyperkähler metric if and only if
\mathfrak{R}
has degree 0;

•

\Phi
defines a locally Hermitian non-Kähler metric if and only if
\mathfrak{R}
has degree 1.

As a consequence, when
\mathfrak{R}
has degree at most
1
,
\Phi
can be written in terms of an axisymmetric harmonic function, and the cone angles can be explicitly determined (see Remark
7.8
and Remark
7.10
). In the degree 0 case, the proof is proceeded by showing that the Gibbons-Hawking construction yields all the possible degree 0 rod structures and then by appealing to the uniqueness of axisymmetric harmonic maps. In the degree 1 case our proof makes use of the geometry of Hermitian gravitational instantons. The heart of the argument is to show that the class of axisymmetric harmonic maps locally generating Hermitian non-Kähler Ricci-flat metrics is open under deformation of rod structures. The latter then relies on the result of Biquard-Gauduchon-LeBrun
[
BGL24
]
for gravitational instantons.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we recall the dimension reduction of 4-dimensional toric Ricci-flat metrics to augmented, axisymmetric harmonic maps into the hyperbolic plane. In Section
3
we make precise definitions of rod structures and enhancements and describe the rod structure and the axisymmetric harmonic maps for a few key examples of toric gravitational instantons. In Section
4
we discuss tame harmonic maps (i.e. axisymmetric harmonic maps tamed by a rod structure) and study their local and global regularity. In particular, we define the
cone angle
along each rod. Then in the case of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
we prove a rigidity result (i.e., globally tame to strongly tame) and an angle comparison theorem (as a result of the Bishop volume comparison). In Section
5
we analyze the bubbling behavior of tame harmonic maps when the rod structure degenerates. This is done by constructing good background maps which are almost harmonic. This analysis allows us to gain information on the asymptotic behavior of the cone angles when the rod structure degenerates. In Section
6
we apply the results in Section
5
to prove Theorem
1.1
. In Section
7
we prove Theorem
1.2
. Section
8
is devoted to further discussion and open questions. In particular, we determine the splitting of the gravitational instantons constructed in Theorem
1.1
when
\beta
approaches
0
.

Notations and Conventions

•

We denote by
\mathbb{H}=\{(\rho,z)|\rho\geq 0\}
the right half plane with boundary. We also use polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
(
\theta\in[0,\pi]
) on
\mathbb{H}
, where
\theta
is the angle measured from the positive
z
-axis. We have

\displaystyle\rho=r\sin\theta,\ \ \ z=r\cos\theta.

•

We have the following actions on
\mathbb{H}
:

Rescaling:

(1.3)

\varphi_{\lambda}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{H};(\rho,z)\mapsto(\lambda^{-1}\rho,\lambda^{-1}z),\ \ \ \ \lambda>0.

Translation:

(1.4)

T_{\underline{z}}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{H};(\rho,z)\mapsto(\rho,z-\underline{z}),\ \ \ \underline{z}\in\mathbb{R}.

•

We denote by
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
the interior of
\mathbb{H}
and we identify
\Gamma=\partial\mathbb{H}
with the real line
\mathbb{R}
parameterized by
z
. We denote

\Gamma_{+}=\Gamma\cap\{z>0\},

\Gamma_{-}=\Gamma\cap\{z<0\}.

•

For an open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}
, we denote
\Omega^{\circ}=\Omega\cap\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
.

•

On
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we use the standard coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and the cylindrical coordinates
(\rho,\phi,z)
. They are related via

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=\rho\cos\phi,

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=\rho\sin\phi,

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle=z.

We also identify the boundary
\Gamma=\partial\mathbb{H}
with the
x_{3}
-axis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

•

We denote by
\mathcal{H}
the hyperbolic upper half plane
\{(X,Y)|X>0\}
endowed with the Riemannian hyperbolic metric

g_{\mathcal{H}}=X^{-2}(dX^{2}+dY^{2}).

•

We also identify
\mathcal{H}
with
SL(2;\mathbb{R})/SO(2)
, the space of
2\times 2
positive symmetric matrices of determinant 1. The identification is given by sending
(X,Y
) to the matrix

(1.5)

\left(\begin{matrix}X+X^{-1}Y^{2}&X^{-1}Y\\
X^{-1}Y&X^{-1}\end{matrix}\right).

Under this identification, for
B\in\mathcal{H}
, for
B_{1},B_{2}\in T_{B}\mathcal{H}
, we have

g_{\mathcal{H}}(B_{1},B_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\Tr(B^{-1}B_{1}B^{-1}B_{2}).

•

For two matrices
A,B\in\mathcal{H}
, we denote by
d(A,B)
the hyperbolic distance between them. It is easy to see that there is a monotonically increasing homeomorphism
F:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that for any
C\geq 0
,

(1.6)

d(A,B)\leq C\Longleftrightarrow\Tr(A^{-1}B-Id)\leq F(C).

Moreover, we can see that
F(C)
is uniformly comparable to
C^{2}
for
C\in(0,1]
.

•

We denote by
SL_{\pm}(2;\mathbb{R})
the subgroup of
GL(2;\mathbb{R})
with determinant
\pm 1
and by
SO_{-}(2)
the subset of
O(2)
with determinant
-1
. Denote

PSO(2)=SO(2)/\{\pm 1\}

PSO_{-}(2)=SO_{-}(2)/\{\pm 1\}.

There is a natural (left) action of
SL_{\pm}(2;\mathbb{R})
on
\mathcal{H}
, given by

(1.7)

D.\Phi=(D^{-1})^{\top}\Phi D^{-1}.

•

For
\lambda\neq 0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote the following matrices in
GL(2;\mathbb{R})

(1.8)

D_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\end{matrix}\right);\ \ \ \ \ L_{\mu}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\mu&1\end{matrix}\right);\ \ \ \ \ U_{\mu}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&\mu\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right);\ \ \ \ \ R_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\lambda\\
\lambda&0\end{matrix}\right).

•

A vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
will be viewed as column vectors and denoted by
(a,b)^{\top}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
we denote the vector

(1.9)

\bv_{\alpha}=(1,\alpha)^{\top}.

•

An element in
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
is denoted by fraktur symbols such as
\mathfrak{v}
,
\mathfrak{w}
, and so on. It is represented as
[(a,b)^{\top}]
for a vector
(a,b)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

•

Given two elements
\mathfrak{v}\neq\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, there is a unique
P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}\in PSO(2)
and
\alpha>0
such that

(1.10)

P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}.\mathfrak{v}=\bv_{\alpha/2},\quad P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}.\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}=\bv_{-{\alpha}/2}.

We denote

(1.11)

\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}:=\alpha.

We also make a convention that
\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}=0
if
\mathfrak{v}=\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
.
There is also a unique
\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}\in PSO_{-}(2)

such that

(1.12)

\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}.\mathfrak{v}=\bv_{2/\alpha},\quad\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}.\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}=\bv_{-2/\alpha}.

We have

(1.13)

\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}=R_{1}P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}.

•

Given an element
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{v}^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
the line orthogonal to
\mathfrak{v}
. There is a unique element
P_{\mathfrak{v}}=P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}}\in PSO(2)
such that

(1.14)

P_{\mathfrak{v}}.\mathfrak{v}=[(1,0)^{\top}],\ \ P_{\mathfrak{v}}.\mathfrak{v}^{\perp}=[(0,1)^{\top}].

•

We denote by
\mathbb{T}
a real 2-dimensional torus, viewed as a compact abelian Lie group and by
\mathfrak{L}
the co-character lattice in
\Lie(\mathbb{T})
, i.e,
\mathfrak{L}
is the lattice in
\mathfrak{t}
consisting of all elements
\xi
such that
\exp(\xi)=\Id
. A
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\mathfrak{L}
is called an integral basis of
\mathbb{T}
.

2.
Local theory

2.1.
Dimension reduction of the Ricci-flat equation

Let
(M,g)
be an oriented 4-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Let
X
be a Killing vector field on
M
and denote by
\eta=X^{\flat}
its dual 1-form. We have
\mathcal{L}_{X}g=0
, which implies that

•

The 1-form
\eta
is divergence free:

(2.1)

d^{*}\eta=0

and satisfies the equation

(2.2)

\nabla^{*}\nabla\eta=Ric.\eta.

•

Acting on
p
-forms,
\mathcal{L}_{X}
commute with
d^{*}
, so the formal adjoint
\mathcal{L}_{X}^{*}\cdot=\eta\wedge d^{*}\cdot+d^{*}(\eta\wedge\cdot)
commutes with
d
:

(2.3)

\mathcal{L}_{X}^{*}d=d\mathcal{L}_{X}^{*}.

Combined with the usual Bochner formula we get

(2.4)

d^{*}d\eta=\Delta_{H}\eta-dd^{*}\eta=2Ric.\eta.

We also have

\mathcal{L}_{X}^{*}(d\eta)=d\mathcal{L}_{X}^{*}\eta=d(\eta\wedge d^{*}\eta+d^{*}(\eta\wedge\eta))=0.

and

\mathcal{L}_{X}^{*}(d\eta)=\eta\wedge d^{*}d\eta+d^{*}(\eta\wedge d\eta).

In particular, if
g
is Einstein, then

d^{*}(\eta\wedge d\eta)=0.

Locally we can find a function
\mathcal{E}
, called the
Ernst potential
, which is unique up to constant addition, such that

d\mathcal{E}=*(\eta\wedge d\eta).

The
twist 1-form

d\mathcal{E}
measures the non-integrability of the orthogonal distribution to
X
.

Definition 2.1
.

A 4-dimensional
toric
Ricci-flat manifold consists of an oriented 4-dimensional Ricci-flat manifold
(M,g)
and a 2-torus
\mathbb{T}
action with the property that every point on
M
has a connected stabilizer group. We denote by
N
the quotient space
M/\mathbb{T}
.

Remark 2.2
.

The last condition is purely technical: it ensures that the quotient space
N
is a smooth manifold with boundary. Without this condition, one would have to deal with orbifolds.

Let
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
be an integral basis of
Lie(\mathbb{T})
. They can be viewed as Killing fields on
M
. Denote by
\eta_{i}
the dual 1-form of
X_{i}
. Then from the above discussion we have the Ernst potentials
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
respectively. We have

\mathcal{L}_{X_{i}}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\langle\eta_{i},d\mathcal{E}_{i}\rangle=*(\eta_{i}\wedge*d\mathcal{E}_{i})=0.

Denote

\Theta_{i}:=*(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge d\eta_{i}),\ i=1,2.

They measure the non-integrability of the horizontal distribution orthogonal to the
\mathbb{T}
orbits. Then

\Theta_{1}=\langle\eta_{2},d\mathcal{E}_{1}\rangle=\mathcal{L}_{X_{2}}\mathcal{E}_{1}

and

\Theta_{2}=-\mathcal{L}_{X_{1}}\mathcal{E}_{2}.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{X_{1}}\eta_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{X_{2}}\eta_{1}=0
, we see that
d\Theta_{1}=d\Theta_{2}=0
, so
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
are both constants.

Definition 2.3
.

A 4-dimensional toric Ricci-flat manifold
(M,g)
is called
integrable
if the horizontal distribution is integrable, or equivalently, if
\Theta_{1}=\Theta_{2}=0
.

The integrability property is also known in the literature as
surface-orthogonality

[
CP02
,
Joy95
]
. In this paper, our main interest lies in the case when the
\mathbb{T}
action has a fixed point, so we will restrict our discussion to the integrable setting.

In the rest of this subsection we will derive the local dimensional reduction of the Ricci-flat equation. It is well-known that this results into an axisymmetric harmonic map into the hyperbolic plane, see for example
[
Lot20
,
KL21
]
. For the convenience of readers we include a brief derivation here. Denote by
M^{\circ}
the set of points in
M
with trivial stabilizer and denote
N^{\circ}=M^{\circ}/\mathbb{T}
.

Locally in
M^{\circ}
we can write the integrable metric
g
in the form

(2.5)

g=\sum_{i,j}G_{ij}(x_{1},x_{2})d\phi_{i}d\phi_{j}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{\alpha}dx_{\beta},

where
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
are local coordinates on
N^{\circ}
,
\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\}
are fiber coordinates such that
\partial_{\phi_{i}}=X_{i}
. We orient the
\mathbb{T}
fibers using the form
d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2}
, which then induces an orientation on the base
N^{\circ}
. If we change the choice of the integral basis via an element
A\in GL(2;\mathbb{Z})
then these orientations are preserved if and only if
\det(A)=1
.

Notice that we have
\eta_{i}=\sum_{j}G_{ij}d\phi_{j}
. We write the
Gram matrix

G=(G_{ij})>0
as

G=\rho\Phi,

where
\rho=\sqrt{\det G}>0,
and
\Phi
is a map into the hyperbolic plane
\mathcal{H}
.
Denote

(2.6)

J:=G^{-1}dG=\rho^{-1}d\rho\cdot\Id+\Phi^{-1}d\Phi.

Below will use
\ \widehat{}\
to denote operators on the base and
\ \widetilde{}\
to denote operators on the fibers. Notice that

\displaystyle d\phi_{j}\wedge\widetilde{*}\eta_{i}=\delta_{ij}\rho d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2},

\displaystyle d(\rho^{-1}\widetilde{*}\eta_{i})=0.

We investigate the Ricci-flat equation for a metric
g
of the form (
2.5
). By (
2.4
),

(2.7)

d^{*}d\eta_{i}=2Ric.\eta_{i}.

We compute

(2.8)

d\eta_{i}=\sum_{j}dG_{ij}\wedge d\phi_{j}=\rho\sum_{j}J_{ji}\wedge\rho^{-1}\eta_{j},

(2.9)

*d\eta_{i}=-\rho\sum_{j}\widehat{*}J_{ji}\wedge\widetilde{*}\rho^{-1}\eta_{j},

(2.10)

d*d\eta_{i}=-\sum_{j}\widehat{d}(\rho\widehat{*}J_{ji})\wedge\widetilde{*}\rho^{-1}\eta_{j}.

It follows that the horizontal components of
Ric.\eta_{i}
always vanishes, and its vertical component vanishes if and only if

(2.11)

\widehat{d}(\rho\widehat{*}J)=0.

Next we deal with the horizontal part of the Ricci curvature. Here we use the Bochner-Weitzenböck formula. For
f=f(x_{1},x_{2})
, we have

(2.12)

\frac{1}{2}\Delta|\nabla f|^{2}=|\text{Hess}f|^{2}+\langle\nabla\Delta f,\nabla f\rangle+Ric(\nabla f,\nabla f),

(2.13)

\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\Delta}|\widehat{\nabla}f|^{2}=|\widehat{\text{Hess}}f|^{2}+\langle\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\Delta}f,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle+\widehat{Ric}(\widehat{\nabla}f,\widehat{\nabla}f).

Notice that
\nabla f
is the horizontal lift of
\widehat{\nabla}f
.

Lemma 2.4
.

The horizontal leaves are totally geodesic.

Proof.

For a general Riemannian submersion, given a geodesic on the base and a lift of the initial point to a fiber, there is always a unique horizontal lift to a geodesic on the total space. So each horizontal tangent direction is tangential to a horizontal geodesic. Since the horizontal distribution is integrable in our case, this shows that the horizontal leaves are totally geodesic.
∎

From the lemma it follows that

(2.14)

\text{Hess}f=\widehat{\text{Hess}}f-df(\Pi),

where
\Pi
is the second fundamental form of the fibers and is given by

(2.15)

\Pi_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\nabla}G_{ij}.

So

\displaystyle|\text{Hess}f|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\widehat{\nabla}^{2}f|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i,j,k,l}G^{ik}G^{jl}\langle\widehat{\nabla}G_{ij},\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle\langle\widehat{\nabla}G_{kl},\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\widehat{\nabla}^{2}f|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\Tr\langle J,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle\langle J,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle.

Taking the trace of (
2.14
) we obtain

(2.16)

\Delta f=\widehat{\Delta}f-\langle\widehat{\nabla}f,H\rangle,

where

H=-\widehat{\nabla}\log\rho

is the mean curvature vector field.
By comparing (
2.12
) and (
2.13
), we obtain that the vanishing of the horizontal part of the Ricci curvature is equivalent to that for all
f
,

\displaystyle\widehat{Ric}(\widehat{\nabla}f,\widehat{\nabla}f)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widehat{\nabla}\log\rho,\widehat{\nabla}|\widehat{\nabla}f|^{2}\rangle+\langle\widehat{\nabla}\langle\widehat{\nabla}\log\rho,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle+\frac{1}{4}\Tr\langle J,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle\langle J,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle

\displaystyle=\widehat{\text{Hess}}\log\rho(\widehat{\nabla}f,\widehat{\nabla}f)+\frac{1}{4}\Tr\langle J,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle\langle J,\widehat{\nabla}f\rangle+\langle\widehat{\nabla}\log\rho,\widehat{\nabla}_{\widehat{\nabla}f}\widehat{\nabla}f-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\nabla}|\widehat{\nabla}f|^{2}\rangle.

It is easy to check that
\widehat{\nabla}_{\widehat{\nabla}f}\widehat{\nabla}f-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\nabla}|\widehat{\nabla}f|^{2}\equiv 0
. So we arrive at

(2.17)

\widehat{Ric}=\widehat{\text{Hess}}\log\rho+\frac{1}{4}\Tr(J\otimes J).

Combining the above we get the dimension reduction of the Ricci-flat equation in terms of a system of two equations

(2.18)

\displaystyle\widehat{d}(\rho\widehat{*}J)=0,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\widehat{Ric}=\widehat{\text{Hess}}\log\rho+\frac{1}{4}\Tr(J\otimes J).

Taking the trace of (
2.18
) we see that

\widehat{d}^{*}\widehat{d}\rho=0,

so
\rho
is a harmonic function on
N^{\circ}
. Let
z
be a local harmonic conjugate of
\rho
, that is, a function such that
\widehat{d}z=\widehat{*}\widehat{d}\rho
; it is well-defined up to an additive constant.

Definition 2.5
.

We say that
(M,g)
is regular if
d\rho\neq 0
on
M^{\circ}
.

Suppose
(M,g)
is regular, then
\{\rho,z\}
form local conformal coordinates on
N^{\circ}
in which we may write

\widehat{g}=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})

for a smooth local function
\nu
. Equation (
2.18
) then becomes

(2.20)

(\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{\rho})_{\rho}+(\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{z})_{z}=0.

If we use the cylindrical coordinates
(\rho,\phi,z)
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
x_{1}=\rho\cos\phi
,
x_{2}=\rho\sin\phi
and
x_{3}=z
, then (
2.20
) is exactly the equation for
\Phi
which induces an axisymmetric harmonic map from a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
into
\mathcal{H}
.

Denote

\mathcal{U}=\Id+\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{\rho},

and

\mathcal{V}=\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{z}.

By looking at different components of (
2.19
), it is equivalent to a combination of three equations below

(2.21)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{\rho z}=0\Leftrightarrow\partial_{z}\nu=\frac{1}{4\rho}\Tr(\mathcal{U}\mathcal{V}),

(2.22)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{\rho\rho}=\widehat{R}_{zz}\Leftrightarrow\partial_{\rho}\nu=-\frac{1}{2\rho}+\frac{1}{8\rho}\Tr(\mathcal{U}^{2}-\mathcal{V}^{2}),

(2.23)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{zz}=\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\nu+\frac{1}{4\rho^{2}}\Tr(\mathcal{V}^{2}).

One can check that (
2.23
) follows from (
2.20
), (
2.21
) and (
2.22
) by taking further derivatives, hence is redundant. The compatibility condition for (
2.21
) and (
2.22
) also follows from (
2.20
).

The upshot is that the Ricci-flat equation for
g
is locally reduced to the following system of equations on the pair
(\Phi,\nu)

(2.24)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{\rho})_{\rho}+(\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{z})_{z}=0.\\
&\partial_{z}\nu=\frac{1}{4\rho}\Tr(\mathcal{U}\mathcal{V}),\\
&\partial_{\rho}\nu=-\frac{1}{2\rho}+\frac{1}{8\rho}\Tr(\mathcal{U}^{2}-\mathcal{V}^{2}).\end{aligned}\right.

2.2.
Augmented harmonic maps

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
.

Definition 2.6
.

A smooth map
\Phi:U\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is called
harmonic
if it induces an axisymmetric harmonic map from a domain
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
into
\mathcal{H}
, that is,
\Phi
satisfies the equation

(2.25)

(\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{\rho})_{\rho}+(\rho\Phi^{-1}\Phi_{z})_{z}=0.

Remark 2.7
.

The terminology of being harmonic used here is simply for notational convenience. It should not be confused with the usual harmonic map from the 2-dimensional space
U
.

Definition 2.8
.

An
augmentation
for a harmonic map
\Phi:U\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a function
\nu
on
U
satisfying (
2.24
).

A pair
(\Phi,\nu)
is called an
augmented
harmonic map.

The above discussion says that a 4-dimensional regular, integrable, toric Ricci-flat metric gives rise to an augmented harmonic map. Conversely, given an augmented harmonic map
(\Phi,\nu)
on an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
, on
U\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
we can write down a Ricci-flat metric

(2.26)

g=e^{2\nu}(dz^{2}+d\rho^{2})+\rho\Phi,

where we view
\rho\Phi
as a family of flat metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(with coordinates
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
as above). Choosing any lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
then yields a toric Ricci-flat metric on
U\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda
.

Notice that if
U
is simply-connected, given a harmonic map
\Phi
, one can use (
2.24
) to uniquely solve the function
\nu
up to an additive constant. Giving an augmentation amounts to fixing a choice of
\nu
.

Remark 2.9
.

On the original 4-manifold
M
, the local coordinates
(\rho,z,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
are usually referred to as the Weyl-Papapetrou coordinates.

Given an augmented harmonic map
(\Phi,\nu)
, it is easy to check that

•

For any
\lambda>0
,
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}T_{\underline{z}}^{*}\Phi,\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}T_{\underline{z}}^{*}\nu)
defines an augmented harmonic map. Here
\varphi_{\lambda}
and
T_{\underline{z}}
are defined in (
1.3
) and (
1.4
).

•

For
P\in SL_{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
(P.\Phi,\nu)
defines an augmented harmonic map, where
P.\Phi
is defined in (
1.7
).

2.3.
Points of non-trivial stabilizer

We introduce a few notations first.

Notation 2.10
.

Given a toric Ricci-flat metric (
M,g)
, we denote

•

by
M^{\sharp}
the set of points fixed by the
\mathbb{T}
action;

•

by
M^{*}
the set of points with stabilizer isomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
;

•

N^{\sharp}=M^{\sharp}/\mathbb{T}\simeq M^{\sharp}
;

•

N^{*}=M^{*}/\mathbb{T}
;

•

N=M/\mathbb{T}
.

It follows from definition that

M=M^{\circ}\cup M^{\sharp}\cup M^{*}.

Locally around a point
p\in M^{*}
, the
\mathbb{T}
action is modeled on the standard action of
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times I
, where the first
\mathbb{S}^{1}
acts by rotation on the
\mathbb{R}^{2}
factor and the second
\mathbb{S}^{1}
action by rotation on the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
factor. It is then easy to see that
N\setminus N^{\sharp}
is naturally a smooth manifold with boundary and the quotient metric is smooth up to the boundary.

Locally around a point
p\in M^{\sharp}
, the
\mathbb{T}
action is modeled on the standard action of
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where the first
\mathbb{S}^{1}
acts by rotation on the first
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the second
\mathbb{S}^{1}
acts by rotation on the second
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By studying the local structure one can see that
M^{\sharp}
(and also
N^{\sharp}
) consists of only isolated points and
N
is naturally a smooth manifold with corner at points in
N^{\sharp}
; the local model around
N^{\sharp}
is the first quadrant in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The metric is smooth up to the corner.

3.
Rod structures and toric gravitational instantons

3.1.
Rod structures

To understand the global theory we introduce the notion of an
enhanced rod structure
. The terminology “rod structure” has been widely used in the physics literature, see for example
[
OR70
,
Har04
,
HIW11
,
CT10
,
KL21
]
. Mathematically, a rod structure serves as a substitute of the Delzant polytope in toric Kähler geometry when the complex structure is absent. Our precise definition used in this paper is given as follows, which suits our purpose and differs slightly from what is used in most literature.

Definition 3.1

(Rod structure)
.

A
rod structure

\mathfrak{R}=(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n},\sharp)
consists of:

•

A collection of
turning points

z_{1}=0<\dots<z_{n}
on
\Gamma:=\mathbb{R}
.

We also set
z_{0}=-\infty
and
z_{n+1}=+\infty
.

For
0\leq j\leq n
, the open interval
\mathfrak{I}_{j}:=(z_{j},z_{j+1})\subset\Gamma
is called a
rod
.

•

A collection of
rod vectors

\mathfrak{v}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{RP}^{1}
satisfying the
non-degeneracy condition
that
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}
for
0\leq j\leq n-1
.

Each
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
can be represented as
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(a_{j},b_{j})^{\top}]
, where
(a_{j},b_{j})^{\top}
is a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

•

An Asymptotic
Type

\sharp\in\{\Ae,\ALFp,\ALFm\}
if
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\neq\mathfrak{v}_{n}
, and
\sharp\in\{\AFa|\beta\in\mathbb{R})\}
if
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}
.

The quantity
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R})=|z_{n}|
is referred to as the
size
of
\mathfrak{R}
.

In the above we assume
n\geq 1
. We also define a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
with
n=0
to be one where the set of turning points is empty and there is exactly one rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
(in particular,
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}
).

When we do not specify the Type, we call
\mathfrak{R}
an
untyped rod structure
; when we relax the non-degeneracy condition we call
\mathfrak{R}
a
weak rod structure
. These notions will make convenient our later discussion. For a weak rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
, there is a natural rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
associated to
\mathfrak{R}
, obtained by combining all the adjacent rods with the same rod vectors into a single rod.

It is often helpful to represent an untyped rod structure by an unbounded polygon in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that the length of each side represents the rod length and the normal direction of each side represents the rod vector. See Figure
1
for an illustration of a rod structure with 2 turning points,
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{2}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
,
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(0,1)^{\top}]
.

\mathfrak{v}_{0}
\mathfrak{v}_{2}
\mathfrak{v}_{1}

Figure 1.
A polygon that represents an untyped rod structure.

Remark 3.2
.

Here
\Ae
stands for Asymptotically Euclidean,
\ALF^{+}
stands for positively oriented Asymptotically Locally Flat,
\ALF^{-}
stands for negatively oriented Asymptotically Locally Flat,
\AFa
stands for Asymptotically Flat with rotation angle
2\pi\beta
. These are possible Asymptotic Types of toric gravitational instantons that we will be interested in this paper, see Section
3.3
for model examples. When we discuss harmonic maps which are strongly tamed by a rod structure, the Asymptotic Type indeed uniquely determines the asymptotic model; see the discussion in Section
4.1
.

Definition 3.3

(Enhancement)
.

Given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
, an
enhancement
for
\mathfrak{R}
is a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that all the rod vectors can be represented by vectors in
\Lambda
.

A rod structure is
enhanceable
if admits an enhancement.

An
enhanced rod structure

(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
consists of a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
and an enhancement
\Lambda
for
\mathfrak{R}
.

Definition 3.4

(Primitive rod vector)
.

Given an enhanced rod structure
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
, for each rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
, a
primitive rod vector

\bbv_{j}
is defined to be a primitive vector in
\Lambda
along the line defined by
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
.

Notice that the primitive rod vector is unique up to multiplication by
-1
. In particular, the norm
|\bbv_{j}|
is a well-defined positive number.

Definition 3.5

(Smoothness)
.

An enhanced rod structure
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
is
smooth
if, for any
0\leq j\leq n-1
,

\Lambda
is generated by primitive rod vectors
\bbv_{j}
along
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
and
\bbv_{j+1}
along
\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}
.

Remark 3.6
.

Comparing with the notion of a rod structure used in most literature, the main difference here is that we have distinguished the lattice data as an “enhancement” for the rod structure. The main purpose is that in this way we have separated the analytic information and the topological information. As can be seen in Section
4
, a rod structure defined as above is sufficient for the analytic study of tame harmonic maps, whereas the enhancement is only necessary when we pass from harmonic maps to toric gravitational instantons.

Definition 3.7
.

We define the following operations on an untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}=(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n})

•

For
\lambda>0
and
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\mathfrak{R}=\left(\{\lambda z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n}\right),

and

T_{\underline{z}}^{*}\mathfrak{R}=\left(\{z_{j}+\underline{z}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n}\right).

•

For
P\in GL_{+}(2;\mathbb{R})
, we define

P.\mathfrak{R}=\left(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{P.\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n}\right).

•

For
P\in GL_{-}(2;\mathbb{R})
, we define

P.\mathfrak{R}=\left(\{z_{n}-z_{n+1-j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{P.\mathfrak{v}_{n-j}\}_{j=0}^{n}\right).

•

We define the
opposite
rod structure

\overline{\mathfrak{R}}=\left(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{R_{1}.\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n}\right),

where
R_{1}
is defined in (
1.8
).

These also induce operations on an enhanced untyped rod structure: if
\mathfrak{R}
is further enhanced by
\Lambda
, then we define

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Lambda:=\Lambda
,

T_{\underline{z}}^{*}\Lambda:=\Lambda
, and define
P.\Lambda
by the natural action.

Definition 3.8
.

For a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}=(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{P.\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n},\sharp)
,
we define the
opposite
rod structure

\overline{\mathfrak{R}}=\left(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{R_{1}.\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n},\overline{\sharp}\right),

where
R_{1}
is defined in (
1.8
).
Here we make the convention that

(3.1)

\overline{\ALFp}=\ALFm,\ \ \overline{\ALFm}=\ALFp,\ \ \overline{\Ae}=\Ae,\ \ \overline{\AFa}=\AF_{-\beta}.

Remark 3.9
.

Notice that we do not define the other operations on Asymptotic Types. In particular, by our convention, when we perform the operation of rescaling, translation, or
GL(2;\mathbb{R})
action, to a rod structure, we only get an untyped rod structure.

Definition 3.10

(Equivalence)
.

•

Two untyped rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{2}
are called
equivalent
if
there is an element
P\in GL(2;\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathfrak{R}_{1}=P.\mathfrak{R}_{2}
.

•

Two enhanced untyped rod structures
(\mathfrak{R}_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(\mathfrak{R}_{2},\Lambda_{2})
are called
equivalent
if there is an element
P\in GL(2;\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathfrak{R}_{2}=P.\mathfrak{R}_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}=P.\Lambda_{1}
.

In general
\mathfrak{R}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
are not equivalent. Instead,
\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
is equivalent to the untyped rod structure
(\{z_{n}-z_{n+1-j}\}_{j=1}^{n},\{\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n})
.

Given an enhanced untyped rod structure
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
we consider the 4-manifold
M^{\circ}=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda
, with the canonical orientation induced by the 4-form
d\rho\wedge dz\wedge d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2}
. It admits the action of a 2-torus
\mathbb{T}
which can be identified with
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda
. The open manifold
M^{\circ}
can be compactified into a toric orbifold
M
by adding points with stabilizers over
\Gamma=\partial\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. Over each rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
the stabilizer is an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
whose Lie algebra is the line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
corresponding to the rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
. At each turning point
z_{j}
we obtain an orbifold point in
M
, a neighborhood of which can be topologically identified with an open subset in
\mathbb{R}^{4}/H
. The orbifold group
H
can be determined as follows. We may identify
\Lambda
with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by an element
D\in GL(2,\mathbb{R})
, such that
\mathfrak{v}_{j-1}=[(0,1)^{\top}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(p,-q)^{\top}]
with
0\leq q\leq p-1
and
(p,q)=1
. Then
H
is isomorphic to the cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acting on
\mathbb{R}^{4}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
in the way that
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(\zeta_{p}z_{1},\zeta_{p}^{q}z_{2})
. In particular if
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
is smooth, then
M
is a smooth manifold. It is also easy to see that equivalent enhanced untyped rod structures yield isomorphic toric orbifolds. If we change
\mathfrak{R}
to the opposite untyped rod structure
\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
, then we simply obtain an isomorphic toric manifold with the opposite orientation. Obviously for this topological discussion the Asymptotic Type does not play a role.

We next define the notion of
degree
for rod structures. Given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
, we can naturally associate a loop
l_{\mathfrak{R}}
in
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
. Start with the point
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and choose a unit representative vector
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
for
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
. Then we inductively choose a unit representative vector
v_{j}
for
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
(for all
j\leq n
) in a way such that
v_{j}
is given by rotating
v_{j-1}
counter-clockwise by an angle smaller than
\pi
. Then for
j\leq n-1
we connect each
v_{j}
and
v_{j+1}
via the natural counter-clockwise rotation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\neq\mathfrak{v}_{n}
, then we further rotate
v_{n}
counter-clockwise by an angle smaller than
\pi
to connect it to
\pm v_{0}
(there is exactly one choice). Passing to the projectivization
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, this defines a loop
l_{\mathfrak{R}}
. It is straightforward to verify that the homotopy class of
l_{\mathfrak{R}}
is well-defined and independent of the initial choice of
v_{0}
.
Denote

(3.2)

\displaystyle e(\mathfrak{R})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{l_{\mathfrak{R}}}d\theta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

where
d\theta
is the standard angular 1-form on
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Definition 3.11
.

We define the degree
d(\mathfrak{R})
of a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
to be

(3.3)

d(\mathfrak{R})=\begin{cases}e(\mathfrak{R})-1,&\ \text{if}\ \sharp=\Ae\ \text{or}\ALFp;\\
e(\mathfrak{R}),&\ \text{if}\ \sharp=\ALFm\ \text{or}\ \AFa.\end{cases}

In the definition, the shift by
-1
is chosen only for notational convenience, such that the statement of Theorem
7.4
is uniform for all asymptotic types. Notice that we always have
d(\mathfrak{R})\geq 0
. Moreover, when
\sharp=\ALFm
we must have
d(\mathfrak{R})\geq 1
and when
\sharp=\AFa
we must have
d(\mathfrak{R})\geq 1
unless there are no turning points.

Notice that in general
d(\mathfrak{R})\neq d(\mathfrak{\overline{R}})
. The degree precisely characterizes connected components of the space of rod structures of fixed Type
\sharp
. Denote by
\mathcal{S}({\sharp\mid z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}})
the space of rod structures of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
, with fixed turning points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
. Note that by definition we have for all
j
,
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}
. So
\mathcal{S}({\sharp\mid z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}})
is an open subset of
(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n+1}
and we equip it with the induced topology.

Proposition 3.12
.

Two rod structures
\mathfrak{R}
and
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}(\sharp\mid z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
are in the same connected component if and only if
d(\mathfrak{R})=d(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By passing to the universal cover
\mathbb{R}^{1}
of
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, where a fundamental domain is given by
[0,\pi)
, the representatives
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}
that we selected above can be identified as real numbers
0=v_{0}<\ldots<v_{n}\in[0,\infty)
. The integer
e(\mathfrak{R})
is exactly the integer
k
such that
v_{n}\in((k-1)\pi,k\pi]
. Note that we moreover have
0<v_{j}-v_{j-1}<\pi
. Hence, the space
\mathcal{S}(\sharp\mid z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
can be identified with the set of
n
-tuple of numbers
0=v_{0}<v_{1}<\ldots<v_{n}
, with
0<v_{j}-v_{j-1}<\pi
and

•

if
\sharp\in\{\Ae,\ALFp,\ALFm\}
, then
v_{n}\not\in\mathbb{Z}\pi
;

•

if
\sharp=\AFa
, then
v_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\pi
.

From here one can conclude.
∎

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{S}(\sharp\mid z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
has exactly
n
connected components except when it is of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
, in which case it has exactly
n-1
connected components when
n\geq 1
.

3.2.
Toric gravitational instantons

Definition 3.13
.

A toric gravitational instanton is a complete oriented toric Ricci-flat 4-manifold
(M,g)
which is integrable and regular, and satisfies that

\int_{M}|\Rm_{g}|^{2}\dvol_{g}<\infty.

Given a toric gravitational instanton, we choose an integral basis
\{\partial_{\phi_{1}},\partial_{\phi_{2}}\}
of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{T})
and use them to identify
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{T})
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The
Gram matrix

G
is globally defined on
N^{\circ}
.
As before we set

\rho:=\sqrt{\det G}\geq 0.

It is a priori defined on
N^{\circ}
, and it extends continuously to
N
and vanishes on
N\setminus N^{\circ}
. There is also an induced orientation on the quotient
N
. If
N
is simply-connected (in particular, if
M
is simply-connected), then we can define a global harmonic conjugate
z
of
\rho
which is continuous up to
N
. Here we are taking the harmonic conjugate with the quotient metric and the induced orientation. That is, the orientation such that
d\rho\wedge dz\wedge d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2}
is positively oriented on
M
.

Definition 3.14
.

A toric gravitational instanton is
proper
if there is a globally defined harmonic conjugate
z
of
\rho
on
N
such that the map
\mathcal{F}:=(\rho,z)
induces a homeomorphism from
N
to
\mathbb{H}
.

This properness assumption is a natural one since it is satisfied by all the known examples of simply-connected toric gravitational instantons.
In this paper we only consider toric gravitational instantons which are proper.

A proper toric gravitational instanton naturally defines a smooth enhanced untyped rod structure using the map
\mathcal{F}
. The turning points are given by the images under
\mathcal{F}
of the points in
M^{\sharp}
and the rods are given by the images of the components of
M^{*}
. The closure of each component of
M^{*}
is topologically either
S^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The rod vector is given by the line in
\Lie(\mathbb{T})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
corresponding to the Lie algebra of the stabilizer
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The smoothness of
M
implies that the rod structure is both enhanced and smooth.

If we change the integral basis by an element
P
in
GL(2;\mathbb{Z})
, namely, using
\{P.\partial_{\phi_{1}},P.\partial_{\phi_{2}}\}
as the new integral basis, then the untyped rod structure is changed to
P^{-1}.\mathfrak{R}
.
For a proper toric gravitational instanton
(M,g)
, if we keep the integral basis but consider the opposite orientation on
M
, then the resulting untyped rod structure is equivalent to
\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
.

3.3.
Key Examples

In this subsection we discuss a few important classes of examples of toric gravitational instantons. We will write down the corresponding rod structures and augmented harmonic maps, which will form the local models and fundamental building blocks for our construction.

Example 3.15

(Flat
\mathbb{R}^{4}
)
.

Consider the flat space
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, which we identify with
\mathbb{C}^{2}
using the complex coordinates
(z_{1},z_{2})
. Write

z_{1}=se^{i\phi_{1}}\sin\theta,\ \ z_{2}=se^{i\phi_{2}}\cos\theta.

Then the flat metric takes the form

g=ds^{2}+s^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\phi_{1}^{2}+\cos^{2}\theta d\phi_{2}^{2}).

Consider the
\mathbb{T}
action generated by
\partial_{\phi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\phi_{2}}
. Then
\rho=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}\sin(2\theta)
and one can take the harmonic conjugate
z=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}\cos(2\theta)
. Denote
r=\sqrt{\rho^{2}+z^{2}}
. The corresponding augmented harmonic map is

\Phi=\rho^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}r-z&0\\
0&r+z\end{pmatrix},\ \ \nu=-\log(2r).

and the quotient metric on
N
is

\widehat{g}=\frac{1}{2r}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2}),

which is isometric to the first quadrant in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. The corresponding rod structure has exactly one turning point
z_{1}=0
and the rod vectors are
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(0,1)^{\top}]
,
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
.

It turns out to be more natural to view
\mathbb{R}^{4}
in terms of the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz (see Section
7.1.1
. Then it is convenient to consider an equivalent untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\Ae}
where
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
,
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
, and the augmented harmonic map is

(3.4)

\Phi^{\Ae}=\rho^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{r}{2}&z\\
z&2r\end{pmatrix},\ \ \nu^{\Ae}=-\log(2r).

We make
\mathfrak{R}^{\Ae}
a rod structure by assigning the Asymptotic Type to be
\Ae
, since the corresponding Ricci-flat metric is the model end for
Asymptotically Euclidean
spaces. See Figure
2
for a picture of the rod structure.

Notice that
\Phi^{\Ae}
is scale invariant, i.e.,
\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\Ae}=\Phi^{\Ae}
for all
\lambda>0
. This is related to the conical nature of the flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]

Figure 2.
The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\Ae}
.

Example 3.16

(Taub-NUT)
.

Consider the
Taub-NUT space
, which is by definition the (positively oriented) Taub-NUT metric. In the Weyl-Papapetrou coordinates it can be explicitly written as

g=H^{-1}(d\phi_{1}+{A}d\phi_{2})^{2}+H(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2}+\rho^{2}d\phi_{2}^{2}),

where

H=1+\frac{1}{2r},

and

{A}=\frac{z}{2r}.

The
\mathbb{T}
action is generated by
\partial_{\phi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\phi_{2}}
.
This is a hyperkähler metric so can be obtained via the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz (see Section
7.1.1
). The orientation is determined by the hyperkähler complex structures. The underlying manifold is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

The augmented harmonic map is given by

(3.5)

\Phi^{\TNp}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}H^{-1}&H^{-1}A\\
H^{-1}A&H\rho^{2}+H^{-1}A^{2}\end{matrix}\right),\ \ \nu^{\TNp}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\frac{1}{2r}).

The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{+}}
consists of two rods
\mathfrak{I}_{0}=(-\infty,0)
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}=(0,\infty)
, with rod vectors given by
{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}=[(\frac{1}{2},1)^{\top}]
and
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=[(-\frac{1}{2},1)^{\top}]
respectively. We assign the Asymptotic Type to be
\ALFp
, since the corresponding Ricci-flat metric is the model end for
Asymptotically Locally Flat
spaces. See Figure
3
.

\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(-\frac{1}{2},1)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(\frac{1}{2},1)^{\top}]

Figure 3.
The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TNp}
.

Example 3.17

(Anti-Taub-NUT)
.

Consider the
anti-Taub-NUT space
, which is by definition the negatively oriented Taub-NUT metric. We can use the same formula as in the previous example, but with
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
interchanged:

g=H^{-1}(d\phi_{2}+{A}d\phi_{1})^{2}+H(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2}+\rho^{2}d\phi_{1}^{2}),

This is
negatively oriented
, because the orientation we use in our setting, which is defined by
d\rho dzd\phi_{1}d\phi_{2}
, is opposite to the orientation determined by the usual hyperkähler complex structure. Note that the anti-Taub-NUT space is Hermitian and conformally Kähler with respect to a different complex structure (see Section
7.1.2
).

The augmented harmonic map is given by

(3.6)

\Phi^{\TNm}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}H\rho^{2}+H^{-1}A^{2}&H^{-1}A\\
H^{-1}A&H^{-1}\end{matrix}\right),\ \ \nu^{\TNm}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\frac{1}{2r}).

The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{-}}
consists of two rods
\mathfrak{I}_{0}=(-\infty,0)
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}=(0,\infty)
, with rod vectors given by
{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}=[(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
and
{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=[(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
respectively. We assign the Asymptotic Type to be
\ALFm
. See Figure
4
.

Notice that
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{-}}
differs from
\mathfrak{R}^{\Ae}
only by the Asymptotic Type. It is a manifestation of the fact that the underlying smooth toric 4-manifolds of the flat metric and the Taub-NUT metric are diffeomorphic.

\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]

Figure 4.
The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TNm}
.

Example 3.18

(Flat
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
)
.

Consider the product space
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. In terms of the polar coordinates
(r,\theta,\phi_{1})
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the rotational coordinate
\phi_{2}
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the standard flat metric can be written as

g=dr^{2}+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\phi_{1}^{2})+d\phi_{2}^{2}.

Consider the
\mathbb{T}
action generated by
\partial_{\phi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\phi_{2}}
. Then
\rho=r\sin\theta
and one can take
z=r\cos\theta
. The augmented harmonic map has the form

(3.7)

\Phi^{\AF_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}\rho&0\\
0&\rho^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \nu^{\AF_{0}}=0.

The quotient metric on
N
is given as

\widehat{g}=d\rho^{2}+dz^{2}.

The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\AF_{0}}
has no turning points and with exactly one rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{0}]
. We assign the Asymptotic Type to be
\AF_{0}
.

More generally, for
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we consider the quotient
M_{\beta}:=(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R})/\langle q_{\beta}\rangle
, where
q_{\beta}
acts as a rotation by angle
2\pi\beta
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a translation by distance
1
on
\mathbb{R}^{1}
. Under the basis
\partial_{\phi_{1}},\beta\partial_{\phi_{1}}+\partial_{\phi_{2}}
the augmented harmonic map has the form

(3.8)

\Phi^{\AFa}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}&\beta\rho^{2}\\
\beta\rho^{2}&\beta^{2}\rho^{2}+1\end{matrix}\right),\ \ \nu^{\AFa}=0.

Note that for
\beta_{1}\equiv\beta_{2}\mod 1
, the
\mathbb{Z}
actions generated by
q_{\beta_{1}}
and
q_{\beta_{2}}
are the same. The difference only lies in our choices of basis to write down the harmonic map. Also notice that different
\Phi^{\AFa}
are related by
\Phi^{\AFa}=U_{-\beta}.\Phi^{\AF_{0}}
. The rod structure agrees with that of
\mathfrak{R}^{\AF_{0}}
, except we now assign the Asymptotic Type to be
\AFa
. See Figure
5
.

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,0)^{\top}]

Figure 5.
The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\AFa}
.

Remark 3.19
.

One may notice that in the above we distinguish only between the Taub-NUT and anti-Taub-NUT spaces, not in the
\Ae
and
\AFa
cases. The reason is as follows. In the
\AFa
case, we know that the space
M_{\beta}
with the opposite orientation is isomorphic to
M_{-\beta}
as oriented Ricci-flat metrics. In the
\Ae
case, we will in Section
4.1
extend each model harmonic map above to a family of model harmonic maps. Using the notations in Definition
4.3
and
4.6
, then we have

R_{1}.\Phi^{\Ae_{\alpha}}=\Phi^{\Ae_{4/\alpha}}.

This means that the flat
\mathbb{R}^{4}
with the opposite orientation can be described using the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\Ae_{4}}
and the harmonic map
\Phi^{\Ae_{4}}
. This is not the case for the Taub-NUT space, since we only have (see Definition
4.4
)

R_{1}.\Phi^{\TNap}=\Phi^{\TNam}.

So we need to use two different families of model harmonic maps.

In Section
4
we will write down more general augmented harmonic maps based on the above four examples. A central philosophy in this paper is that the above families form the essential building blocks for constructing new gravitational instantons. As an illustration of this idea we investigate the example of Euclidean Kerr spaces, and show that in a limit situation they decouple into the union of a Taub-NUT space and an anti-Taub-NUT space.

Example 3.20

(Kerr)
.

The Euclidean Kerr spaces, modulo rescalings, form a one parameter family of asymptotically flat gravitational instantons
g_{a}
(
a\in(-\infty,\infty)
) on
S^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Recall that there is an explicit formula (see for example
[
CT10
]
)

(3.9)

g_{a}=\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}(d\phi_{1}+a\sin^{2}\vartheta\,d\phi_{2})^{2}+\frac{1}{\Sigma}{\sin^{2}\vartheta}(ad\phi_{1}-(s^{2}-a^{2})d\phi_{2})^{2}+\Sigma(\frac{1}{\Delta}ds^{2}+d\vartheta^{2}),

where
\Sigma
and
\Delta
are defined as

(3.10)

\Sigma=s^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\vartheta,\qquad\Delta=s^{2}-2ms-a^{2}.

Here the parameters
a
, and the coordinates
s
,
\vartheta
take the ranges

\quad a\in(-\infty,\infty),\quad s\geq s_{a},\quad\vartheta\in[0,\pi],

where
s_{a}
is defined as

s_{a}=m+\sqrt{m^{2}+a^{2}}.

We always fix
m=\frac{1}{2}
to eliminate the scaling freedom.
The Weyl–Papapetrou coordinates
(\rho,z,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
are related to the above coordinates
(r,\vartheta,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
via

(3.11)

\rho=\sqrt{s^{2}-2ms-a^{2}}\,\sin\vartheta,\qquad z=(s-m)\cos\vartheta+z_{a},

where

z_{a}:=\sqrt{m^{2}+a^{2}}.

The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\Kerr_{a}}
consists of 3 rods given by

\mathfrak{I}_{0}=(-\infty,0),\quad\mathfrak{I}_{1}=(0,2z_{a}),\quad\mathfrak{I}_{2}=(2z_{a},\infty).

Notice that here we have normalized the
z
coordinate such that the first turning point occurs at
z=0
.
The corresponding rod vectors are given by

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{2}=[(0,1)^{\top}],\qquad\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(\frac{1}{\kappa_{a}},\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\kappa_{a}})^{\top}],

where

\kappa_{a}:=\frac{z_{a}}{2ms_{a}},\qquad\Omega_{a}:=\frac{a}{2ms_{a}}.

The enhancement
\Lambda
is generated by
(0,1)^{\top}
and
(\frac{1}{\kappa_{a}},\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\kappa_{a}})^{\top}
. When
a=0
we get the Euclidean Schwarzschild space; this is the only case when the metric
g_{a}
is asymptotic to the flat product
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{1}
at infinity. When
\Omega_{a}/\kappa_{a}
is rational the asymptotic cone at infinity of
g_{a}
is a flat orbifold
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{R}
; when
\Omega_{a}/\kappa_{a}
is irrational the asymptotic cone at infinity of
g_{a}
is isometric to the half plane
\mathbb{H}
. See the discussion in
[
LS24
]
. With the canonical orientation
d\rho\wedge dz\wedge d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2}
, we refer to these gravitational instantons as
Kerr spaces
.

The augmented harmonic map for
g_{a}
is given by

(3.12)

\Phi^{\Kerr_{a}}=\frac{1}{\Sigma\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\Delta+a^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta&-2asm\sin^{2}\vartheta\\
-2asm\sin^{2}\vartheta&\sin^{2}\vartheta(a^{2}\rho^{2}+(s^{2}-a^{2})^{2})\end{matrix}\right),

(3.13)

\nu^{\Kerr_{a}}=\frac{1}{2}\log(\frac{\Sigma\sin^{2}\vartheta}{\rho^{2}\cos^{2}\vartheta+(s-m)^{2}\sin^{4}\vartheta}).

Now we consider the limit of
(\Phi^{\Kerr_{a}},\nu^{\Kerr_{a}})
as
a\rightarrow\infty
. Naturally we need to consider the pointed limits based at the two turning points. First we consider the turning point
z=0
. We fix a constant
C>0
large and consider the limit of
(\Phi^{\Kerr_{a}},\nu^{\Kerr_{a}})
as
a\rightarrow\infty
, on the region where
r^{2}=z^{2}+\rho^{2}\leq C
and
|\rho|\geq C^{-1}
. Using the equation

(3.14)

\frac{\rho^{2}}{(s-m)^{2}-m^{2}-a^{2}}+\frac{(z-z_{a})^{2}}{(s-m)^{2}}=1,

it is straightforward to obtain the following asymptotics

(3.15)

s=a+\frac{1}{2}(r-z+2m)+O(a^{-1}),

(3.16)

\sin^{2}\vartheta=(r+z)a^{-1}+O(a^{-2}),

(3.17)

\Sigma=2(m+r)a+O(1),

(3.18)

\Delta=(r-z)a+O(1).

It then follows that

(3.19)

\Phi^{\infty}:=\lim_{a\rightarrow\infty}\Phi^{\Kerr_{a}}=\frac{1}{2(m+r)\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}2r&-2m(r+z)\\
-2m(r+z)&(r+z)(\rho^{2}+(r-z+2m)^{2})\end{matrix}\right)

and

(3.20)

\nu^{\infty}:=\lim_{a\rightarrow\infty}\nu^{\Kerr_{a}}=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{m+r}{r}.

Recall that we have fixed
m=\frac{1}{2}
. Then one can check that

(3.21)

U_{-m}.\Phi^{\infty}=\eta^{*}\Phi^{\TNp},\quad\nu^{\infty}=\nu^{\TNp}=\nu^{\TNm},

where

\eta:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{H};\quad(\rho,z)\mapsto(\rho,-z).

Notice
\eta^{*}\Phi^{\TNp}
is essentially equivalent to
\Phi^{\TNm}
: when we define the anti-Taub-NUT space by changing the orientation on the Taub-NUT space, instead of interchanging the order of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
, we may change the orientation on the quotient space
\mathbb{H}
, which has the effect of replacing
z
by
-z
. Similarly, we can study the pointed limit based at the other turning point using the translation
T_{2z_{a}}
, where one sees instead
(U_{m}.\Phi^{\TNp},\nu^{\TNp})
. The following Figure
6
illustrates the situation.

\mathfrak{v}_{0}
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
\mathfrak{v}_{2}
2z_{a}
a\to\infty
\mathfrak{v}_{0,\infty}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{1,\infty}=[(1,1)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{1,\infty}
\mathfrak{v}_{2,\infty}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
Taub-NUT
anti-Taub-NUT

Figure 6.
The splitting of Kerr spaces

The above discussion shows that as
a\rightarrow 0
, we can see both
\Phi^{\TNp}
and
\Phi^{\TNm}
as pointed limits of
\Phi^{\Kerr_{a}}
. At the level of gravitational instantons, we may say that the Kerr spaces
g_{a}
split into the union of a Taub-NUT space and an anti-Taub-NUT space. By Lemma
4.40
, the
L^{2}
energy of
g_{a}
is given by

\int|\Rm(g_{a})|^{2}\dvol_{g_{a}}=16\pi^{2},

whereas the curvature energy of a Taub-NUT space is
8\pi^{2}
, so there is no energy loss in this splitting process. Geometrically this is an interesting phenomenon, which extends to more general situation. See the discussion in Section
8
.

As a remark, notice that the Kerr spaces are Hermitian non-Kähler (or, Type
\II
in terms of the terminology of Section
7.1
). According to
[
BG23
]
(see also Section
7.3
), they can alternatively be described using toric Kähler geometry, in terms of a piecewise linear convex function in one variable. However it does not seem straightforward to see the above splitting in this viewpoint. The reason is that among the two splitting limits, the anti-Taub-NUT is of Type
\II
but the Taub-NUT is of Type
\I
, so can not be put in the framework of toric Kähler geometry applied in
[
BG23
]
.

4.
Tame harmonic maps

In this section we study the analytic aspect of axisymmetric harmonic maps tamed by a rod structure.

4.1.
Model maps and tameness

First we write down local and infinity model harmonic maps. The local models involve
rod models
and
turning point models
. Then we define the notion of tameness for harmonic maps.

Definition 4.1

(Rod model)
.

Consider the harmonic map defined on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
given by

\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}:=\left(\begin{matrix}\rho&0\\
0&\rho^{-1}\end{matrix}\right).

This is the harmonic map associated to the flat product metric on
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, see (
3.7
).
In general for
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
we define

(4.1)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}=P_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]},

where
P_{\mathfrak{v}}
is defined in (
1.14
).

Definition 4.2

(Turning point model)
.

For
\alpha>0
we consider the harmonic map defined on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
given by

(4.2)

\Phi_{[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}],[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{\alpha r}{2}&z\\
z&\frac{2r}{\alpha}\end{matrix}\right).

When
\alpha=1
this is the harmonic map associated to the flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, see (
3.4
).
In general, for two elements
\mathfrak{v}\neq\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we define

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}=P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi_{[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}],[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]},

where
P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
is defined in (
1.10
) and
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
is defined in (
1.11
).

Next, we introduce 4 families of asymptotic (at infinity) models of augmented harmonic maps. These are motivated by the examples discussed in Section
3.3
.
Consider

(4.3)

\Phi=\rho^{-1}\left(\begin{matrix}H_{\alpha}^{-1}A_{\alpha}^{2}+\rho^{2}H_{\alpha}&H_{\alpha}^{-1}A_{\alpha}\\
H_{\alpha}^{-1}A_{\alpha}&H_{\alpha}^{-1}\end{matrix}\right).

and

(4.4)

\nu=\frac{1}{2}\log H_{\alpha},

for functions
A_{\alpha}
and
H_{\alpha}
to be specified below.

Definition 4.3

(
\Ae
model family)
.

The
\Ae
model family
(\Phi^{\Aea},\nu^{\Aea})
(for
\alpha>0
) is formed by setting

H_{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{2r},\ \ \ \ A_{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha z}{2r}.

One can check that indeed we have

\Phi^{\Aea}=\Phi_{[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}],[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]}.

More generally,
given
\mathfrak{v}\neq\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we define the
\Ae
model augmented harmonic map to be

(4.5)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\Ae}=P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\Aea},\ \ \ \nu_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\Ae}=\nu^{\Aea}=\frac{1}{2}\log(\frac{\alpha}{2r})

for
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.4

(
\TN^{-}
model family)
.

The
\TN^{-}
model family
(\Phi^{\TNam},\nu^{\TNam})
(for
\alpha\geq 0
) is formed by setting

(4.6)

H_{\alpha}=1+\frac{\alpha}{2r},\ \ \ \ A_{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha z}{2r}.

More generally, for
\mathfrak{v}\neq\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
we define the
\ALF^{-}
model augmented harmonic map to be

(4.7)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\ALF^{-}}=P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\TNam},\ \ \ \nu_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\ALFm}=\nu^{\TNam}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\frac{\alpha}{2r}),

for
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.5

(
\TN^{+}
model family)
.

The
\TN^{+}
model family
(\Phi^{\TNap},\nu^{\TNap})
(for
\alpha>0
) is defined with

\Phi^{\TNap}=R_{1}.\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha}},

\nu^{\TNap}=\nu^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha}}.

where
R_{1}
is given in (
1.8
).
More generally, for
\mathfrak{v}\neq\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
we define the
\ALF^{+}
model augmented harmonic map to be

(4.8)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\ALF^{+}}=P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{4/\alpha}},\ \ \ \nu_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\ALFp}=\nu^{\TN_{4/\alpha}^{+}}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\frac{2}{\alpha r}),

for
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
.

Notice that

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\ALF^{+}}=P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}R_{1}P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}.\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{\ALF^{-}}.

Definition 4.6

(
\AF
model family)
.

The
\AF
model family
(\Phi^{\AFa},\nu^{\AFa})
(for
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
)
is given by

(4.9)

\Phi^{\AFa}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}&\beta\rho^{2}\\
\beta\rho^{2}&\beta^{2}\rho^{2}+1\end{matrix}\right)=U_{-\beta}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]},\quad\nu^{\AFa}=0.

Given
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the
\AFa
model augmented harmonic map to be

(4.10)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}}^{\AFa}=P_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\AFa},\ \ \ \nu_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}}^{\AFa}=0.

Remark 4.7
.

Notice that by definition

\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{0}}=\Phi^{\AF_{0}}.

This fact will be important for our discussion in Section
5
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{H}
. We denote
U^{\circ}=U\cap\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
.

Definition 4.8

(Local tameness)
.

A smooth map
\Phi:U^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is said to be
locally tamed
by an untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
if

•

For any rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
and
z\in\mathfrak{J}_{j}\cap U
, there is a neighborhood
V
of
z
such that

\sup_{V^{\circ}}d(\Phi,\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}})<\infty;

•

For each turning point
z_{j}\in U
, there is a neighborhood
V
of
z_{j}
such that

\sup_{V^{\circ}}d(\Phi,T_{z_{j}}^{*}\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j},\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}})<\infty.

Definition 4.9

(Global tameness)
.

A smooth map
\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is said to be
globally tamed
by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
if it is locally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
and

\sup_{\mathbb{H}^{\circ}}d(\Phi,\Phi^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}})\leq C.

Definition 4.10

(Strong tameness)
.

We say a smooth map
\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is
strongly tamed
by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
if it is globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
and

d(\Phi,\Phi^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}})=O(r^{-1}),\ \ r\rightarrow\infty.

It is easy to check by definition that

•

For
\alpha>0
,
\Phi^{\Aea}
is strongly tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\Aea}
which is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
, with a single turning point
z_{1}=0
and with rod vectors given by

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}],\ \ \mathfrak{v}_{1}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}].

•

For
\alpha>0
,
\Phi^{\TNam}
is strongly tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha}}
which is of Asymptotic Type
\ALFm
, with a single turning point
z_{1}=0
and with rod vectors given by

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}],\ \ \mathfrak{v}_{1}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}].

•

For
\alpha>0
,
\Phi^{\TNap}
is strongly tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{+}_{\alpha}}=\overline{\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha}}}
which is of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
, with a single turning point
z_{1}=0
and with rod vectors given by

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{2}{\alpha}}],\ \ \mathfrak{v}_{1}=[\bv_{-\frac{2}{\alpha}}].

•

For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Phi^{\AFa}
is strongly tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\AFa}
which is of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
, with no turning points and with
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{0}]
.

In the next two subsections we will study the regularity of tame harmonic maps. From the PDE point of view, this question fits into the general study of harmonic maps with prescribed codimension 2 singularities, see
[
Wei20
,
LT92
,
HKWX22
]
for example. The focus of these previous works was on harmonic maps arising from the Lorentzian setting, which has similar but different asymptotics near the rods. In our Riemannian setting, we will combine these analytical results with the geometric point of view and take advantage of the regularity results for the Riemannian Einstein equation. The main result of this section is the following

Theorem 4.11
.

•

(Local) An augmented harmonic map, which is locally tamed by a smooth enhanced untyped rod structure, defines a toric Ricci-flat metric with possible codimension 2 conical singularities.

•

(Global) A smooth enhanced rod structure strongly tames a unique harmonic map on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. Moreover, there is a unique augmentation which defines a toric gravitational instanton with possible codimension 2 conical singularities.

The first part will be proved in Section
4.2
and the second part will be proved in Section
4.3
.

4.2.
Local theory

4.2.1.
Near a rod interior

Let
(\Phi,\nu)
be an augmented harmonic map defined on an open set
U\subset\mathbb{H}
which is locally tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
and assume
U\cap\Gamma
is contained in a rod
\mathfrak{I}
. Without loss of generality, we assume the rod vector is
\mathfrak{v}=[\bv_{0}]
, and the corresponding axisymmetric harmonic map is defined on the ball
B_{1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{H}=\{(X,Y)|X>0\}
. We set

X=e^{u+\log\rho},\quad Y=v,

then the harmonic map equation for
\Phi
reduces to the following system on
(u,v)

(4.11)

\displaystyle\Delta u=-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}e^{-2u}|\nabla v|^{2},

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}e^{-2u}\nabla v\right)=0.

The tameness assumption implies that
\sup_{U}d(\Phi,\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})\leq C_{0}
, which gives

(4.13)

\displaystyle|u|+\rho^{-1}|v|\leq CC_{0}.

Here,
C
is a constant independent of
C_{0}
.
A straightforward application of
[
LT92
,
Ngu11
]
gives the following regularity result.

Proposition 4.12

(
C^{1,\alpha}
regularity)
.

We have the following uniform bounds on
B_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)
:

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}v|\leq CC_{0}\rho^{2-k},\text{ for $k=0,1,2,3$},

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq CC_{0},\text{ for any $0<\alpha<1$},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}u|\leq CC_{0}\rho^{1-k}\text{ for any $k=1,2,3$}.

Here the constant
C
depends on
\alpha
but is independent of
C_{0}
.

Proof.

First by Theorem 4.1 and Lemma 5.1 in
[
LT92
]
we know that
u
extends to a Hölder continuous function across
\Gamma
and

|\nabla u|\leq C\rho^{-1+\epsilon_{0}}

for a uniform constant
C
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Notice that the arguments in
[
LT92
]
use a monotonicity formula for local minimizing harmonic maps. But as observed in
[
Ngu11
]
(see also
[
HKWX22
]
), this can be replaced by a Caccioppoli inequality. Since we have

C^{-1}\rho^{-2}\leq\rho^{-2}e^{-2u}\leq C\rho^{-2}

and

|\nabla u|\leq C\rho^{-1+\epsilon_{0}},

with the bound

\rho^{-1}|v|\leq CC_{0},

by applying Theorem 1 in
[
Ngu11
]
to the equation

\mathrm{div}(\rho^{-2}e^{-2u}\nabla v)=0,

we get
|v|\leq CC_{0}\rho^{2}
. From here it is a simple rescaling argument, using the standard Schauder estimate, to bound the higher derivatives of
v
. The bound on
u
then follows easily from (
4.11
).

∎

Remark 4.13
.

It is worth pointing out that in the Lorentzian setting of
[
LT92
,
Ngu11
,
HKWX22
]
the corresponding harmonic map has a different singular behavior and the regularity obtained therein is of
C^{3,\alpha}
. In our setting, we can only obtain
C^{1,\alpha}
regularity using directly the PDE result.

Now we turn to the geometry of the Ricci-flat metric associated to
\Phi
. We write the Gram matrix as

G=\rho\Phi=\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u}+e^{-u}v^{2}&e^{-u}v\\
e^{-u}v&e^{-u}\end{matrix}\right).

From this one can compute the 1-forms

(4.14)

\mathcal{U}=\rho G^{-1}G_{\rho}=\left(\begin{matrix}2+\rho u_{\rho}+\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}v_{\rho}v&\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}v_{\rho}\\
-2v-2\rho u_{\rho}v-\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}v_{\rho}v^{2}+\rho v_{\rho}&-\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}v_{\rho}v-\rho u_{\rho}\end{matrix}\right),

(4.15)

\mathcal{V}=\rho G^{-1}G_{z}=\left(\begin{matrix}\rho u_{z}+\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}vv_{z}&\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}v_{z}\\
-2\rho u_{z}v-\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}v^{2}v_{z}+\rho v_{z}&-\frac{1}{\rho}e^{-2u}vv_{z}-\rho u_{z}\end{matrix}\right).

We denote by
O^{\prime}(\rho^{k})
a function which is bounded by
C\rho^{k}
and with derivative bounded by
C\rho^{k-1}
. Then we see that

\displaystyle\Tr(\mathcal{U}^{2})

\displaystyle=2\rho^{2}u_{\rho}^{2}+4\rho u_{\rho}+2e^{-2u}v_{\rho}^{2}+4=4+4\rho u_{\rho}+O^{\prime}(\rho^{2}),

\displaystyle\Tr(\mathcal{V}^{2})

\displaystyle=2\rho^{2}u_{z}^{2}+2e^{-2u}v_{z}^{2}=O^{\prime}(\rho^{2}),

\displaystyle\Tr(\mathcal{U}\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle=2\rho u_{z}\left(\rho u_{\rho}+1\right)+2e^{-2u}v_{z}v_{\rho}=2\rho u_{z}+O^{\prime}(\rho^{2}).

Using (
2.21
) and (
2.22
), we see that

(4.16)

\displaystyle(2\nu-u)_{\rho}=O^{\prime}(\rho),

\displaystyle(2\nu-u)_{z}=O^{\prime}(\rho).

It follows that
\nu
is a
C^{1,\alpha}
function on
B_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)
.
Moreover, there is a constant
c_{0}
such that

2\nu-u=c_{0}

on
\Gamma
.

As in (
2.26
), we consider

g=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+G,

which is a well-defined Ricci-flat Riemannian metric on
U^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Now we take the lattice
\Lambda
generated by
[(2\pi e^{c_{0}/2},0)^{\top}]
and
[(0,2\pi)^{\top}]
, and consider the quotient metric
g_{\Lambda}
on
U^{\circ}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Consider the coordinate functions
x=\rho\cos(e^{-c_{0}/2}\phi_{1}),y=\rho\sin(e^{-c_{0}/2}\phi_{1}),z,\phi_{2}
, which diffeomorphically identify
U^{\circ}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
with an open subset
V^{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The Killing fields
e^{-c_{0}/2}\partial_{\phi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\phi_{2}}
correspond to the rotation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the translation on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The
\mathbb{R}^{1}
is parametrized by
z
. We set
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
to be the closure of
V^{\circ}
by adding points with
\rho=0
.

Lemma 4.14
.

The Ricci-flat metric
g
extends to a Lipschitz (i.e.
C^{0,1}
) metric on
V
.

Proof.

This follows from a straightforward computation. Notice that the map
(x,y,z,\phi_{2})\mapsto(\rho,z)
is distance decreasing so it suffices to check that the components of
g
in the
(dx,dy,dz,d\phi_{2})
frame are Lipschitz functions in
(\rho,z)
. To see this, one simply substitutes
d\rho=\rho^{-1}(xdx+ydy)
and
d\phi_{1}=\rho^{-2}(xdy-ydx)
and check the Lipschitz continuity of the components of
g
. We omit the details.
∎

Now using standard theory of harmonic coordinates we arrive at

Proposition 4.15
.

One can find a new
C^{1,\alpha}
coordinate system in a neighborhood of
0\in V
in which the components of
g
are smooth. In particular,
g
defines a smooth toric Ricci-flat metric.

Proof.

Since
g
is Lipschitz one can find a local
W^{2,p}
(for
p
arbitrarily large) harmonic coordinate system
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
. In these coordinates the components
g_{ij}
are
W^{1,p}
. Since
g
is Ricci-flat whenever it is smooth, we have the equation

\Delta g_{ij}+\partial g*\partial g*g^{-1}*g^{-1}=0

holds on
V^{\circ}
. Since
dg_{ij}
is in
L^{p}
and
V\setminus V^{0}
has Hausdorff codimension 2 (noticing that
g
is Lipschitz on
V)
, it is easy to see that the equation holds weakly across the singularities.
Using elliptic regularity and bootstrapping we obtain that
g_{ij}\in C^{\infty}
. Since a Killing field of a smooth Riemannian metric is always smooth, it follows that the metric
g
is indeed a toric Ricci-flat metric.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Since
\rho
has intrinsic meaning given by
\sqrt{\det G}
, it follows that a posteriori that
u
and
v
has improved regularity. For example we know that
u_{\rho}=0
when
\rho=0
.

Given a rod
\mathfrak{I}
with rod vector
\mathfrak{v}
, for any nonzero vector
v
in the line
\mathfrak{v}
, from the above discussion we see that

\lim_{\rho\to 0}\frac{1}{e^{\nu}\rho}\sqrt{\rho\Phi(v,v)}=c,

for a positive constant
c
, where the limit is taken uniformly for points in
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
