approaching the interior of the rod
\mathfrak{I}
.

Definition 4.17
.

We define the
normalized rod vector
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}
for the rod
\mathfrak{I}
to be the vector in the line corresponding to
\mathfrak{v}
, which is unique up to
\pm 1
, such that along
\mathfrak{I}
we have

\lim_{\rho\to 0}\frac{1}{e^{\nu}\rho}\sqrt{\rho\Phi(\overline{\mathfrak{v}},\overline{\mathfrak{v}})}=1.

Example 4.18
.

For the model augmented harmonic maps defined at the beginning of this section, we can explicitly compute the normalized rod vectors:

•

For the model
(\Phi^{\Aea},\nu^{\Aea})
and
(\Phi^{\TNam},\nu^{\TNam})
, the normalized rod vectors are given by
\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
and
\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
on
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
;

•

For the model
(\Phi^{\TNap},\nu^{\TNap})
, the normalized rod vectors are given by
\bv_{\frac{2}{\alpha}}
and
\bv_{-\frac{2}{\alpha}}
on
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
;

•

For the model
(\Phi^{\AFa},\nu^{\AFa})
, the normalized rod vector is given by
\bv_{0}
on
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
.

Now suppose that we are given a smooth enhancement
\Lambda
and let
\bbv
be a primitive rod vector along
\mathfrak{v}
. Consider the manifold
M^{\circ}:=U^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda
. Endowed with the quotient metric
g_{\Lambda}
,
M^{\circ}
is naturally a toric Ricci-flat manifold. As in Section
3
, there is a partial compactification
M
of
M^{\circ}
to a smooth toric manifold by adding points with stabilizers over
\Gamma\cap U
.

Definition 4.19

(Cone angle)
.

The cone angle
\vartheta(\mathfrak{I})
along a rod
\mathfrak{I}
, associated to the augmented harmonic map
(\Phi,\nu)
locally tamed by the enhanced rod structure
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
, is defined to be

\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I})=2\pi\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{e^{\nu}\rho}\sqrt{\rho\Phi(\bbv,\bbv)}.

In particular, we have

2\pi\cdot\bbv=\pm\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I})\cdot\overline{\mathfrak{v}},

and for any constant
C
,

\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu+C}(\mathfrak{I})=e^{-C}\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I}).

By Proposition
4.15
, the metric
g_{\Lambda}
has cone angle
\vartheta(\mathfrak{I})
along the 2-sphere in
M
corresponding to the rod
\mathfrak{I}
. This is the precise meaning of being
conical
in this paper. These types of conical singularities are very mild—they arise from an identification with an incorrect period, similar to orbifold singularities. In particular, the curvature of
g_{\Lambda}
is uniformly bounded across the conical singularities.

Next we explain the convergence of normalized rod vectors for a sequence of augmented harmonic maps. First we introduce a notion of convergence of harmonic maps for the convenience of later discussion. Suppose we have a sequence of smooth maps
\Phi^{k}
on
U^{\circ}\subset\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
where
U\subset\mathbb{H}
is an open subset with
U^{\circ}:=U\cap\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
, which is locally tamed by an untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{k}
such that for each
k
,
U\cap\Gamma
is contained in a unique rod
\mathfrak{I}^{k}
of
\mathfrak{R}^{k}
. Suppose that the corresponding rod vectors
\mathfrak{v}^{k}
converge to a limit
\mathfrak{v}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
\mathfrak{R}^{\infty}
be an untyped rod structure such that one of the rods contains
U\cap\Gamma
and the corresponding rod vector is
\mathfrak{v}^{\infty}
. Let
\Phi^{\infty}
be a smooth map on
U^{\circ}
which is locally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}^{\infty}
.

Definition 4.20
.

We say
\Phi^{k}
converges to a limit map
\Phi^{\infty}
in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
U
if the following hold

•

For any compact
K\subset U\cap\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
,
\Phi^{k}
converges to
\Phi^{\infty}
in
C^{1,\alpha}(K)
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

•

For any
z\in U\cap\Gamma
, there is a neighborhood
V
of
z
in
\mathbb{H}
, such that if we represent
P_{\mathfrak{v}^{k}}.\Phi^{k}
by functions
(u^{k},v^{k})
and
P_{\mathfrak{v}^{\infty}}.\Phi^{\infty}
by functions
(u^{\infty},v^{\infty})
, then
(u^{k},v^{k})
converges to
(u^{\infty},v^{\infty})
in
C^{1,\alpha}(V)
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Now suppose we have a sequence of augmented harmonic maps
(\Phi^{k},\nu^{k})
on
U
which are locally tamed by rod structures
\mathfrak{R}^{k}
and with a uniform bound
d(\Phi^{k},\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}^{k}})\leq C
. From the uniform estimate in Proposition
4.12
, by passing to a subsequence
\Phi^{k}
converges
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
U
to a limit harmonic map
\Phi^{\infty}
locally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}^{\infty}
. Furthermore, we have

d(\Phi^{\infty},\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}^{\infty}})\leq C.

Passing to a further subsequence we may find constants
C_{k}
such that
\nu^{k}+C_{k}
converges uniformly to a limit augmentation
\nu^{\infty}
. It follows that we have the convergence of normalized rod vectors

(4.17)

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}e^{C_{k}}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}^{k}=\overline{\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}.

Suppose furthermore that each
\mathfrak{R}^{k}
is enhanced by
\Lambda_{k}
and let
\bbv^{k}
be the corresponding primitive vector along
\mathfrak{v}^{k}
. It follows that

(4.18)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\vartheta_{\Phi^{k},\nu^{k}}(\mathfrak{I}^{k})}e^{C_{k}}\bbv^{k}=\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}}{2\pi}.

In particular, to understand the limiting behavior of the cone angles
\vartheta_{\Phi^{k},\nu^{k}}(\mathfrak{I}^{k})
, it suffices to study the limiting behavior of the enhancements
\Lambda_{k}
.

4.2.2.
Near a turning point

We again let
(\Phi,\nu)
be an augmented harmonic map defined on
U^{\circ}=U\cap\mathbb{H}
for some pen subset
U\subset\mathbb{H}
, which is locally tamed by an untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
U
is a neighborhood of the turning point
(0,0)\in\Gamma
and
U\cap\Gamma
contains exactly two rods
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
.

Again, there is a PDE study of the singular behavior at the turning points in the Lorentzian setting, see
[
HKWX22
]
. In our case we will instead use geometry to directly prove the regularity of the induced toric Ricci-flat metric, which fits our purpose in this paper. As in
[
HKWX22
]
it is convenient to apply an element in
SL(2;\mathbb{R})
such that the rod vectors are given by
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}=[v_{0}]
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=[v_{1}]
where

\displaystyle v_{0}=(1,1)^{\top},

\displaystyle v_{1}=(1,-1)^{\top}.

Then the model map
\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{1}}
given in (
4.2
) becomes

(4.19)

\Phi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{0},\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{\sin\theta}&\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\vskip 10.0pt\\
\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}&\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\end{matrix}\right),

where we used the polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
on
\mathbb{H}
, with
\rho=r\sin\theta
and
z=r\cos\theta
for
\theta\in[0,\pi]
.
Writing

\Phi=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{u}+e^{-u}v^{2}&e^{-u}v\\
e^{-u}v&e^{-u}\end{matrix}\right).

The harmonic map equation reduces to the following system of two equations:

(4.20)

\displaystyle\Delta u+e^{-2u}|\nabla v|^{2}=0,

(4.21)

\displaystyle\Delta v-2\nabla u\cdot\nabla v=0,

By assumption we have
d(\Phi,\Phi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{0},\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}})<\infty
, which implies that

(4.22)

|u-\log\sin\theta|\leq C,|v-\cos\theta|\leq C\sin\theta.

Consider the Ricci-flat metric on
U^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

g=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+\rho\Phi

Using the study in Section
4.2.1
we know that along each rod
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}(\alpha=0,1)
we have that

\lim_{\rho\to 0}\frac{1}{e^{\nu}\rho}\sqrt{{\rho\Phi(v_{\alpha},v_{\alpha})}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}c^{2}_{\alpha}>0.

Now we take the lattice
\Lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
generated by
v_{\alpha}/c_{\alpha}
. It follows that from Proposition
4.15
that
g
descends to a toric Ricci-flat metric over
U^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda
and it naturally extends to a smooth toric Ricci-flat metric on
B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, such that
v_{0}/c_{0}
and
v_{1}/c_{1}
generate the standard torus action.

Lemma 4.21
.

Over
U\setminus\{0\}
we have

(4.23)

\displaystyle|\partial_{r}^{m}\partial_{\theta}^{n}(v-\cos\theta)|\leq Cr^{-m}(\sin\theta)^{2-m-n}\text{ for $0\leq m+n\leq 3$},

(4.24)

\displaystyle|\partial_{r}^{m}\partial_{\theta}^{n}(u-\log\sin\theta)|\leq Cr^{-m}(\sin\theta)^{1-m-n}\text{ for $1\leq m+n\leq 3$}.

Proof.

Note that the harmonic map equations (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) are invariant under the rescaling
u_{R}(r,\theta)=u(R^{-1}r,\theta)
and
v_{R}(r,\theta)=v(R^{-1}r,\theta)
.
By considering the rescaled maps
(u_{R},v_{R})
, it suffices to prove uniform estimates on a fixed annulus
A_{{\frac{1}{2}},1}
in
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. Away from the rods, the estimates follow from the standard interior estimates of Schoen-Uhlenbeck. It suffices to prove the estimates in a neighborhood of a rod. For simplicity we consider the rod
\theta=\pi
.

Denote

\widehat{\Phi}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{matrix}\right)\Phi\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{matrix}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{matrix}e^{u}+e^{-u}(v+1)^{2}&e^{u}+e^{-u}(v^{2}-1)\\
e^{u}+e^{-u}(v^{2}-1)&e^{u}+e^{-u}(v-1)^{2}\end{matrix}\right).

Then
\widehat{\Phi}
has bounded distance to
\frac{1}{\rho}\mathrm{diag}(r+z,r-z)
, hence to the model
\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}
on the annulus
A_{{\frac{1}{2}},1}
near the rod
\theta=\pi
. Set

\widehat{\Phi}=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{\widehat{u}}+e^{-\widehat{u}}{\widehat{v}}^{2}&e^{-\widehat{u}}\widehat{v}\\
e^{-\widehat{u}}\widehat{v}&e^{-\widehat{u}}\end{matrix}\right),

then one can apply the estimates in Proposition (
4.12
) to the functions
(\widehat{u}-\log\rho,\widehat{v})
near
\theta=\pi
and obtain that

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}\widehat{v}|\leq C\rho^{2-k}\text{ for $k=0,1,2,3$},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}(\widehat{u}-\log\rho)|\leq C\rho^{1-k}\text{ for $k=1,2,3$}.

Returning to the original
(u,v)
we obtain the desired conclusion.∎

Lemma 4.22
.

Over
B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}
, we have
C^{-1}g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\leq g\leq Cg_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
.

Proof.

Notice that the flat metric
g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
can be written as

g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}=\frac{1}{2r}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+\rho\Phi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{0},\tilde{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}}.

It suffices to show that
e^{2\nu}
is uniformly comparable to
r^{-1}
.

Under the above rescaling we have
\nu_{R}(r,\theta)=\nu(R^{-1}r,\theta)
. Applying the estimate (
4.16
) to
\nu_{R}
on the fixed annulus
A_{\frac{1}{2},1}
we see that
\nu_{\theta}
is uniformly bounded.

Now consider
(u,v)
on
A_{1/R,1}
. Let
\widehat{\Phi}
be as given in the proof of Lemma
4.21
. From the discussion in Section
4.2.1
, we know that
2\nu-\widehat{u}+\log\rho
is a constant on the rod
\theta=\pi
in
A_{1/R,1}
. This together with our assumption
|u-\log\sin\theta|<C
imply that

|(2\nu+\log r)|_{\{\theta=\pi\}}|<C

in
A_{1/R,1}
, for
C
independent of
R
.
Let
R\to\infty
, the uniform bound on
\nu_{\theta}
then implies that
e^{2\nu}
is uniformly comparable to
r^{-1}
on
B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}
, as desired.
∎

It follows that the metric completion of
g
is given by adding the origin
0
in
B_{1}(0)
and the distance function to the origin
0
with respect to the metric
g
is comparable to
r^{{\frac{1}{2}}}
. From Lemma
4.21
with a rescaling argument, we obtain the curvature bound

|\Rm_{g}|_{g}\leq C\dist_{g}(0,\cdot)^{-2}.

Proposition 4.23
.

There is a diffeomorphism
F:B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}\to B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}
, such that
F^{*}g
extends as a
C^{\infty}
metric to
B_{1}(0)
.

Proof.

Using the chopping theorem of Cheeger-Gromov we may find a sequence of neighborhoods
V_{j}
of
0
such that
\partial V_{j}\subset A_{j^{-1},2j^{-1}}(0)
and
\partial V_{j}
has second fundamental form bounded by
C_{j}
. Applying the Chern-Gauss-Bonnet theorem on
B_{{\frac{1}{2}}}(0)\setminus V_{j}
and the Ricci-flat equation, and letting
j\rightarrow\infty
we see that

\int_{B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}}|\Rm_{g}|_{g}^{2}\dvol_{g}<\infty.

Then by the result of Bando-Kasue-Nakajima
[
BKN89
]
(see also
[
DS14
]
, Section 5) the conclusion follows.
∎

4.3.
Global theory

The following existence result should be standard (see for example
[
Wei20
]
,
[
KL21
]
). For completeness and for our later purpose we include a short proof here.

Theorem 4.24
.

Given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
, there is a unique harmonic map
\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
.

Proof.

We first prove the uniqueness statement. Suppose we have two harmonic maps
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}
which are strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
. It is a standard computation that the function
f=d(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
can be viewed as an axisymmetric sub-harmonic function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Gamma
. The strong tameness assumption implies that
f
is uniformly bounded and decays to
0
at infinity. In particular,
f
extends to a nonnegative subharmonic function on the entire
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so it must vanish identically by the maximum principle.

Now we proceed to prove the existence. We only deal with the case of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
(where
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}
); the other cases are similar. Given
\mathfrak{R}
, choose
N
bigger than the size
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R})
, we want to construct a good background smooth map
\widehat{\Phi}:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
. Choose
\tau>0
such that
100\tau\leq|z_{j}-z_{j-1}|
for all
j
. We first define
\widehat{\Phi}
to be equal to

•

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}^{\AFa}
outside
B_{2N}(0)
;

•

T_{z_{j}}^{*}\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j},\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}}
in
B_{\tau}(z_{j})
for a turning point
z_{j}
;

•

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}}
in
N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{j})\setminus(B_{5\tau}(z_{j})\cup B_{5\tau}(z_{j+1}))
for a finite rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
;

•

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}
in
(B_{N}(0)\cap N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{0}))\setminus B_{5\tau}(z_{1})
and
(B_{N}(0)\cap N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{n}))\setminus B_{5\tau}(z_{n})
.

It is easy to check that

•

d(\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}},T_{z_{j}}^{*}\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j},\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}})<\infty
in
(B_{5\tau}(z_{j})\cap N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}))\setminus B_{\tau}(z_{j})
for a turning point
z_{j}
;

•

d(\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}},T_{z_{j}}^{*}\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{j},\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}})<\infty
in
(B_{5\tau}(z_{j})\cap N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{j+1}))\setminus B_{\tau}(z_{j})
for a turning point
z_{j}
;

•

d(\Phi^{\AFa}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}},\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}})<\infty
in
(B_{2N}(0)\cap N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{0}))\setminus B_{N}(0)
and
(B_{2N}(0)\cap N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{I}_{n}))\setminus B_{N}(0)
.

So by Lemma
4.25
below and a similar interpolation argument, one can extend
\widehat{\Phi}
to
N_{\tau}(\Gamma)\setminus\Gamma
such that
|\Delta\widehat{\Phi}|\leq C
. Since
\mathcal{H}
is simply-connected we can further extend
\widehat{\Phi}
smoothly to
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
such that on
B_{2N}(0)\setminus B_{N}(0)
we have
d(\widehat{\Phi},\Phi^{\AFa}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}})\leq C
. Notice that
\Delta\widehat{\Phi}=0
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus{B_{2N}(0)}
. The function

(4.25)

F(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|x-y|^{-1}|\Delta\widehat{\Phi}|(y)dy>0

is well-defined, It satisfies that
\Delta F=-|\Delta\widehat{\Phi}|
and
F=O(r^{-1})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
.
Now for any
\delta>0
, on the domain
U_{\delta}=B_{1/\delta}(0)\cap\{\rho>\delta\}
one can solve the Dirichlet problem to get a smooth harmonic map
\Phi_{\delta}:U_{\delta}\to\mathcal{H}
with boundary value
\widehat{\Phi}|_{\partial U_{\delta}}
. As
\mathcal{H}
is negatively curved, we have

\Delta{d(\Phi_{\delta},\widehat{\Phi})}\geq-|\Delta\widehat{\Phi}|=\Delta F

in the weak sense, which gives

\Delta(d(\Phi_{\delta},\widehat{\Phi})-F)\geq 0.

Maximum principle then implies that over
U_{\delta}
we have

d(\Phi_{\delta},\widehat{\Phi})\leq F.

Passing to a limit as
\delta\to 0
, we get a smooth harmonic map
\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{H}
with
d(\Phi,\widehat{\Phi})\leq F
.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

Suppose
\Psi
is harmonic map on
B_{\delta}(0)\cap\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
such that

•

d(\Psi,\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})\leq c
;

•

writing
\Psi
as
(u,v)
as in Section
4.2.1
then
|\nabla^{k}u|,|\nabla^{k}v|<C_{k}
.

Then there are
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{\delta}{100})
and
C_{k}^{\prime}>0
depending only on
c
and
C_{k}
, and a smooth map
\Phi:N_{\epsilon}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, such that

•

\Phi=\Psi
in
B_{\epsilon}(0)
,
\Phi=\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}
in
N_{\epsilon}(\Gamma)\setminus B_{10\epsilon}(0)
;

•

writing
\Phi
in terms of
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
as in Section
4.2.1
again then
|\nabla^{k}u^{\prime}|,|\nabla^{k}v^{\prime}|<C_{k}^{\prime}
.

In particular,
|\Delta\Phi|\leq C
.

Proof.

We can express
\Psi
in terms of a pair of two functions
(u,v)
as in Section
4.2.1
. On the other hand, the rod model map
\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}
corresponds to
(u_{0},v_{0})\equiv(0,0)
. The assumption implies that
|u|+\rho^{-1}|v|\leq c
. Using the estimates in Proposition
4.12
and a simple cut-off function
\chi=\chi(z)
one can achieve the conclusion.
∎

Let
\Phi
be a harmonic map on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
which is strongly tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
. For each
\sharp
we define the model augmentation

\nu^{\sharp}=\nu_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}}^{\sharp},

using the definitions in Section
4.1
.

Proposition 4.26
.

There is a unique choice of the augmentation
\nu
such that
\nu=\nu^{\sharp}+O(r^{-1})
.

Proof.

First consider the case where
\Phi
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. Without loss of generality we assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
. Similar to the local analysis in Section
4.2
, since
d(\Phi,\Phi^{\Ae_{\alpha}})<C/r
, by scaling down one can show that
\nu=\nu^{\Ae_{\alpha}}+\sigma+O(r^{-1})
for a constant
\sigma
. We only need to choose
\nu
such that
\sigma=0
.

Next consider the
\AFa
case. We may assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{0}]
. In this case for
R
large we consider a modified rescaling

\Phi_{R}=D_{\sqrt{R}}.U_{\beta}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi

\nu_{R}=\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\nu.

It is easy to see that

D_{\sqrt{R}}.U_{\beta}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi^{\AFa}=\Phi^{\AF_{0}}.

Then on the annulus we have
A_{1,2}

d(\Phi_{R},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})\leq CR^{-1}.

Then we can apply the local uniform estimates as in Section
4.2
to get that
\nu=\sigma+O(r^{-1})
for a constant
\sigma
. We choose
\nu
such that
\sigma=0
.

The
\ALFm
case can be treated in a similar fashion. Again we assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
. As in the
\AFa
case we need to perform a modified rescaling.
First consider

\Phi_{R}=D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi.

One can check that for any
\delta>0
small, on
A_{1,2}\setminus N_{\delta}(\Gamma)
, we have a uniform control on the corresponding operation on the model
\widetilde{\Phi}_{R}:=D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha}}
:

d(\widetilde{\Phi}_{R},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{R}.

From this one obtains that
\nu=\sigma+O(r^{-1})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
on the set
\{\rho\geq\delta r\}
. We choose
\nu
such that
\sigma=0
.

It remains to understand
\nu
in the set
\{\rho\leq\delta r\}
. We only consider the case when
z<0
. Consider

\Phi_{R}=D_{\sqrt{R}}.L_{-\alpha/2}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi.

Correspondingly for
\widetilde{\Phi}_{R}:=D_{\sqrt{R}}.L_{-\alpha/2}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}
, we have that on
A_{1,2}\cap N_{\delta}(\Gamma_{-})
,

d(\widetilde{\Phi}_{R},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{R}.

Then as before we again obtain that
\nu=O(r^{-1})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
, on the set
\{\rho\leq\delta r\}
.

The
\ALFp
case is similar and we omit the details.
∎

Definition 4.27

(Normalized augmentation)
.

We call the unique choice
\nu
in Proposition
4.26
the
normalized
augmentation.

Remark 4.28
.

Suppose we only consider the normalized augmentation, then by Theorem
4.24
and the discussion at the end of Section
4.2
we know that for each rod
\mathfrak{I}
, the normalized rod vector associated to
\mathfrak{I}
depends continuously on the rod vectors. If we further fix an enhancement, then the cone angle along
\mathfrak{I}
depends continuously on the rod lengths. This is sufficient for our purpose in this paper. It may be interesting to explore better behavior of the cone angle function. We mention that in the Lorentzian setting, the analogous cone angle function was shown to be differentiable in
[
HKWX25
]
.

4.4.
From tame harmonic maps to gravitational instantons

Consider an augmented harmonic map
(\Phi,\nu)
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
which is globally tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
. Given any smooth enhancement
\Lambda
, the quotient
M^{\circ}:=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda
is then naturally a toric Ricci-flat manifold, with the metric given by

(4.26)

g_{\Lambda}=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+\rho\Phi.

As before
M^{\circ}
may be compactified into a smooth toric manifold
M
by adding points with stabilizers over
\Gamma=\partial\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. The regularity results in Section
4.2
imply that
g_{\Lambda}
is conical, with a cone angle
\vartheta_{j}=\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I}_{j})
along the spheres
S_{j}^{2}
corresponding to the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
.

Using the model augmented harmonic maps introduced at the beginning of this section, we obtain asymptotic models of gravitational instantons.

•

(M^{\Ae},g^{\Ae})
: consider the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\Ae_{1}}
with the smooth enhancement
\Lambda
generated by
\bv_{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\bv_{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and use the augmented harmonic map
(\Phi^{\Ae_{1}},\nu^{\Ae_{1}})
.
This is the flat
\mathbb{R}^{4}
(as discussed in Example
3.15
).

•

(M^{\TNm},g^{\TNm})
: consider the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{-}_{1}}
with the smooth enhancement
\Lambda
generated by
\bv_{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\bv_{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and use the augmented harmonic map
(\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{1}},\nu^{\TN^{-}_{1}})
. This is the anti-Taub-NUT space (as discussed in Example
3.17
).

•

(M^{\TNp},g^{\TNp})
: consider the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\TN^{+}_{1}}
with the smooth enhancement
\Lambda
generated by
\bv_{2}
and
\bv_{-2}
, and use the augmented harmonic map
(\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{1}},\nu^{\TN^{+}_{1}})
. This is the Taub-NUT space (as discussed in Example
3.16
).

•

(M^{\AFa},g^{\AFa})(\beta\in\mathbb{R})
: consider the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\AFa}
with the standard smooth enhancement
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and use
(\Phi^{\AFa},\nu^{\AFa})
. This is the quotient
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}/\langle q_{\beta}\rangle
, as discussed in Example
3.18
. Notice that
(M^{\AFa},g^{\AFa})
and
(M^{\AF_{\beta+1}},g^{\AF_{\beta+1}})
are isometric as toric flat manifolds.

In each of the above model space, we denote by
\mathfrak{r}
the distance function.

Definition 4.29
.

A
\mathbb{T}
-isometry
between two toric Ricci-flat 4-manifolds
(M_{1},g_{1})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})
is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
F:M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
such that
F^{*}g_{2}=g_{1}
and
F(t.x)=t.F(x)
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

Definition 4.30
.

A 4-dimensional toric Ricci-flat end
(M,g)
is said to be of Asymptotic Type

•

\ALe
if it has a finite cover which admits a
\mathbb{T}
-isometry to the end of the model
(M^{\Ae},g^{\Ae})
, such that

|\nabla^{k}_{g}(g-g^{\Ae})|_{g}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-k-\delta})

for some
\delta>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

\ALF^{\pm}
if it has a finite cover which admits a
\mathbb{T}
-isometry to the end of the model
(M^{\ALF^{\pm}},g^{\ALF^{\pm}})
, such that

|\nabla^{k}_{g}(g-g^{\ALF^{\pm}})|_{g}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-k-\delta})

for some
\delta>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

\AFa
if it admits a
\mathbb{T}
-isometry to the end of the model
(M^{\AFa},g^{\AFa})
, such that

|\nabla^{k}_{g}(g-g^{\AFa})|_{g}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-k-\delta})

for some
\delta>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 4.31
.

One can check that (see Remark
3.19
) if we reverse the orientation, then up to an automorphism of the torus
\mathbb{T}
, an
\ALE
end is still an
\ALE
end, an
\ALF^{\pm}
end becomes an
\ALF^{\mp}
end, and an
\AFa
end becomes an
\AF_{-\beta}
end. The automorphism of the torus
\mathbb{T}
changes the
\mathbb{T}
action, but it does not change the geometry of the metrics.

Definition 4.32
.

A toric gravitational instanton
(M,g)
is said to be of Asymptotic Type
\ALe/\ALFp/\ALFm/\AFa
if its end is of Asymptotic Type
\ALe/\ALFp/\ALFm/\AFa
.

Remark 4.33
.

In the literature sometimes the
\ALF^{\pm}
and
\AFa
Asymptotics Types are both referred to as the
\ALF
Asymptotic Type. We choose to distinguish between these two cases, partly due to the fact that in the
\AFa
case we have the extra continuous parameter
\beta
which changes the asymptotic behavior of the metric, but in the
\ALF
case such a continuous parameter does not exist, by the result proved in
[
LS24
]
.

Now given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
. Let
(\Phi,\nu)
be an augmented harmonic map on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
which is globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
, with
\nu
normalized. Given any lattice
\Lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}
are generated by vectors in
\Lambda
, as above by working on the complement of a large compact set
K\subset\mathbb{H}
, we obtain a toric Ricci-flat
end

(E_{\Lambda},g_{\Lambda})
with possible conical singularities. We can make the end smooth by choosing a canonical lattice
\Lambda
.

Definition 4.34

(Canonical lattice)
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}
be a rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
. We define the canonical lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}

associated to
\mathfrak{R}
as follows.

•

If
\sharp=\Ae,\ALF^{-}
, then
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is generated by
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}}^{-1}.\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
and
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}}^{-1}.\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, where
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
;

•

If
\sharp=\ALF^{+}
, then
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is generated by
\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}}^{-1}.\bv_{\frac{2}{\alpha}}
and
\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}}^{-1}.\bv_{-\frac{2}{\alpha}}
, where
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
;

•

If
\sharp=\AFa
, then
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is generated by
P_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}.\bv_{0}
and
P_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}.[(0,1)^{T}]
.

Remark 4.35
.

If
\mathfrak{R}
is enhanceable, then
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is an enhancement for
\mathfrak{R}
.

Proposition 4.36
.

The toric Ricci-flat end
(E_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}},g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
is smooth without conical singularities and of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
.

Proof.

We only discuss the case of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
; the other cases are similar. Without loss of generality we assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
as before. In this case,
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is generated by
\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
and
\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.
Then the cone angle

\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I}_{0})=\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I}_{n})=2\pi.

In particular we know
(M,g)
is smooth outside a compact set. It follows from Proposition
A.1
proved in the Appendix, that
g
has an end of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
.
∎

Now let
\Lambda
be a smooth enhancement for
\mathfrak{R}
. If
\Lambda
coincides with
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
, then
(M,g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
is a gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
, but with possible codimension 2 conical singularities in the compact region. In general, the space is asymptotic to a quotient of the model ends.

Remark 4.37
.

Conversely, one can show that any toric gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\ALE/\ALF^{\pm}/\AF_{\beta}
(up to scaling) may arise from a harmonic map which is strongly tamed by an enhanced rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\ALE/\ALF^{\pm}/\AFa
, for an appropriate choice of a smooth enhancement. Here in the
\ALF^{\pm}/\AFa
case a scaling of the metric is necessary, since the model end is not invariant under rescaling.

4.5.
The case of Asymptotic Type
\Ae

We first prove a rigidity result.

Proposition 4.38
.

Suppose
\Phi
is a harmonic map globally tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. Then there is a
Q\in SL(2;\mathbb{R})
with
Q.\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{0}
,
Q.\mathfrak{v}_{n}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}
, such that
Q.\Phi
is the unique harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
Q.\mathfrak{R}
(as produced in Theorem
4.24
). Here we make
Q.\mathfrak{R}
into a rod structure by assigning its Asymptotic Type to be
\Ae
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume
\bv_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
for some
\alpha>0
.
We first choose an arbitrary augmentation
\nu
. Let
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}=\mu_{0}\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{n}=\mu_{n}\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
be the normalized rod vectors on the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{n}
respectively. Then we adjust
\nu
by a constant such that
\mu_{0}\cdot\mu_{n}=1
. There is the matrix
P\in SL(2;\mathbb{R})
that maps
\mu_{0}\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}},\mu_{n}\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
to
\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}},\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.
Replacing
\Phi
by
P.\Phi
we can assume
\mu_{0}=\mu_{n}=1
.

Define
\Lambda
to be the lattice generated by
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{n}
.
From Section
4.2
, we know that
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda,\Phi,\nu)
defines a smooth toric Ricci-flat end
(M,g)
. Denote by
\partial_{\phi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\phi_{2}}
the Killing fields on
M
corresponding to
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{n}
respectively.

As in the proof of the local regularity near a turning point in Section
4.2.2
, by scaling down
we know
g
is uniformly equivalent to the flat metric
g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
and with quadratic curvature decay. Applying the Chern-Gauss-Bonnet theorem in Lemma
4.40
we see that
\int_{M}|\Rm_{g}|^{2}\dvol_{g}<\infty
.

Now we may apply the arguments of Bando-Kasue-Nakajima
[
BKN89
]
to conclude that
g
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. Note that only the information of a Ricci-flat
end
is needed for the arguments in
[
BKN89
]
to go through. One can diffeomorphically identify
M
with
\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus K
for some compact
K
, such that

|\nabla^{k}(g-g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}})|=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-4-k})\quad\text{for all }k\geq 0.

Since a Killing field
X
for
g
satisfies
\nabla_{g}^{*}\nabla_{g}X=0
, using the asymptotics of
g
and standard elliptic theory for
g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
, we see that there is a rotational Killing field
\widetilde{X}
for
\mathbb{R}^{4}
such that

|\nabla^{k}(X-\widetilde{X})|_{g}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{1-\epsilon-k})

for some
\epsilon>0
. It follows that the torus
\mathbb{T}
action is asymptotic to a standard
\mathbb{T}
action on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, in the sense that the distance between the corresponding
\mathbb{T}
orbits is of order
O(\mathfrak{r}^{1-\epsilon})
.

By the standard center-of-mass techniques of Grove-Karcher (see, for example,
[
Ron96
]
), we may assume the diffeomorphism is equivariant with respect to the
\mathbb{T}
action. Thus, one can identify the Killing fields
\partial_{\phi_{1}}
and
\partial_{\phi_{2}}
as the Killing fields on
(\mathbb{R}^{4},g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}})
which give rise to the harmonic map
\Phi^{\Aea}
.

Denote by
G=\rho\Phi
and
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{\rho}\widetilde{\Phi}
the Gram matrices associated to
g
and
g_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
, respectively. Notice that

\widetilde{\Phi}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\rho}}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{\alpha\widetilde{r}}{2}&\widetilde{z}\\
\widetilde{z}&\frac{2\widetilde{r}}{\alpha}\end{matrix}\right).

The asymptotics of
g
imply that

|\nabla^{k}(\widetilde{G}G^{-1})|_{g}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-\epsilon-k})\quad\text{for all }k\geq 0.

In particular,
d(\widetilde{\Phi},\Phi)=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-\epsilon})
. On the
\mathbb{T}
-quotient, we have
\widetilde{\rho}\rho^{-1}=1+O(r^{-\epsilon})
, which then gives
\widetilde{z}=z+O(r^{1-\epsilon})
. It then follows that

d(\widetilde{\Phi},\Phi^{\Aea})=O(r^{-\epsilon}).

Altogether, we obtain

d(\Phi,\Phi^{\Aea})=O(r^{-\epsilon}).

Arguing as in the proof of the uniqueness part in Theorem
4.24
we conclude that
\Phi=\Phi^{\Aea}
.

∎

Remark 4.39
.

It may be possible to prove the above result by a pure PDE method. Although not needed in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, similar rigidity results also hold in the
\ALF^{\pm}
and
\AFa
case, see Proposition
8.2
.

Lemma 4.40
.

Let
(M,g)
be a 4-dimensional Ricci-flat end satisfying
|\Rm_{g}|=O(r^{-2})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
, then we have

\int_{M}|\Rm_{g}|^{2}\dvol_{g}<\infty.

In particular, if
(M,g)
is complete, then it is a gravitational instanton, and moreover,

(4.27)

\chi(M)=\int_{M}|\Rm_{g}|^{2}\dvol_{g}+\delta(M),

where
\delta(M)=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}
if
M
is of Asymptotic Type
\ALe
with asymptotic cone
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
, and
\delta(M)=0
otherwise.

Proof.

The first fact follows from the theorem of Cheeger-Gromov and Gauss-Bonnet-Chern theorem on 4-manifolds with boundary. For the second statement, if
(M,g)
has Euclidean volume growth then we know it is of Asymptotic Type
\ALe
by
[
BKN89
]
and
|\Rm|=O(r^{-3})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
, then the desired formula follows from Gauss-Bonnet-Chern theorem. If
(M,g)
has non-Euclidean volume growth then we know the end of
M
has an
\mathcal{F}
-structure so we can exhaust
M
by bounded domains
M_{j}
with
\chi(M_{j})=\chi(M)
. Then we apply the chopping theorem of Cheeger-Gromov and the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern theorem to conclude, noticing that the boundary integral goes to zero as
j\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

In the case of
2
turning points, we can now classify all harmonic maps which are strongly tamed by a rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
.
Given
n_{1},n_{2},a>0
, we consider the
Eguchi-Hanson harmonic map

(4.28)

\Phi_{\EH;n_{1},n_{2},a}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}H^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{2}+\rho^{2}H&H^{-1}\mathcal{A}\\
H^{-1}\mathcal{A}&H^{-1},\end{matrix}\right),

where

H=\frac{n_{1}}{2r}+\frac{n_{2}}{2r_{2a}},

\mathcal{A}=\frac{n_{1}z}{2r}+\frac{n_{2}(z-2a)}{2r_{2a}},

and

r_{2a}=\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-2a)^{2}}.

The associated rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\EH;n_{1},n_{2},a}
consist of two turning points
z_{1}=0
and
z_{2}=2a
, and three rod vectors

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}}],\ \ \mathfrak{v}_{1}=[\bv_{\frac{n_{2}-n_{1}}{2}}],\ \ \mathfrak{v}_{2}=[\bv_{-\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}}].

We choose the augmentation
\nu=\frac{1}{2}\log H
, then one can compute the normalized rod vectors are given by

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}=\bv_{\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}},\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}=\bv_{\frac{n_{2}-n_{1}}{2}},\ \ \overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}=\bv_{-\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}}.

The associated gravitational instantons are the well-known Eguchi-Hanson gravitational instantons. These are hyperkähler metrics, which can also be obtained via the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz. See the discussion in Section
7.1.1
.

Suppose we are given an enhancement
\Lambda
. Then we can find primitive rod vectors
\bbv_{0},\bbv_{1},\bbv_{2}
such that

\bbv_{1}=\gamma_{0}\bbv_{0}+\gamma_{2}\bbv_{2}

for some
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{2}>0
.
Denote
\vartheta_{j}=\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I}_{j})
. Then we have

\vartheta_{1}\bv_{\frac{n_{2}-n_{1}}{2}}=\gamma_{0}\vartheta_{0}\bv_{\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}}+\gamma_{2}\vartheta_{2}\bv_{-\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}}.

In particular, we have

(4.29)

\vartheta_{1}=\gamma_{0}\vartheta_{0}+\gamma_{2}\vartheta_{2}.

The upshot is that the cone angles along any two rods determine the cone angle along the third rod.

Lemma 4.41
.

Any harmonic map
\Phi
strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
with
2
turning points is given by the Eguchi-Hanson harmonic map
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{2}}^{-1}.\Phi_{\EH;n_{1},n_{2}}
for some
n_{1},n_{2}>0
, where
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{2}=n_{1}+n_{2}
.

Proof.

It follows from the definition that
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{2}}.\Phi
is strongly tamed by the rod structure
P.\mathfrak{R}
(with Asymptotic Type again
\Ae
). We can find unique
n_{1},n_{2}>0
such that the rod vectors coincide with that of
\mathfrak{R}_{\EH;n_{1},n_{2}}
.
The conclusion then follows from the uniqueness statement in Theorem
4.24
.
∎

We now prove an
angle comparison
result, which is a consequence of the Bishop-Gromov volume monotonicity. Let
(\Phi,\nu)
be an augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
and suppose that
\Lambda
is a smooth enhancement generated by vectors
\bbv_{0}\in\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\bbv_{n}\in\mathfrak{v}_{n}
. For each
j=0,\cdots,n
, we denote
\vartheta_{j}=\vartheta_{\Phi,\nu}(\mathfrak{I}_{j})
.

Proposition 4.42
.

Suppose
n\geq 2
, then we have

(4.30)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}\geq\vartheta_{n},\ \ \ \ and\ \ \ \ \vartheta_{n-1}\geq\vartheta_{0}.

Proof.

Since the inequalities are preserved if we adjust
\nu
by a constant, we may assume all the cone angles
\vartheta_{j}\in(0,2\pi)
. We only prove the first inequality; the second one can be proved in the same way.

As before we let
(M,g)
be the toric Ricci-flat metric with cone angle
\vartheta_{j}
along the 2-sphere
S_{j}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
. Since the enhancement is smooth,
M
is a smooth manifold. For
q\in M
denote by
B_{r}(q)\subset M
the geodesic ball of radius
r
around
q
. Denote by
p\in M^{\sharp}
the point corresponding to the turning point
z_{1}
.

We claim that the function

F(r)=\omega_{4}^{-1}r^{-4}\Vol_{g}(B_{r}(p))

is decreasing in
r
, where
\omega_{4}
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. This is a version of the Bishop-Gromov inequality and it is not difficult to adapt the usual proof to our conical setting. The key observation is the weak convexity lemma below, which says that
M^{\circ}
is geodesically convex. Choose a sequence of points
p_{j}\in M^{\circ}
that converges to
p
and denote

F_{j}(r)=\omega_{4}^{-1}r^{-4}\Vol_{g}(B_{r}(p_{j})).

From the geodesic convexity we know that the inequality

\Delta d(p_{j},\cdot)\leq\frac{3}{d(p_{j},\cdot)}

holds weakly on
M^{\circ}\setminus\{p_{j}\}
. Integrating this and noticing that the complement
M\setminus M^{\circ}
has zero Hausdorff measure, we obtain the monotonicity of
F_{j}
. Taking limit as
j\rightarrow\infty
the claim then follows.

Now it is a simple computation that

\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}F(r)=\vartheta_{0}\cdot\vartheta_{1}.

On the other hand, since
\Phi
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
, we see that

\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}F(r)=\vartheta_{0}\cdot\vartheta_{n}.

So we have
\vartheta_{1}\geq\vartheta_{n}
.

∎

Lemma 4.43

(Weak convexity)
.

M^{\circ}
is geodesically convex when all
\vartheta_{j}<2\pi
, i.e., any minimizing geodesic connecting
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
in
M^{\circ}
is contained in
M^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma:[0,L]\rightarrow M^{\circ}
be a unit-speed minimizing geodesic from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}\in M^{\circ}
. Suppose the claim does not hold and let
t_{0}>0
be the first time such that
w=\gamma(t_{0})\in M\setminus M^{\circ}
.

Consider the tangent cone
\mathcal{C}
at
w
, which is given by
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\vartheta_{j}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
if
w
is contained in
M^{*}
, and is
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\vartheta_{j}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\vartheta_{j+1}}
if
w
is a point in
M^{\sharp}
. Here
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\vartheta}
denotes the 2-dimensional flat cone with angle
\vartheta
. It is easy to see that after passing to a subsequence
\gamma
scales to a limit minimizing geodesic line
\gamma_{\infty}
in
\mathcal{C}
passing through the limit
w_{\infty}
of
w
. In particular,
w
must be contained in
M^{*}
, since there is no geodesic line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\vartheta_{j}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\vartheta_{j+1}}
when
\vartheta_{j},\vartheta_{j+1}<2\pi
passing through the origin
w_{\infty}
. The line
\gamma_{\infty}
must be contained in the singular set of
\mathcal{C}
. So
\gamma
must be tangential to the closure of some component of
M^{*}
, say
S_{j}
, at the point
w
. The latter is a submanifold diffeomorphic to
S^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Next we claim that for some
\epsilon>0
small,
\gamma|_{[t_{0}-\epsilon,t_{0}]}
must be contained in
M\setminus M^{\circ}
entirely, which leads to a contradiction. To see this, notice that by the local analysis in Section
4.2
,
locally near
w
we may rescale the primitive rod vector
\bbv_{j}
to unwrap the conical singularity. More precisely, in a neighborhood
U
of
w
, consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover
\widehat{U}
of
U\setminus S_{j}
, obtained by unwrapping along the primitive rod vector
\bbv_{j}
. Next, take the
\mathbb{Z}
-quotient of
\widehat{U}
, generated by the normalized rod vector
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}
, and complete the space by adding back the part
U\cap S_{j}
, denoted by
S_{j}^{\prime}
. This construction yields a smooth toric Ricci-flat metric
(U^{\prime},g^{\prime})
, containing a totally geodesic 2-dimensional submanifold
S_{j}^{\prime}
.

Now, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, the restriction
\gamma|_{[t_{0}-\epsilon,t_{0}]}
lifts to a minimizing geodesic
\gamma^{\prime}
in
U^{\prime}
, which is tangent to some point
w^{\prime}\in S_{j}^{\prime}
but not contained entirely in
S_{j}^{\prime}
. However, since
S_{j}^{\prime}
is totally geodesic and
g^{\prime}
is smooth, it follows that any geodesic tangent to
S_{j}^{\prime}
at a point must lie entirely within
S_{j}^{\prime}
. This leads to a contradiction.
∎

5.
Degeneration of rod structures and bubbling of harmonic maps

In this section, we examine the limiting behavior of a sequence of harmonic maps which are strongly tamed by a sequence of enhanced rod structures. For our purpose in Section
6
, it is crucial to analyze the bubbling phenomenon when turning points come together or diverge to infinity. This is done in Section
5.2
and
5.3
. We were informed by Marcus Khuri that similar bubbling analysis in the Lorentzian setting was performed in
[
HKWX25
]
for a different purpose. In Section
5.4
we apply the bubbling analysis to obtain information on the limiting behavior of cone angles when we work with a fixed enhancement and Asymptotic Type but vary the rod lengths.

5.1.
Rescaling of model harmonic maps

We first study the effect of the rescaling map
\varphi_{\lambda}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
on the model families of harmonic maps we wrote down in Section
4.1
. For the
\Ae
family we have rescaling invariance, that is, for any
\lambda>0
,

(5.1)

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\Aea}=\Phi^{\Aea}.

For the
\AF
family we have

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\AFa}=\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}(U_{-\beta}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})=U_{-\beta}.D_{{\sqrt{\lambda}}}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}=(U_{-\beta}D_{{\sqrt{\lambda}}}U_{\beta}).\Phi^{\AFa}.

For the
\TN^{-}
family we have

(5.2)

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}=\frac{\lambda}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}(1+\frac{{\alpha}\lambda}{2r})^{-1}(\frac{{\alpha}z}{2r})^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}(1+\frac{{\alpha}\lambda}{2r})&\ \ \ \ (1+\frac{{\alpha}\lambda}{2r})^{-1}\frac{{\alpha}z}{2r}\\
\\
(1+\frac{{\alpha}\lambda}{2r})^{-1}\frac{{\alpha}z}{2r}&(1+\frac{{\alpha}\lambda}{2r})^{-1}\end{matrix}\right)

We now examine the behavior of
\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}
as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
when
\lambda
also varies.
Notice that in any bounded subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
, the family
\Phi^{\TNam}
is uniformly bounded for
\alpha\in[0,2]
. The following lemma says that as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
, we also maintain a uniform control of
\Phi^{\TNam}
outside a fixed neighborhood of the turning point. Recall that on
\mathbb{H}
we have polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
satisfying
\rho=r\sin\theta
and
z=r\cos\theta
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For all
r_{0}>0
there is a
C(r_{0})>0
such that for all
\alpha\in[0,2]
we have

•

d(\Phi^{\TNam},\Phi_{[\bv_{\alpha/2}]})\leq C(r_{0})
, if
r>r_{0}
and
\theta>\frac{\pi}{4}
;

•

d(\Phi^{\TNam},\Phi_{[\bv_{-\alpha/2}]})\leq C(r_{0})
, if
r>r_{0}
and
\theta<\frac{3\pi}{4}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the first item; the other one is similar. By a straightforward computation, we have

\displaystyle\Tr((L_{\alpha/2}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})^{-1}\Phi^{\TNam})

\displaystyle=\Tr(\Phi^{-1}_{[\bv_{0}]}(L_{-\alpha/2}.\Phi^{\TNam}))=\rho^{-2}H_{\alpha}^{-1}(A_{\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{2})^{2}+H_{\alpha}+H_{\alpha}^{-1},

\displaystyle=H_{\alpha}^{-1}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4r^{2}}\frac{(1+\cos\theta)^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta}+H_{\alpha}+H_{\alpha}^{-1},

where
H_{\alpha}
and
A_{\alpha}
are given in (
4.6
).
The right hand side is clearly bounded by
C(r_{0})
when
\theta\in(\frac{\pi}{4},\pi]
. Next we have

\Tr((L_{\alpha/2}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})^{-1}\Phi_{[\bv_{\alpha/2}]})=\Tr(\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}^{-1}(L_{-\alpha/2}P_{[\bv_{\alpha/2}]}^{-1}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}))<C.

The conclusion then follows from (
1.6
). The last inequality is also a direct computation.
∎

For the
\TN^{+}
family we have a similar statement, with essentially the same proof. We omit the details.

Lemma 5.2
.

For all
r_{0}>0
there is a
C(r_{0})>0
such that for all
\alpha\in[1,\infty)
we have

•

d(\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{4/\alpha}},\Phi_{[\bv_{\alpha/2}]})\leq C(r_{0})
, if
r>r_{0}
and
\theta>\frac{\pi}{4}
;

•

d(\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{4/\alpha}},\Phi_{[\bv_{-\alpha/2}]})\leq C(r_{0})
, if
r>r_{0}
and
\theta<\frac{3\pi}{4}
.

Now, we take into account the parameter
\lambda>0
. If
\alpha\lambda\leq 2
, then we have

(5.3)

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}=D_{\sqrt{\lambda}}.\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha\lambda}},

and we may apply Lemma
5.1
. In particular, the behavior of
\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}
is controlled by the action of the twist
D_{\sqrt{\lambda}}
away from the turning point.

If
\alpha\lambda\geq\frac{1}{4}
, then we instead write

(5.4)

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}=D_{{1}/{\sqrt{\alpha}}}.\Upsilon_{\alpha\lambda}.

Here, we define

(5.5)

\Upsilon_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\rho}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha H_{\alpha}^{-1}A_{1}^{2}+\rho^{2}\alpha^{-1}H_{\alpha}&\alpha H_{\alpha}^{-1}A_{1}\\
\alpha H_{\alpha}^{-1}A_{1}&\alpha H_{\alpha}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Notice that if
\alpha=\infty
, we have

(5.6)

\Upsilon_{\infty}=\frac{1}{\rho}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{r}{2}&z\\
z&2r\end{pmatrix}=\Phi^{\Ae_{1}},

which is the harmonic map associated with the standard flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
r_{0}\in(0,1)
there is a
C(r_{0})
such that for all
{\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{4}
we have

•

d(\Upsilon_{{\alpha}},\Phi_{[\bv_{{1}/{2}}]})\leq C(r_{0})
, if
r_{0}<r<1/r_{0}
and
\theta>\frac{\pi}{4}
;

•

d(\Upsilon_{{\alpha}},\Phi_{[\bv_{-{1}/{2}}]})\leq C(r_{0})
, if if
r_{0}<r<1/r_{0}
and
\theta<\frac{3\pi}{4}
.

Proof.

Again it suffices to consider the first inequality. We have

\displaystyle\Tr((L_{1/2}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})^{-1}\Upsilon_{{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=\Tr(\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}^{-1}(L_{{-{1}/{2}}}.\Upsilon_{{\alpha}}))=\rho^{-2}{\alpha}H_{\alpha}^{-1}(A_{1}+\frac{1}{2})^{2}+({\alpha}^{-1}H_{\alpha}+{\alpha}H_{\alpha}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\alpha H_{\alpha}^{-1}\frac{1}{4}\frac{(1+\cos\theta)^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta}+({\alpha}^{-1}H_{\alpha}+{\alpha}H_{\alpha}^{-1}).

This is uniformly bounded as long as
r
is bounded. Now notice as before we have

\Tr((L_{1/2}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]})^{-1}\Phi_{[\bv_{1/2}]})=\Tr(\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}^{-1}(L_{-1/2}P_{[\bv_{1/2}]}^{-1}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}))<C.

The last inequality can be proved in a similar way.
∎

In particular, if
\alpha\lambda\geq\frac{1}{4}
, the behavior of
\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}
is controlled by the action of the twist
D_{{1}/{\sqrt{\alpha}}}
away from the turning point.

Fix a smooth function

\sigma:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}

such that

(5.7)

\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\lambda},&\text{if }\alpha\lambda<\frac{1}{4},\\
\sqrt{\lambda}/2\leq\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)\leq 2\sqrt{\lambda},&\text{if }\alpha\lambda\in[\frac{1}{4},1],\\
1/\sqrt{\alpha},&\text{if }\alpha\lambda>1.\end{cases}

Notice that for all
\alpha>0
and
\lambda>0
we have

(5.8)

\alpha\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)^{2}\leq 4,

and

(5.9)

\alpha\sigma(4/\alpha,\lambda)^{-2}\geq 1.

For
\lambda>0
and
\alpha\geq 0

we set

(5.10)

\Phi^{\lambda;\alpha}=D_{\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)}^{-1}.\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}.

Notice that here we allow
\alpha=0
. One can see that the harmonic map
\Phi^{\lambda;\alpha}
is locally tamed by the untyped weak rod structure with only one turning point
z_{1}=0
and two rod vectors
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{1}
given by

(5.11)

\displaystyle[\bv_{\alpha\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)^{2}/2}]\text{ and }[\bv_{-\alpha\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)^{2}/2}].

The upshot is that, by Lemma
5.1
-
5.3
, for any value of
\alpha\geq 0,\lambda>0
, up to some explicit twist that lies in a bounded subset of
SL(2;\mathbb{R})
, the map
\Phi^{\lambda;\alpha}
always has a controlled distance to one of the model maps on the corresponding regions away from the turning point.
For the
\TN^{+}
family the rescaled harmonic map can be controlled using the fact that

(5.12)

\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}\Phi^{\TNap}=R_{1}.D_{\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)}.\Phi^{\lambda;\alpha}=D_{\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)}^{-1}.R_{1}.\Phi^{\lambda;\alpha}.

5.2.
Background map in a fixed scale

Definition 5.4

(
(\epsilon,\delta)
-separate rod structure)
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{\epsilon}{100})
, a rod structure is called
(\epsilon,\delta)
-separate
if any two turning points
z_{j}
and
z_{k}
satisfy either
|z_{j}-z_{k}|\leq\delta
or
|z_{j}-z_{k}|\geq\epsilon
.

Now suppose we are given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
with
n
turning points. If
\mathfrak{R}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-separate, then we can partition the set of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}
into the union of
s\leq n
clusters,
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
,
\cdots
,
\mathfrak{C}_{s}
. Each cluster is given by

\mathfrak{C}_{p}=\{z_{p,1},\dots,z_{p,n_{p}}\}

with
z_{p,1}<\dots<z_{p,n_{p}}
and
p=1,\ldots,s
, satisfying the conditions

z_{p,n_{p}}-z_{p,1}\leq\delta,\quad\text{and}\quad z_{p+1,1}-z_{p,n_{p}}\geq\epsilon,

where we drop the second condition when
p=s
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\leq\delta
\leq\delta
\geq\epsilon
\mathfrak{C}_{p}
\mathfrak{C}_{p+1}
\mathfrak{J}_{p}
\mathfrak{w}_{p}
\mathfrak{w}_{p-1}
\mathfrak{w}_{p+1}

Figure 7.
Clusters of
(\epsilon,\delta)
-separate rods.

For
p\geq 1
, denote by
\mathfrak{J}_{p}
the rod of
\mathfrak{R}
connecting
z_{p,n_{p}}
and
z_{p+1,1}
. Denote by
\mathfrak{w}_{p}
the rod vector associated with the rod
\mathfrak{J}_{p}
. We also set
\mathfrak{w}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{w}_{s}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}
.
For
\tau>0
, we define

\mathfrak{J}_{p}^{\tau}=N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{J}_{p})\setminus N_{\tau}(\mathfrak{C}_{p}\cup\mathfrak{C}_{p+1}).

For each
p
with
n_{p}>1
, we define

\underline{z}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})=z_{p,1},

\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p})=(z_{p,n_{p}}-z_{p,1})^{-1},

and (see (
1.11
))

\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})=\mathfrak{w}_{p-1}\wedge\mathfrak{w}_{p}.

We then define an untyped rod structure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{p}}
associated with
\mathfrak{C}_{p}
,
whose turning points are given by

\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p})(z_{p,\alpha}-z_{p,1}),\quad\alpha=1,\cdots,n_{p},

and whose rod vectors coincide with those of
\mathfrak{R}
inside the cluster
\mathfrak{C}_{p}
on the finite rods and are given by
\mathfrak{w}_{p-1}
and
\mathfrak{w}_{p}
on the infinite rods. Finally, we define the untyped rod structure

(5.13)

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{p}}=\begin{cases}D_{\sigma(\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p}),\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p}))}^{-1}.P_{\mathfrak{w}_{p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{p}}.\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{p}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})<1,\\
D_{\sigma(4/\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p}),\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p}))}.P_{\mathfrak{w}_{p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{p}}.\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{p}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\geq 1.\end{cases}

By construction we have
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{p}})=1
. Note that the infinite rod vectors of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{p}}
are given by

(5.14)

\begin{cases}[\bv_{\frac{1}{2}\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\sigma(\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p}),\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p}))^{2}}]\text{ and }[\bv_{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\sigma(\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p}),\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p}))^{2}}],&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})<1,\\
[\bv_{\frac{1}{2}\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\sigma(4/\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p}),\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p}))^{-2}}]\text{ and }[\bv_{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\sigma(4/\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p}),\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{p}))^{-2}}],&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\geq 1.\end{cases}

Recall that for
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we have defined

(5.15)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}=P_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}.\Phi_{[\bv_{0}]}.

For
\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{R}
, we now define

(5.16)

\Phi_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime};\underline{z}}:=T_{\underline{z}}^{*}P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\TNam},

where
\alpha=\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
(see (
1.11
)), and

(5.17)

\overline{\Phi}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime};\underline{z}}:=T_{\underline{z}}^{*}\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{4/\alpha}}=T_{\underline{z}}^{*}{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}.\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{4/\alpha}}.

For the definition of
P_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
and
\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
see (
1.10
) through (
1.13
).

The proofs of the following two propositions are very similar to the construction in the proof of Theorem
4.24
, using Lemma
4.25
and the fact that
\mathcal{H}
is simply-connected. See Figure
8
. We omit the details.

Proposition 5.5

(Unrescaled model maps)
.

For all
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
\delta\in(0,\frac{\epsilon}{100})
and
\alpha_{0}>1
, there exits a constant
C=C(\epsilon,\alpha_{0})
such that for any rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
satisfying

•

\mathfrak{R}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-separate with size
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R})\leq 1
;

•

if the Asymptotic Type
\sharp\in\{\Ae,\ALF^{-},\ALF^{+}\}
, then
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\bv_{\alpha/2}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}=\bv_{-\alpha/2}
with

–

\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
if
\sharp=\Ae
or
\sharp=\ALFm
;

–

\alpha\in[\alpha_{0}^{-1},\infty)
if
\sharp=\Ae
or
\sharp=\ALFp
;

•

if the Asymptotic Type
\sharp=\AFa
, then
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}=\bv_{0}
and
|\beta|\leq\alpha_{0}
,

there is a smooth map

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sharp}:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\bigcup_{p=1}^{s}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}

such that

•

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sharp}=\Phi^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{n}}

(
see (
4.5
), (
4.7
), (
4.8
)
)
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(0)
;

•

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sharp}=\begin{cases}\Phi_{\mathfrak{w}_{p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{p};z_{p,1}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})<1,\\
\overline{\Phi}_{\mathfrak{w}_{p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{p};z_{p,1}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\geq 1,\end{cases}
on
N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\setminus N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})
for
p=1,\cdots,s
;

•

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sharp}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{w}_{p}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\cap\mathfrak{J}_{p}^{\frac{\epsilon}{5}}
;

•

|\Delta\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sharp}|\leq C
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\bigcup_{p=1}^{s}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})
;

•

For any
k\geq 1
,
|\nabla^{k}\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sharp}|\leq C(k,\epsilon)
on
B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(0)\setminus\bigcup_{p=1}^{s}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})
.

Remark 5.6
.

The constant
\alpha_{0}
is imposed to prevent the degeneration of the infinite rods which may destroy the uniform estimates. However we allow
\alpha\rightarrow 0
in the
\ALFm
case and
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
in the
\ALFp
case, since we have uniform control of the model maps in these settings, as discussed in Section
5.1
.

Proposition 5.7

(Rescaled model maps)
.

For all
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{\epsilon}{100})
, there exits a
C=C(\epsilon)
such that for all
\lambda>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
, and any rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
with rod vectors
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}
given by

\begin{cases}[\bv_{\alpha\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)^{2}/2}]\text{ and }[\bv_{-\alpha\sigma(\alpha,\lambda)^{2}/2}],\quad\text{if }\alpha<1;\\
[\bv_{\alpha\sigma(4/\alpha,\lambda)^{-2}/2}]\text{ and }[\bv_{-\alpha\sigma(4/\alpha,\lambda)^{-2}/2}],\quad\text{if }\alpha\geq 1,\end{cases}

there is a smooth map

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\lambda;\alpha}:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\bigcup_{p=1}^{s}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}

such that

•

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\lambda;\alpha}=\begin{cases}\Phi^{\lambda;\alpha},&\text{if }\alpha<1,\\
R_{1}.\Phi^{\lambda;4/\alpha},&\text{if }\alpha\geq 1,\end{cases}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(0)
;

•

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\lambda;\alpha}=\begin{cases}\Phi_{\mathfrak{w}_{p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{p};z_{p,1}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})<1,\\
\overline{\Phi}_{\mathfrak{w}_{p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{p};z_{p,1}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\geq 1,\end{cases}
on
N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})\setminus N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})
;

•

\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\lambda;\alpha}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{w}_{p}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\cap\mathfrak{J}_{p}^{\frac{\epsilon}{5}}
;

•

|\Delta\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\lambda;\alpha}|\leq C
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\bigcup_{p=1}^{s}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})
;

•

For any
k\geq 1
,
|\nabla^{k}\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\lambda;\alpha}|\leq C(k,\epsilon)
on
B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(0)\setminus\bigcup_{p=1}^{s}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{p})
.

5.3.
Uniform control of tame harmonic maps

Given a sequence of rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
with r
\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{R}_{i})\leq N
. We assume that for all
i
, the Asymptotic Type
\sharp_{i}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
is either
\Ae
, or
\ALFp
, or
\ALFm
, or
\AF_{\beta_{i}}
, and
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R}_{i})
uniformly bounded. Without loss of generality we may assume that for each
i
,
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R}_{i})\leq 1
and
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
has
n
turning points. We denote

\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i}):=\mathfrak{v}_{i,0}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{i,n}\in[0,\infty).

In addition, we assume there exists
\alpha_{0}>1
such that

•

\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})\leq\alpha_{0}
if
\sharp_{i}=\Ae
or
\sharp_{i}=\ALF^{-}
;

•

\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})\geq\alpha_{0}^{-1}
if
\sharp_{i}=\Ae
or
\sharp_{i}=\ALF^{+}
;

•

|\beta_{i}|\leq\alpha_{0}
if
\sharp_{i}=\AF_{\beta_{i}}
;

Again these assumptions are imposed in order to gain a uniform control for all
i
. Passing to a subsequence we may assume
\beta_{i}
has a finite limit
\beta_{\infty}
if
\sharp_{i}=\AF_{\beta_{i}}
, and
\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})
has a limit in
[0,\infty]
otherwise.

By Theorem
4.24
, there is a unique harmonic map
\Phi_{i}:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
. Our goal is to understand the limit and bubbling behavior of
\Phi_{i}
by comparing it with an explicitly constructed background map
\Psi_{i}
.

We can pass to a subsequence and assume that
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
is
(\epsilon,\delta_{i})
-separate for some fixed
\epsilon>0
and a sequence
\delta_{i}\to 0
.
Moreover, there exist exactly
s
clusters
\mathfrak{C}_{i,1},\dots,\mathfrak{C}_{i,s}
, with
s
independent of
i
, such that as
i\to\infty
, each
\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}
converges to a unique limit point
z_{\infty,p}
, and each
\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}
converges to a limit rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,p}
. We also set
z_{\infty,0}=-\infty
and
z_{\infty,s+1}=+\infty
. We denote

(5.18)

\lambda_{i,p}:=\lambda(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})

and

(5.19)

\alpha_{i,p}:=\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}).

In this way, we obtain a limit weak rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
, with turning points given by
\{z_{\infty,p}\}_{p=1}^{s}
and rod vectors given by
\{\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,p}\}_{p=0}^{s}
. We assign the Asymptotic Type of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
to be

•

\Ae
if
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
are of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
;

•

\AF_{\beta_{\infty}}
if
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
are of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{\beta_{i}}
with
\beta_{i}\rightarrow\beta_{\infty}
;

•

\ALF^{-}
if
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
are of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
and
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})>0
;

•

\ALFp
if
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
are of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
and
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})<\infty
;

•

\AF_{0}
if
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
are of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
and
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})=0
;

•

\AF_{0}
if
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
are of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
and
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\alpha(\mathfrak{R}_{i})=\infty
.

The rod structure is
weak
in general, since it is possible that some adjacent rod vectors coincide. Each limit turning point
z_{\infty,p}
is associated with a multiplicity
m_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, namely, the number of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
converging to
z_{\infty,p}
.

Definition 5.8
.

The weak rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
is referred to as a
weak limit
of the rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
.

To define the background maps
\Psi_{i}
we first set

\Psi_{i}:\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\bigcup_{p}N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}

to be the map

(5.20)

P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,0},\mathfrak{w}_{i,s}}^{-1}.\Psi_{P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,0},\mathfrak{w}_{i,s}}.\mathfrak{R}_{i}}^{\sharp}

given by Proposition
5.5
. By definition and Proposition
5.5
, in each cluster region
N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})\setminus N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})
, the expression (
5.20
) coincides with

(5.21)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p};z_{i,p,1}}=P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}^{-1}.T_{z_{i,p,1}}^{*}\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha_{i,p}}},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}<1,

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p};z_{i,p,1}}=P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}^{-1}.T_{z_{i,p,1}}^{*}\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{4/\alpha_{i,p}}},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1.

In particular, we see that for each
p
with
m_{p}=1
,
\Psi_{i}
obviously extends to
N_{\frac{\epsilon}{20}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})
on which it is harmonic and locally tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
.

For each
p
with
m_{p}>1
, we consider the sequence of rescaled rod structures (as defined in (
5.13
))

\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}:=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}}.

Again by passing to a further subsequence we may assume this has a limit weak, untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
as above. Furthermore,
\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
is
(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta_{i}^{\prime})
-separate for some fixed
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
and a sequence
\delta_{i}^{\prime}\to 0
.

Next we extend the domain of definition of
\Psi_{i}
to a smaller scale. Denote by
N^{\prime}_{i,p}
the subset of
N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})
obtained by removing the union of the
\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{20\lambda_{i,p}}
neighborhood of the clusters of
\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
. We now divide into two cases.

If
\alpha_{i,p}<1
, then we define
\Psi_{i}
on
N_{i,p}^{\prime}
to be

(5.22)

P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}^{-1}D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}.T_{{z}_{i,p,1}}^{*}\varphi_{1/\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}},

where
\sigma
is defined in (
5.7
) and
\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}
is given in Proposition
5.7
. Note that for
\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}
, on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(0)
, by definition it satisfies

D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}.\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}=D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}.\Phi^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}=\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha_{i,p}}}.

Hence, (
5.22
) clearly matches with (
5.21
) on the overlapping region
N_{\epsilon/10}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})\setminus N_{\epsilon/20}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})
(see Figure
9
). In other words, we have extended the domain of definition of
\Psi_{i}
into a smaller scale around the cluster
\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}
.

If
\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1
, then we define
\Psi_{i}
on
N_{i,p}^{\prime}
to be

(5.23)

P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}^{-1}D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}.T_{{z}_{i,p,1}}^{*}\varphi_{1/\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}.

For
\Psi^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}
, near infinity it satisfies

D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}.\Psi_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}}^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}=D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}.R_{1}.\Phi^{\lambda_{i,p};4/\alpha_{i,p}}=\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}\Phi^{\TN^{+}_{4/\alpha_{i,p}}}.

Again, (
5.23
) also matches with (
5.21
) on the overlapping region
N_{\epsilon/10}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})\setminus N_{\epsilon/20}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})
.

Note that from the matching above, we moreover have

(5.24)

\Psi_{i}=\begin{cases}\Phi_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p};z_{i,p,1}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})<1,\\
\overline{\Phi}_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p};z_{i,p,1}},&\text{if }\alpha(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})\geq 1,\end{cases}

on
N_{\frac{\epsilon}{10}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})\setminus N_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}\lambda_{i,p}}}(\mathfrak{C}_{i,p})
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

cluster

\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
deeper cluster

\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(0)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(0)}
.

) and (

) this is

which exactly matches
\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}\Phi^{\lambda_{i,p};\alpha_{i,p}}
over the overlap.

We may perform the above construction for each cluster
\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}
. Then we repeat this procedure for deeper scales, after passing to further subsequences. Notice that this process must terminate after finitely many steps, since the number of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
is uniformly bounded. In particular, in each scale we have
(\epsilon,\delta_{i})
separation for the rescaled rod structure, for a possibly smaller
\epsilon>0
.

The upshot is that after passing to a subsequence we have obtained a good model map
\Psi_{i}
for each
i
. By construction we know
\Delta\Psi_{i}
is zero except on the union of controlled number of annuli of the form
A_{r,Nr}
(centered at different points in
\Gamma
) in
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
for some uniform constant
N>0
. On each annuli we have a bound
|\Delta\Psi_{i}|\leq cr^{-2}
for a uniform constant
c>0
by scaling. We have a simple lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
f
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
which is supported in an annulus of the form
A_{r,Nr}
on which we have a bound
|f|\leq cr^{-2}
. Then there is a constant
C>0
depending only on
c
and
N
, such that on
\mathbb{R}^{3}

(1+r)\cdot|\mathcal{N}_{f}|\leq C,

where

\mathcal{N}_{f}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{f(y)}{|x-y|}dy.

Proof.

Denote
\bar{f}(x)=r^{2}f(rx)
. Then
\bar{f}
is supported on
A_{1,N}
and
|\bar{f}|\leq c
. Also
\mathcal{N}_{\bar{f}}(x)=\mathcal{N}_{f}(rx)
. It is then easy to estimate that
(1+r)\mathcal{N}_{\bar{f}}
is uniformly bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by a constant
C>0
depending only on
c
and
N
. ∎

Proposition 5.10
.

We have the following uniform pointwise bound for all
i

(5.25)

\sup_{\mathbb{H}^{\circ}}d(\Phi_{i},\Psi_{i})\leq C(1+r)^{-1}.

Proof.

This follows from the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
4.24
, using the above Lemma.
∎

By construction we have a good understanding on the sequence
\Psi_{i}
in all scales. The above bound then leads to a uniform control of
\Phi_{i}
in all scales, which allows us to get a
bubble tree
convergence for the family.

Recall that in the construction we have obtained a limit weak rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. Denote by
\mathcal{T}
the set of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. Let
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty}
be the natural rod structure associated to
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
obtained by merging possible adjacent rods sharing the same rod vector, and let
\Phi^{\prime}_{\infty}
be the unique harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, which is obtained by Theorem
4.24
.
Away from
\mathcal{T}
, by construction we know
\Psi_{i}
converges in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathcal{T}
to a limit map
\Psi_{\infty}
, which is locally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
. One can check that even though the convergence is only locally over
\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathcal{T}
, due to the the explicit model we use in the construction of
\Psi_{i}
, (
5.24
), and Lemma
5.1
and
5.2
, the limit map
\Psi_{\infty}
is indeed smooth on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
and strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, and it is harmonic outside a compact subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
.

By the bound in Proposition
5.10
, passing to a subsequence we see that
\Phi_{i}
also converges, in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathcal{T}
, to a limit harmonic map
\Phi_{\infty}
, which is locally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
and by Proposition
5.10
we have

d(\Phi_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty}^{\prime})\leq C(1+r)^{-1}.

It follows that
\Phi_{\infty}
is also strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, so the uniqueness statement in Theorem
4.24
implies that
\Phi_{\infty}=\Phi_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

Near a limit turning point
z_{\infty,p}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, as before after passing to a subsequence the rescaled sequence
\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
converges to a limit weak, untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
. Denote by
\mathcal{T}_{p}
the set of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
. Again we get an untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
naturally associated to
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
. Furthermore passing to a further subsequence we may assume the following to hold

•

Either
\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1
for all
i
, or
\alpha_{i,p}<1
for all
i
;

•

There is a limit
\alpha_{i,p}\rightarrow\alpha_{\infty,p}\in[0,\infty]
as
i\rightarrow\infty
;

•

If
\alpha_{i,p}<1
for all
i
, then as
i\rightarrow\infty
, there are limits

\alpha_{i,p}\lambda_{i,p}\rightarrow\kappa_{\infty,p}\in[0,\infty];

(5.26)

\sigma_{i,p}:=\alpha_{i,p}\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})^{2}\rightarrow\sigma_{\infty,p}\in[0,\infty].

•

If
\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1
for all
i
, then as
i\rightarrow\infty
, there are limits

\alpha_{i,p}^{-1}\lambda_{i,p}\rightarrow\kappa_{\infty,p}\in[0,\infty];

\sigma_{i,p}:=\alpha_{i,p}\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})^{-2}\rightarrow\sigma_{\infty,p}\in[0,\infty].

Denote

(5.27)

\Psi_{i,p}:=\begin{cases}\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}T_{-z_{i,p,1}}^{*}D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}.\Psi_{i},&\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}<1;\\
\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}T_{-z_{i,p,1}}^{*}D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}.\Psi_{i},&\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1.\end{cases}

Then
\Psi_{i,p}
is locally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
.
There are five cases:

(1)

\sigma_{\infty,p}=0
. By (
5.9
) in this case we must have
\alpha_{i,p}<1
. By (
5.14
), the two infinite rod vectors coincide. Then we endow
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
with Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. In this case
\kappa_{\infty,p}=0
.

(2)

\sigma_{\infty,p}=\infty
. By (
5.8
) in this case we must have
\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1
. Again by (
5.14
), the two infinite rod vectors coincide. Then we endow
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
with Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. In this case
\kappa_{\infty,p}=0
.

(3)

\sigma_{\infty,p}\in(0,\infty)
and
\kappa_{\infty,p}<\infty
and
\alpha_{i,p}<1
. By (
5.7
) in this case we must have
\kappa_{\infty,p}>0
. Notice by construction
\lambda_{i,p}\rightarrow\infty
so
\alpha_{\infty,p}=0
. In this case the two infinite rod vectors are different. Then we endow
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
with Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
.

(4)

\sigma_{\infty,p}\in(0,\infty)
and
\kappa_{\infty,p}<\infty
and
\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1
. By (
5.7
) in this case we must have
\kappa_{\infty,p}>0
. Notice by construction
\lambda_{i,p}\rightarrow\infty
so
\alpha_{\infty,p}=\infty
. In this case the two infinite rod vectors are different. Then we endow
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
with Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{+}
.

(5)

\sigma_{\infty,p}\in(0,\infty)
,
\kappa_{\infty,p}=\infty
. In this case the two infinite rod vectors are different. Then we endow
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
with Asymptotic Type
\Ae
.

In each case, Using Lemma
5.1
, Lemma
5.3
, and the construction in Proposition
5.7
, one can check that
\Psi_{i,p}
converges in
C^{1,\alpha}
, locally over
\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{p}
, to a smooth limit map
\Psi_{\infty,p}
. As before, an explicit computation show that
\Psi_{\infty,p}
is globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
.

By Proposition
5.10
, we see that after passing to a subsequence

(5.28)

\Phi_{i,p}:=\begin{cases}\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}T_{-z_{i,p,1}}^{*}D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}.\Phi_{i},&\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}<1;\\
\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}T_{-z_{i,p,1}}^{*}D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}.\Phi_{i},&\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1,\end{cases}

converges in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{p}
, to a limit harmonic map
\Phi_{\infty,p}
with

\sup_{\mathbb{H}^{\circ}}d(\Phi_{\infty,p},\Psi_{\infty,p})\leq C.

In particular,
\Phi_{\infty,p}
is globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
. If
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
, then by Proposition
4.38
, we know that for some
Q\in SL(2;\mathbb{R})
,
Q.\Phi_{\infty,p}
is strongly tamed by
Q.\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
(where we assign the Asymptotic Type to be
\Ae
). In the other cases we have similar statements (see Proposition
8.2
), but we do not need them until Section
8
. See also Remark
4.39
.

One can continue this process and obtain a bound on all the rescaled limits of
\Phi_{i}
in terms of the explicit limits of the model map
\Psi_{i}
. We call any one of such rescaled limits of
\Phi_{i}
a
bubble limit
.
By the above discussion we know that a bubble limit is globally tamed by some rod structure. Passing to a subsequence we then obtain a bubble tree structure for the sequence
\Phi_{i}
.

5.4.
Limiting behavior of the cone angles

Fix
n\geq 2
and an Asymptotic Type
\sharp\in\{\Ae,\ALFm,\ALFp,\AFa(\beta\in\mathbb{R})\}
.
Consider the space
\mathcal{S}(n,\sharp)
of all rod structures
\mathfrak{R}
which are of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
and with
n
turning points. It can be naturally identified with a subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n+1}
so has the induced topology.

For each
\mathfrak{R}\in\mathcal{S}(n,\sharp)
, by Theorem
4.24
and Proposition
4.26
there is a unique augmented harmonic map
(\Phi_{\mathfrak{R}},\nu_{\mathfrak{R}})
which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
and is such that
\nu_{\mathfrak{R}}
is normalized. From the discussion in Section
5.3
(see also Remark
4.28
) we know that for each fixed
j\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
, the normalized rod vector
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}
(as an element in
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\{\pm 1\}
) depends continuously on
\mathfrak{R}
. Recall that there is a canonical lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
given in Definition
4.34
that ensures the toric Ricci-flat end is smooth without conical singularities.

From now on we further fix
n+1
elements
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}
,
j=0,\cdots,n-1
. Consider the space
\mathcal{S}(\sharp|\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n})
of all enhanced rod structures
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}})
such that
\mathfrak{R}\in\mathcal{S}(n,\sharp)
, with rod vectors given by
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}
and with enhancement given by the canonical lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Since
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}
are fixed, it follows that
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is isomorphic to a fixed lattice
\Lambda
. Then
\mathcal{S}(\sharp|\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n})
is parametrized by the positions of the turning points. For
j\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
we then obtain the cone angle function

\vartheta_{j}:=\vartheta_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{R}},\nu_{\mathfrak{R}}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}).

It can be viewed as a continuous function of
z_{2},\cdots,z_{n}
(recall that
z_{1}=0
by definition), or equivalently of the rod lengths
l_{j}=z_{j+1}-z_{j}
, due to our considerations in Section
5.3
. By assumption we have

\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{n}\equiv 2\pi.

For our applications in this paper we are interested in the behavior of
\vartheta_{j}
when some of the turning points come together or diverge to infinity. The main complication lies in the fact when we rescale the rod structure and the harmonic map to obtain the bubble limits, we need to apply various twists (see the
D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}
introduced in (
5.28
)).

5.4.1.
Turning points coming together

Suppose now we have a sequence of enhanced rod structures
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Lambda)
in
\mathcal{S}(\sharp|\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n})
with
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R}_{i})
uniformly bounded and a fixed lattice
\Lambda
. Suppose we have a limit weak untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
as
i\rightarrow\infty
. Denote by
\mathcal{T}
the set of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
, and let
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
be the natural untyped rod structure associated to
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. We also endow
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
with the enhancement
\Lambda
and the Asymptotic Type
\sharp
. Notice that the infinite rod vectors of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
are again given by
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}.

Let
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
be the unique augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
such that
\nu_{i}
is normalized, and let
(\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty})
be the unique augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
such that
\nu_{\infty}
is normalized. Any rod
\mathfrak{I}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
is the limit of rods
\mathfrak{I}^{i}_{j}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
for some
j\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
.
The discussion in Section
5.3
implies that
\Phi_{i}
converges in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{T}
to
\Phi_{\infty}
. Since both
\nu_{i}
and
\nu_{\infty}
are normalized, we also have the local convergence of
\nu_{i}
to
\nu_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. Moreover, the normalized rod vector
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}
for the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i}
determined by
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
converges to the normalized rod vector for the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
determined by
(\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty})
, hence we have

(5.29)

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=\vartheta_{\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty}}(\mathfrak{I}).

Since
\Phi_{\infty}
is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, it follows that if the limits of
\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{k}^{i}
get merged into the same rod of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, then we have

(5.30)

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{k}^{i}).

Now consider a turning point
z_{\infty,p}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
with
m_{p}\geq 2
. For
i
large there is a corresponding cluster
\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}=\{z_{i,p,1},\cdots,z_{i,p,n_{p}}\}
. We rescale
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
around
z_{i,p,1}
to obtain rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{{i,p}}
(see (
5.13
)). The latter converges to a limit weak rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
, which then induces an associated rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
. The maps
\Phi_{i,p}
as given in (
5.28
) converge in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{p}
to the first bubble limit
\Phi_{\infty,p}
at
z_{\infty,p}
.
We know the latter is globally tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
. Notice that for the bubble limits, in general we do not have naturally associated enhancements, due to the fact that the fixed lattice
\Lambda
may get distorted in the rescaling process.

Denote the rescaled augmentation

(5.31)
