\widetilde{\nu}_{i}:=\varphi_{\lambda_{i}}^{*}T_{-{z}_{i,p,1}}^{*}\nu_{i}.

There are constants
c_{i}
such that
\nu_{i,p}:=\widetilde{\nu}_{i}+c_{i}
converges smoothly to a limit augmentation
\nu_{\infty,p}
for
\Phi_{\infty,p}
locally on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
, by the convergence of
\Phi_{i,p}
. Suppose we have a sequence of rods
\mathfrak{I}^{i}_{j}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
which converges to a limit rod
\mathfrak{I}
in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
. Denote by
\mathfrak{I}^{i^{\prime}}_{j}
the corresponding rod in
\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\prime}
the corresponding rod vector. Let
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}\in\mathfrak{v}_{j}
the normalized rod vector with respect to
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
, and denote by
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\prime}
the normalized rod vector with respect to
(\Phi_{i,p},\widetilde{\nu}_{i}+c_{i})
. The above discussion implies that
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}
converges to the normalized rod vector
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty}
along the rod
\mathfrak{I}
with respect to
(\Phi_{\infty,p},\nu_{\infty,p})
.

Lemma 5.11
.

In the above setting
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is either of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
or of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
.

Proof.

By definition by passing to a subsequence we know that in our setting
\alpha_{i,p}
is independent of
i
, since
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}
are fixed. If
\alpha_{i,p}\equiv 0
, then it follows that
\sigma_{\infty,p}=0
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. If
\alpha_{i,p}\equiv\alpha>0
, then since
\lambda_{i,p}\rightarrow\infty
, we must have
\kappa_{\infty,p}=\infty
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is then of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
.
∎

Now we first assume
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
, then by Proposition
4.38
we know that
Q.\Phi_{\infty,p}
is strongly tamed by
Q.\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
for a matrix
Q\in SL(2;\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, by our analysis in Section
5.3
in this case we have
\sigma_{\infty,p}\in(0,\infty)
. Indeed, since we have fixed the rod vectors
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}
,
\sigma_{i,p}
is independent of
i
for
i
large.

Let
\mathfrak{I}
be an infinite rod of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
. We know by construction that before rescaling the normalized rod vector
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}
converges to a normalized rod vector in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. So we have

C^{-1}\leq|\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}|\leq C.

It is straightforward to see that

(5.32)

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}e^{c_{i}}\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i},&\text{if }\ \alpha_{i,p}<1;\\
e^{c_{i}}\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i},&\text{if}\ \alpha_{i,p}\geq 1.\end{cases}

It follows from the uniform bound on
\sigma_{i,p}
and the convergence of
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}
that
e^{c_{i}}
is uniformly comparable to
1/\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}
, hence can be chosen such that

e^{c_{i}}\equiv 1/\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}.

Moreover, we let
\Lambda_{i,p}
be the enhancement for
\mathfrak{R}_{i,p}
defined by

(5.33)

\Lambda_{i,p}=\begin{cases}D_{\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}^{-1}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}.\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}},&\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}<1;\\
D_{\sigma(4/\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})}P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}.\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}},&\text{if }\alpha_{i,p}\geq 1.\end{cases}

Now for any
\mathfrak{I}
in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
, because of (
5.32
),
e^{c_{i}}\equiv 1/\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}
, and our particular choice of lattice (
5.33
), we have

(5.34)

\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}^{i}_{j})=\vartheta_{\Phi_{i,p},\nu_{i,p}}(\mathfrak{I}^{i^{\prime}}_{j}).

Note that since the rod vectors
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}
are fixed, the above
\Lambda_{i,p}
does not depend on
i
if
i
is large. Passing to a subsequence there is a limit enhancement
\Lambda_{\infty,p}
for
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
. Let
\bbv_{j}\in\mathfrak{v}_{j}
be the primitive rod vector in
\Lambda
, then it naturally induces a primitive rod vector
\bbv_{j}^{i^{\prime}}
along the rod
\mathfrak{I}^{i^{\prime}}_{j}
in
\Lambda_{i,p}
, which then converges to a primitive rod vector
\bbv_{\infty}
along the rod
\mathfrak{I}
in
\Lambda_{\infty,p}
. It follows that

(5.35)

\lim_{i\to\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}^{i}_{j})=\vartheta_{\Phi_{\infty,p},\nu_{\infty,p}}(\mathfrak{I}).

If
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
has 2 turning points, then by Proposition
4.38
and Lemma
4.41
, without loss of generality, up to modification by some
Q\in SL(2;\mathbb{R})
, we may assume
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{R}_{\EH;n_{1},n_{2}}
, and
\Phi_{\infty,p}^{\prime}=\Phi_{\EH;n_{1},n_{2}}
for some
n_{1},n_{2}
. Denote the 3 rods of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
by
\mathfrak{I}^{\infty}_{0},\mathfrak{I}^{\infty}_{1},\mathfrak{I}^{\infty}_{2}
. They come as the limits of 3 rods
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}^{i},\mathfrak{I}_{j_{1}}^{i},\mathfrak{I}_{j_{2}}^{i}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
. We may find primitive rod vectors
\bbv_{j_{0}},\bbv_{j_{1}},\bbv_{j_{2}}
in
\Lambda
along these rods respectively, such that

(5.36)

\bbv_{j_{1}}=\gamma_{0}\bbv_{j_{0}}+\gamma_{2}\bbv_{j_{2}}

for some
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{2}>0
. Then by (
4.29
) we have

(5.37)

\vartheta_{\Phi_{\infty,p},\nu_{\infty,p}}(\mathfrak{I}^{\infty}_{1})=\gamma_{0}\vartheta_{\Phi_{\infty,p},\nu_{\infty,p}}(\mathfrak{I}^{\infty}_{0})+\gamma_{2}\vartheta_{\Phi_{\infty,p},\nu_{\infty,p}}(\mathfrak{I}^{\infty}_{2})

Hence,

(5.38)

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j_{1}}^{i})}{\gamma_{0}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}^{i})+\gamma_{2}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j_{2}}^{i})}=1.

If
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
has more than 2 turning points, then we do not have as explicit information on the angle function as above, but we always have a
comparison property
. Namely, suppose
\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,m}
are the rod vectors of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
and suppose furthermore that the
limit lattice
\Lambda_{\infty,p}

•

is generated by primitive vectors
\bbv_{0}\in\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,0},\bbv_{m}\in\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,m}
;

•

gives a smooth enhancement for
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
.

Notice that the first assumption can always be achieved by passing to a coarser lattice before taking limit.
Under the above assumptions, by the the above discussion and Proposition
4.42
we get

Proposition 5.12
.

For
i
sufficiently large, suppose
\mathfrak{I}_{0}^{i}
,
\mathfrak{I}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{I}_{m-1}^{i},\mathfrak{I}_{m}^{i}
are the rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
that converge to the limit rods in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
with rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,0},\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,1},\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,m-1},\mathfrak{v}_{\infty,m}
respectively.
Then

(5.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{1}^{i})\geq\lim_{i\to\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{m}^{i}),

\displaystyle\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{m-1}^{i})\geq\lim_{i\to\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{0}^{i}).

Now we assume
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
, then we must have

\alpha_{i,p}\equiv 0\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma(\alpha_{i,p},\lambda_{i,p})=\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}.

Let
\mathfrak{I}
be a finite rod of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
, then it arises as the rescaled limit of the corresponding rod
\mathfrak{I}^{i}_{j}
in
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
for some
j\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
. The corresponding rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
is independent of
i
.
Let
{\mathfrak{I}}^{i}_{l}
be the rod of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
that converges to an infinite rod
\widetilde{\mathfrak{I}}
in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
. Denote by
\mathfrak{v}
the rod vector along
\widetilde{\mathfrak{I}}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

If
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq\mathfrak{v}
, then

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=\infty.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume
\mathfrak{v}=[\bv_{0}]
, hence for
\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}
, we know
\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1}=\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}=[\bv_{0}]
and
P_{\mathfrak{w}_{i,p-1},\mathfrak{w}_{i,p}}
is the identity map.
By construction we have the convergence of the harmonic maps

\Phi_{i,p}=\varphi_{\lambda_{i,p}}^{*}T_{-z_{i,p,1}}^{*}D_{\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}}^{-1}.\Phi_{i}

to
\Phi_{\infty,p}
in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{\infty,p}
, which is globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty,p}
. Let
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i}
be the normalized rod vectors for the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{l}^{i}
respectively, which are determined by
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
.
Denote by
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i^{\prime}}
the corresponding normalized rod vectors determined by
(\Phi_{i,p},\widetilde{\nu}_{i}+c_{i})
, where recall
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}
is defined in (
5.31
) and
c_{i}
is a suitably chosen constant to ensure the convergence of the augmentation
\tilde{\nu}_{i}+c_{i}
.
Since
\Phi_{\infty,p}
is the first bubble limit, in the original scale
{\mathfrak{I}}_{l}^{i}
converges to a limit rod in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
, so we have a uniform bound

C^{-1}\leq|\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i}|\leq C.

It is easy to check that

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i^{\prime}}=e^{c_{i}}D^{-1}_{\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}}\sqrt{\lambda_{i,p}}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i}.

Since
[{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i}]=[\bv_{0}]
, we see that

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i^{\prime}}=e^{c_{i}}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i}

and

C^{-1}e^{c_{i}}\leq|\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{l}^{i^{\prime}}|\leq Ce^{c_{i}}.

Since we have the convergence of normalized rod vectors of
(\Phi_{i,p},\widetilde{\nu}_{i}+c_{i})
in the rescaled limit
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
, we must have
|c_{i}|<C
. Suppose now that
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(a,b)^{\top}]
, where
b\neq 0
since
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq\mathfrak{v}
. Then we can write

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}=\mu_{i}(a,b)^{\top}

and

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{i}e^{c_{i}}(a,\lambda_{i,p}b)^{\top}.

If
b\neq 0
, since
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i^{\prime}}
converges, it is easy to see that
\mu_{i}
is uniformly comparable to
\lambda_{i,p}^{-1}
, so it converges to
0
. Hence we conclude that
\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I})\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

The above discussion provides information on the behavior of the cone angle function along the rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
which survive in the first bubble limit (namely,
(\Phi_{\infty,p},\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime})
). Note that by our assumption
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Lambda)\in\mathcal{S}(\sharp|\mathfrak{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{n})
, so all the rod vectors are fixed and do not depend on
i
. However, it does not yield a useful statement if the first bubble limit has a
trivial
rod structure, namely, if
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has no finite rods. This means that either
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has only one rod if it is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
, or
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has exactly two rods (which are the two infinite rods) if it is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. If these occur, we need to pass to even smaller scales.
Notice that from our initial hypothesis, it follows that the untyped rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
always has a finite rod. So in this situation we consider a turning point
q
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
which is not a turning point of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
. In other words,
q
lies in the interior of a rod
\mathfrak{I}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
and adjacent rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}
(sharing same rod vectors however) merge together at
q
. Denote by
\mathfrak{v}
the rod vector along
\mathfrak{I}
. In this case we see in the next scale the bubble limit at
q
, denoted by
(\Phi_{\infty,2},\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,2}^{\prime})
, must be of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
, since both of its infinite rod vectors coincide with
\mathfrak{v}
. It is possible that
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,2}^{\prime}
is again trivial, then there is another merging of rods in this bubble limit. If this occurs then we pass to a further smaller scale and obtain another bubble limit of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
whose infinite rod vectors again coincide with
\mathfrak{v}
. We can continue this process until at some scale we see a bubble limit
(\Phi_{\infty,k},\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,k}^{\prime})
whose rod structure contains more than one rod, then it has at least one finite rod. In all the intermediate scales we obtain bubble limits which are trivial and with the rod vector given by
\mathfrak{v}
. By construction,
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,k}^{\prime}
is non-trivial so it has a finite rod whose rod vector differs from
\mathfrak{v}
. Suppose we have a rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
which survives in this bubble limit and the limit rod vector differs from
\mathfrak{v}
. We may view such a bubble limit as a first
non-trivial
bubble limit.

Figure
10
gives an illustration of this phenomenon. We consider rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
with
4
turning points, whose rod length
l_{4}\ll l_{2}\ll l_{3}\ll l_{1}\to 0
. The limit rod structure is a trivial rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. In the first bubble limit only
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
survives and the rod structure is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. It is again trivial since
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
coincides with the infinite rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{5}
. In the next scale
\mathfrak{I}_{3}
survives in the bubble limit, and the rod structure is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
but is again trivial, since
\mathfrak{v}_{3}=\mathfrak{v}_{1}=\mathfrak{v}_{5}
. Then in the next scale
\mathfrak{I}_{2}
survives in the bubble limit. The corresponding rod structure that is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
is non-trivial and this bubble limit is the first non-trivial bubble limit. The rod
\mathfrak{I}_{4}
only survives after passing to the next scale.

\mathfrak{v}_{0}
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
\mathfrak{v}_{2}
\mathfrak{v}_{5}
l_{1}
l_{2}
l_{3}
l_{4}

Figure 10.
A sequence of rod structures with 4 finite rods
\mathfrak{I}_{1},\mathfrak{I}_{2},\mathfrak{I}_{3},\mathfrak{I}_{4}
whose lengths are given by
l_{4}\ll l_{2}\ll l_{3}\ll l_{1}\to 0
. The infinite rods are
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{5}
.

For the above situation, we have a similar conclusion as in Proposition
5.13
.

Proposition 5.14
.

We have

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=\infty.

Proof.

The key point to notice is that all the intermediate scales share the same infinite rod vector so when we pass to a smaller scale the accumulated twists are simply diagonal matrices of the form
D_{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}
for
\lambda_{i}>0
. The case when the first bubble limit
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
can be proved by the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
5.13
. In the case when
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
, one may reduce to the
\AF_{0}
case due to (
5.35
). Essentially, in this case the twists we need to use in order to obtain the first bubble limit
(\Phi_{\infty,p},\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime})
are uniformly bounded.
∎

By passing to smaller scales one may gain more information on the cone angles along other even shorter rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
. The analysis in general would involve understanding the iterative effect of the twists from all the intermediate scales. For our purpose we will not need this so we do not investigate it in detail.

5.4.2.
Turning points diverging to infinity

We first consider a sequence
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Lambda)
in
\mathcal{S}(\AFa|\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n})
for some
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathfrak{s}_{i}:=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R}_{i})\rightarrow\infty
. Again notice that the enhancement is a fixed lattice
\Lambda
. Without loss of generality we assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}=[\bv_{0}]
, and it follows that
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Let
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
(
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
) be the unique augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
such that
\nu_{i}
is normalized.

We define the twisted scaling down of the form

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}:=(U_{-\beta}D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{\beta}).\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\mathfrak{R}_{i},

which satisfies that
\mathfrak{s}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i})=1
. The twist by the action of
U_{-\beta}D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{\beta}
is necessary in order to apply the discussion in Section
5.3
. The point is that while our original harmonic map
\Phi_{i}
is asymptotic to a fixed
\AFa
model, the scaling down
\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\Phi_{i}
is instead asymptotic to

\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\Phi^{\AFa}=(U_{-\beta}D_{1/\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{\beta}).\Phi^{\AFa}.

However, the twisted scaling down

\widehat{\Phi}_{i}:=(U_{-\beta}D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{\beta}).\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\Phi_{i}

is again asymptotic to
\Phi^{\AFa}
, and it is indeed strongly tamed by
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}
. So we can apply the discussion in Section
5.3
to the new sequence of rod structures
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}
. We also define

\widehat{\nu}_{i}=\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\nu_{i},

then one can check that it gives the normalized augmentation for
\widehat{\Phi}_{i}
, as now we have

\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{0}^{i}=U_{-\beta}D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{\beta}\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}^{-1}}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}\equiv\bv_{0}.

As before passing to a subsequence there is a weak limit rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
. Again let
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
be the rod structure associated to
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
and denote by
\mathcal{T}
the set of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. We may assume that
\widehat{\Phi}_{i}
converges, in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{T}
, to a limit harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

Suppose a rod
\mathfrak{I}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
is the limit of the rod
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{j}^{i}
for some fixed
j\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
. It is easy to see that the limit rod vector
\mathfrak{v}
along
\mathfrak{I}
is given by

\mathfrak{v}=\begin{cases}[\bv_{0}],&\text{if}\ \mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq[(-\beta,1)^{\top}];\\
[(-\beta,1)^{\top}],&\text{if}\ \mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(-\beta,1)^{\top}].\end{cases}

The infinite rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
clearly have rod vector
[\bv_{0}]
. It is possible that some finite rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
are merged into the infinite rods of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
; such rods must arise from limits of rods
\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i}
with
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq[(-\beta,1)^{\top}]
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
(a,b)^{T}
be a primitive rod vector in
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
for
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Lambda)
. If the corresponding limit rod
\mathfrak{I}
belongs to the infinite rod or
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(-\beta,1)^{\top}]
, then

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=2\pi|a+\beta b|.

Proof.

Since
\widehat{\nu}_{i}
is normalized, the normalized rod vector along the infinite rods
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{0}^{i}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{n}^{i}
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}
, with respect to
(\widehat{\Phi}_{i},\widehat{\nu}_{i})
, is given by
\bv_{0}
. It follows that the normalized rod vector along other
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{j}^{i}
satisfies that

C^{-1}\leq|\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{j}^{i}|\leq C

due to the convergence of
(\widehat{\Phi}_{i},\widehat{\nu}_{i})
. Note that there is an explicit relation between
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}
and
\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{j}^{i}

\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{j}^{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{-\beta}D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}U_{\beta}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}.

Now let
(a,b)^{\top}
be a primitive rod vector in
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
, where
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}^{i}=\mu_{i}(a,b)^{\top}
. It follows that

\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{j}^{i}=\mu_{i}(a+b\beta-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}b\beta,\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}b)^{\top}.

If
\mathfrak{I}
belongs to the infinite rod in
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
then we have
\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{j}^{i}\to\bv_{0}
, which implies that

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{i}=\frac{1}{a+b\beta}.

So we have

\lim_{i\to\infty}\vartheta_{(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=2\pi|a+\beta b|.

If
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(-\beta,1)^{\top}]
, then as
|\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{v}}}_{j}^{i}|
remains bounded, we have
\mu_{i}
is uniformly comparable to
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
. In this case we then have

\lim_{i\to\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=0.

∎

Remark 5.16
.

If
\mathfrak{I}
does not belong to the infinite rod and
\mathfrak{v}_{j}\neq[(-\beta,1)^{\top}]
, then from the above argument we can only say that the limit of the angle function is finite.

Finally we consider a sequence
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Lambda)
in
\mathcal{S}(\ALFm|\mathfrak{v}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{v}_{n})
such that
\mathfrak{s}_{i}:=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{R}_{i})\rightarrow\infty
. Without loss of generality we assume in addition that
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\alpha/2}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}=[\bv_{-\alpha/2}]
for some fixed
\alpha>0
, where then it follows
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is generated by
\bv_{\alpha/2}
and
\bv_{-\alpha/2}
. Let
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
(
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
) be the unique augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
such that
\nu_{i}
is normalized.

Again we need to consider the scaling down. For the model map, we have by (
5.3
) that

\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}=D_{1/\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}.\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha/\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}}.

So we should consider the twisted scaling down

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}:=D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}.\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\mathfrak{R}_{i}

and

\widehat{\Phi}_{i}:=D_{\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}}.\varphi_{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}^{*}\Phi_{i}.

One can see that
\widehat{\Phi}_{i}
is strongly tamed by
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}
which is of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
. Then we can apply the discussion in Section
5.3
.

As before passing to a subsequence there is a weak limit rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. Again let
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
be the rod structure associated to
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
and denote by
\mathcal{T}
the set of turning points of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
. Since
\alpha/\sqrt{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\rightarrow 0
one sees that
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
should be equipped with Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. We may assume that
\widehat{\Phi}_{i}
converges, in
C^{1,\alpha}
locally over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{T}
, to a limit harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}
by passing to a subsequence.

Suppose a rod
\mathfrak{I}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
is the limit of the rod
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}_{j}^{i}
for some fixed
j\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
. One can see that the limit rod vector
\mathfrak{v}
along
\mathfrak{I}
is given by
[\bv_{0}]
if
\mathfrak{v}\neq[(0,1)^{\top}]
, and agrees with
\mathfrak{v}
if
\mathfrak{v}=[(0,1)^{\top}]
. Similar to the proof in the
\AFa
case, we have

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
(a,b)^{\top}
be the primitive rod vector in
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
for
(\mathfrak{R}_{j},\Lambda)
. If
\mathfrak{I}
belongs to the infinite rod or
a=0
, then

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{\Phi_{i},\nu_{i}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j}^{i})=2\pi|a|.

6.
Applications

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
. Our strategy is to design a smooth enhanced rod structure
(\mathfrak{R},\Lambda)
and demonstrate that the rod lengths can be adjusted such that the augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
and of a given Type, has cone angle
2\pi
along every rod. This will then generate a toric gravitational instanton.

6.1.
The case of two turning points

As a warm-up, we consider rod structures with exactly two turning points. It is easy to see that in this case the degree of the rod structure is at most one. According to Theorem
7.4
later the gravitational instantons we obtain below must be of Type
\I
or Type
\II
(for their definitions, see Section
7.1
), thus belong to the known class of examples, but the arguments below illustrates the key idea in proving Theorem
1.1
. It also explains the fact that the Taub-NUT metric serves as building blocks for obtaining the other Type
\II
gravitational instantons, including the Schwarzschild, Kerr, Taub-Bolt spaces, albeit in a non-explicit way.

6.1.1.
The
\AF
case

Let
(\mathfrak{R}_{l},\Lambda)
be the smooth enhanced rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
with exactly two turning points
z_{0}=0
and
z_{1}=l>0
and with the enhancement
\Lambda
generated by

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{2}=[\bv_{0}],\quad\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(0,1)^{\top}].

It is easy to see that
d(\mathfrak{R}_{l})=1
.
Fix
\beta\in(-1,1)
. By Theorem
4.24
and Proposition
4.26
, we have a unique augmented harmonic map
(\Phi_{l},\nu_{l})
which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
, such that
\nu_{l}
is normalized. By definition, we have

\vartheta_{\Phi_{l},\nu_{l}}(\mathfrak{I}_{0})=\vartheta_{\Phi_{l},\nu_{l}}(\mathfrak{I}_{2})=2\pi.

Denote

\Theta(l)=\vartheta_{\Phi_{l},\nu_{l}}(\mathfrak{I}_{1}).

By (
5.29
),
\Theta
is a continuous function of
l\in(0,\infty)
.

Proposition 6.1
.

We have

\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\Theta(l)=\infty,

and

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\Theta(l)=2\pi|\beta|.

Proof.

The first claim follows from Proposition
5.13
and the second claim follows from Proposition
5.15
.
∎

It follows that for each
\beta\in(-1,1)
there is an
l\in(0,\infty)
such that
\Theta(l)=2\pi
. Then the corresponding
(\Phi_{l},\nu_{l})
defines a smooth AF gravitational instanton. These are exactly the Kerr spaces by Corollary
7.5
.

Using the fact that Kerr spaces are of Type
\II
and the result of Biquard-Gauduchon
[
BG23
]
, we have an explicit formula for
\Theta(l)
. In particular, we know that for each
\beta\in(-1,1)
, there is a unique
l=l(\beta)>0
such that
\Theta(l)=2\pi
. Moreover, when
\beta=0
we get the Schwarzschild space and when
\beta\rightarrow 1
we have
l(\beta)\rightarrow\infty
. As discussed in Example
3.20
, as
\beta\rightarrow 1
, in the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff sense the Kerr space splits into the union of a Taub-NUT space and an anti-Taub-NUT space. The case of
\beta\to-1
is similar.

6.1.2.
The
\ALFm
case

Denote by
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-},l}
the smooth enhanced rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
, with exactly two turning points
z_{1}=0
and
z_{2}=l>0
and with the enhancement
\Lambda
generated by primitive rod vectors

\bbv_{0}=\bv_{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\bbv_{1}=(2,0)^{\top},\quad\bbv_{2}=\bv_{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Denote by
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l}
the smooth enhanced rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
, with exactly two turning points
z_{1}=0
and
z_{2}=l>0
, with the enhancement
\Lambda
generated by primitive rod vectors

\bbv_{0}=\bv_{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\bbv_{1}=(0,1)^{\top},\quad\bbv_{2}=\bv_{-\frac{1}{2}}.

See Figure
11
for an illustration of the rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-},l}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l}
. It is easy to see that
d(\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-},l})=2
and
d(\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l})=1
.

\mathfrak{v}_{2}=[(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(2,0)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-},l}
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l}
l
l
\mathfrak{v}_{2}=[(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(0,1)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top}]

Figure 11.
The rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-},l}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l}
.

Denote by
(\Phi_{\dTN^{-},l},\nu_{\dTN^{-}})
and
(\Phi_{\TB^{-},l},\nu_{\TB^{-}})
the corresponding augmented harmonic maps tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-},l}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l}
respectively. Then in each case the cone angles along the rods
\mathfrak{v}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{2}
are fixed to be
2\pi
. We denote

\Theta_{\TB^{-}}(l)=\vartheta_{\Phi_{\TB^{-},l},\nu_{\TB^{-},l}}(\mathfrak{I}_{1})

and

\Theta_{\dTN^{-}}(l)=\vartheta_{\Phi_{\dTN^{-},l},\nu_{\dTN^{-},l}}(\mathfrak{I}_{1}).

By (
5.29
),
\Theta_{\TB^{-}}
and
\Theta_{\dTN^{-}}
are continuous functions of
l\in(0,\infty)
.

Proposition 6.2
.

We have

\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\Theta_{\TB^{-}}(l)=\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\Theta_{\dTN^{-}}(l)=4\pi.

Proof.

As
l\rightarrow 0
, both family of rod structures converges to the same limit rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}=\mathfrak{R}^{\TNm}
. By Proposition
5.10
in both cases we know there is a unique bubble limit
\Phi_{\infty,1}
which is tamed by a rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. By Proposition
4.38
and (
5.38
) we get that

\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\Theta_{\TB^{-}}(l)=\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\Theta_{\dTN^{-}}(l)=4\pi.

∎

Proposition 6.3
.

We have

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\Theta_{\TB^{-}}(l)=0,

and

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\Theta_{\dTN^{-}}(l)=4\pi.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.17
.
∎

As a consequence there must exist an
l>0
such that
(\Phi_{\TB^{-},l},\nu_{\TB^{-},l})
defines a gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
. Indeed, it is of Type
\II
and by Proposition
7.5
, we know there is exactly one
l
such that
\Theta_{\TB^{-}}(l)=2\pi
, which isometrically is the
Taub-Bolt metric
. Indeed, the Taub-Bolt metric (up to scaling) can be explicitly written as

(6.1)

f(r)(d\phi_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cos\theta d\phi_{1})^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{f(r)}+(r^{2}-\frac{1}{16})(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\phi_{1}^{2})

with

f(r)=\frac{r^{2}+\frac{1}{16}-\frac{5}{8}r}{r^{2}-\frac{1}{16}},\quad r\geq\frac{1}{2}\text{ and }\theta\in[0,\pi].

The enhancement is generated by
(1,\frac{1}{2})^{\top},(1,-\frac{1}{2})^{\top}
, with

\rho=\sqrt{r^{2}-\frac{5}{8}r+\frac{1}{16}}\sin\theta,\quad z=(r-\frac{5}{16})\cos\theta+\frac{3}{16}.

One can check that the rod structure is exactly given by the
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{-},l}
in Figure
11
with here
l=\frac{3}{8}
.
We shall refer to the Taub-Bolt metric with the orientation
d\rho\wedge dz\wedge d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2}
as the
anti-Taub-Bolt space
(so with the opposite orientation there is the
Taub-Bolt space
). Note that the natural
S^{2}
has self-intersection
-1
.

Note that similar discussion in the
\dTN^{-}
case is not sufficient to yield a gravitational instanton. This is not surprising, since if we consider the opposite rod structure which we denote by
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{+}_{l}}
, then one can see that
d(\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{+}_{l}})=0
and the associated harmonic map must be of Type
\I
in the sense of Definition
7.1
by Theorem
7.4
.
As discussed in Remark
7.8
, in the Type
\I
case the cone angle functions do not depend on the lengths of the rods. In particular, going back to
\mathfrak{R}_{\dTN^{-}}
, this implies that for all
l>0
,
\Theta_{\dTN^{-}}=4\pi
. One can take an obvious
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quotient to obtain a gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{-}
. The latter is exactly a negatively oriented double Taub-NUT space (by Proposition
7.5
).

6.1.3.
The
\ALFp
case

We can also recover the
Taub-Bolt space
. Recall the metric is explicitly given by (
6.1
). This time we consider the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{+},l}
with turning points
z_{1}=0,z_{2}=l>0
and with the enhancement
\Lambda
generated by primitive rod vectors

\bbv_{0}=(\frac{1}{2},1)^{\top},\quad\bbv_{1}=(1,0)^{\top},\quad\bbv_{2}=(\frac{1}{2},-1)^{\top}.

The Asymptotic Type is taken to be
\ALFp
. See Figure
12
.

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(\frac{1}{2},1)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{2}=[(\frac{1}{2},-1)^{\top}]
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
l

Figure 12.
The rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{+},l}
.

As in Section
6.1.2
, one can argue that the angle along the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
satisfies that
\Theta(l)\to 0
as
l\to\infty
and
\Theta(l)\to 4\pi
as
l\to 0
, hence there is an
l
such that the harmonic map strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{+},l}
with normalized augmentation and the enhancement
\Lambda
gives rise to a toric gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
. Notice that
d(\mathfrak{R}_{\TB^{+},l})=1
so by Theorem
7.4
the gravitational instanton is of Type
\II
in the sense of Definition
7.1
. Again by Proposition
7.5
this is isometric to the Taub-Bolt metric, explicitly given by (
6.1
). We shall refer to the Taub-Bolt metric with this orientation as the
Taub-Bolt space
.

6.2.
The general case

Fix
\beta\in(0,1)
. Given an integer
n\geq 1
, and
\bm{l}=(l_{1},\cdots,l_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, we consider the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\bm{l}}=(\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n+1},\{\mathfrak{v}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n+1},\AFa)
, where

•

z_{1}=0
,
z_{j+1}-z_{j}=l_{j}
for
j=1,\cdots,n
;

•

\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{n+1}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
;

•

for
j\neq 0,n+1
,
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(0,1)^{\top}]
when
j
is odd, and
\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(-1,1)^{\top}]
when
j
is even.

See Figure
13
for an illustration of
\mathfrak{R}_{\bm{l}}
with
5
turning points.

\mathfrak{v}_{0}
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
\mathfrak{v}_{2}
\mathfrak{v}_{3}
\mathfrak{v}_{4}
\mathfrak{v}_{5}

Figure 13.
A rod structure with 5 turning points.

One can compute that

(6.2)

d(\mathfrak{R}_{\bm{l}})=\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\rfloor.

We will always take the smooth enhancement
\Lambda
to be the standard lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. One can check that it gives rise to the toric 4-manifold diffeomorphic to
X_{n}
(see (
1.2
)). We let
(\Phi_{\bm{l},\beta},\nu_{\bm{l},\beta})
be the augmented harmonic map which is strongly tamed by the enhanced rod structure
(\mathfrak{R}_{\bm{l}},\Lambda)
and such that
\nu_{\bm{l},\beta}
is normalized.

Theorem
1.1
is a consequence of the following

Theorem 6.4
.

There exists an
\bm{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
such that
(\mathfrak{R}_{\bm{l}},\Lambda,\Phi_{\bm{l},\beta},\nu_{\bm{l},\beta})
defines a toric
\AF_{\beta}
gravitational instanton.

Remark 6.5
.

From our proof it is not clear whether
\bm{l}
depends uniquely or continuously on
\beta
. See Section
8
for more discussion.

Remark 6.6
.

When
n=1
or
n=2
, we have
d(\mathfrak{R}_{l})=1
, and the gravitational instantons constructed above coincide with the Kerr spaces and the Chen-Teo spaces; when
n\geq 3
we have
d(\mathfrak{R}_{l})>1
, and the gravitational instantons constructed above is not locally Hermitian. These follow from the general result in Theorem
7.9
, using (
6.2
) and the classification of Hermitian toric AF gravitational instantons by Biquard-Gauduchon
[
BG23
]
.

Denote by
\vartheta_{j}(\bm{l})=\vartheta_{\Phi_{\bm{l},\beta},\nu_{\bm{l},\beta}}(\mathfrak{I}_{j})
the cone angle along the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
. Since
\nu_{\bm{l},\beta}
is normalized we know
\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{n+1}=2\pi
. We define a map

(6.3)

\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+};(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\mapsto(\vartheta_{1}(\bm{l}),\ldots,\vartheta_{n}(\bm{l})).

It suffices to show that the point
{\bm{\vartheta}}_{0}:=(2\pi,\ldots,2\pi)
is contained in the image of
\mathcal{A}
. We introduce an auxiliary map

\mathcal{A}_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+};(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\mapsto(e^{-l_{1}}(2\pi+1),\ldots,e^{-l_{n}}(2\pi+1)).

Define a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
by

\mathcal{S}:=\{\bm{l}=(l_{1},\cdots,l_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{n}^{+}\mid t\mathcal{A}(\bm{l})+(1-t)\mathcal{A}_{0}(\bm{l})\neq\bm{\vartheta}_{0},\ \ \forall\ \ t\in[0,1]\}.

For
N>\epsilon>0
, we denote the cube

\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}=[\epsilon,N]^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}.

Proposition 6.7
.

There exist
\epsilon
and
N
such that

\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}\subset\mathcal{S}.

Proof of Theorem
6.4
.

We prove by contradiction. Suppose
\bm{\vartheta}_{0}
is not in the image of
\mathcal{A}
. Denote by
\pi
the radial projection map from
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\setminus\{\bm{\vartheta}_{0}\}
to the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Let
\epsilon
and
N
be given in Proposition
6.7
. Then the map

\pi\circ\mathcal{A}|_{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}}:{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{n-1}

is homotopic to
\pi\circ\mathcal{A}_{0}|_{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}}
. It is clear that
\pi\circ\mathcal{A}_{0}|_{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}}
has a nonzero mod-2 degree but
\pi\circ\mathcal{A}|_{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}}
is contractible. Contradiction.
∎

Now we prove Proposition
6.7
.

Lemma 6.8
.

There exists an
N_{0}>0
, such that

\mathcal{C}_{N_{0}}:=\{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\mid l_{j}>N_{0}\ \text{for some $j$}\}\subset\mathcal{S}.

Proof.

Otherwise, there is a sequence
\bm{l}_{k}=(l_{k,1},\cdots,l_{k,n})\notin\mathcal{S}
and
j_{0}\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
with

l_{k,j_{0}}=\max_{j=1,\ldots,n}l_{k,j}\to\infty

as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Since

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}e^{-l_{k,j_{0}}}(2\pi+1)=0,

it follows that

\vartheta_{k,j_{0}}(\bm{l}_{k})\geq 2\pi
for
k
large. However, according to Proposition
5.15
, we must have

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{j_{0}}(\bm{l}_{k})\leq 2\pi\max(\beta,1-\beta)<2\pi,

which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

There exits an
\epsilon_{0}>0
small such that

\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon_{0},N_{0}}:=\{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\mid\text{$l_{j}<2N_{0}$ for all $j$, and $l_{j}<\epsilon_{0}$ for some $j$}\}\subset\mathcal{S}.

Proof.

Otherwise, there exists a sequence
\bm{l}_{k}\notin\mathcal{S}
and
j_{0}
with
\sup_{j}l_{k,j}<2N_{0}
and
l_{k,j_{0}}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Passing to a suitable subsequence we may obtain a bubble tree convergence of the rod structures. We pick a first bubble limit
(\Phi_{\infty,p},\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime})
as shown in Section
5.3
. There are two cases.

Case (1)
:
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. If
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has more than one rod, then we must see a finite rod
\mathfrak{I}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
whose rod vector is different from the infinite rod vector. Suppose
\mathfrak{I}
contains the rescaled limit of the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{\flat}}
.
By Proposition
5.13
we know the cone angle
\vartheta_{j_{\flat}}(\bm{l}_{k})\rightarrow\infty
. Since
l_{k,j_{\flat}}\to 0
, for
k
large we have

e^{-l_{k,j_{\flat}}}(2\pi+1)>2\pi.

So
\bm{l}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}
. This is a contradiction.

If
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has only one rod, i.e.,
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is trivial, then we can pass to smaller scales and go to a first non-trivial bubble limit, which must be of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. It follows from Proposition
5.14
that there is a
j_{\flat}
such that
\vartheta_{j_{\flat}}(\bm{l}_{k})\rightarrow\infty
, so as before we obtain a contradiction.

Case (2)
:
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. If
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has no finite rods, i.e.,
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
is trivial, then we can pass to smaller scales and go to a first non-trivial bubble limit that is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. Then as in
Case (1)
we obtain a contradiction.

Now we assume
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
has a finite rod. Suppose the
j_{a}
-th rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{a}}
and the
j_{b}
-th rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{b}}
converge to the infinite rods of
\mathfrak{R}
, where
j_{a}<j_{b}
. Then
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{a}}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{b}}
do not vanish in the original scale. So passing to the
\Ae
bubble limit and applying the angle comparison (Proposition
5.12
) at
z_{j_{a}+1}
, it follows that we have

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{j_{a}+1}(\bm{l}_{k})\geq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\vartheta_{j_{b}}(\bm{l}_{k}).

However, since both
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{a}}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{b}}
survive in the original scale, we have

2N_{0}>l_{k,j_{a}},l_{k,j_{b}}>\delta

for a small constant
\delta
. As
l_{k,j_{a}+1}\to 0
, for
k
large we have

e^{-l_{k,j_{a}+1}}(2\pi+1)>e^{-l_{k,j_{b}}}(2\pi+1),

Hence for
t\in[0,1]
and
k
large enough, we have

(1-t)\vartheta_{j_{a}+1}(\bm{l}_{k})+te^{-l_{k,j_{a}+1}}(2\pi+1)>(1-t)\vartheta_{j_{b}}(\bm{l}_{k})+te^{-l_{k,j_{b}}}(2\pi+1),

These imply that
\bm{l}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}
. Contradiction.
∎

Now choose
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
and
N\in(N_{0},2N_{0})
, then we have

\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon,N}\subset\mathcal{C}_{N_{0}}\cup\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon_{0},N_{0}}\subset\mathcal{S}.

This completes the proof of Proposition
6.7
.

7.
Rod structures of low degrees

7.1.
Ricci-flat metrics with special geometry

As in
[
Li23b
,
Li23a
]
, for convenience of discussion we divide 4-dimensional oriented Ricci-flat metrics into three classes.

(1)

Type
\I
:
W^{+}\equiv 0
. This is equivalent to saying that
g
is locally Kähler (hence locally hyperkähler).

(2)

Type
\II
:
W^{+}
has exactly one eigenvalue of multiplicity one everywhere. This is equivalent to saying that
g
is locally conformally Kähler but non-Kähler.

(3)

Type
\III
:
W^{+}
has three distinct eigenvalues generically.

In the first two cases the Ricci-flat metrics have special geometry. In particular, they are closely related to complex geometry. The latter fact leads to many deep results for Type
\I
and
\II
gravitational instantons.

Given a harmonic map
\Phi
in our setting, we can similarly define its Type. For this purpose, we need to pick an augmentation
\nu
and consider the Ricci-flat metric

g=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+\rho\Phi,

endowed with the canonical orientation given by
d\rho\wedge dz\wedge d\phi_{1}\wedge d\phi_{2}
.

Definition 7.1
.

We say a harmonic map
\Phi
is Type
\I/\II/\III
if the metric
g
is Type
\I/\II/\III
with respect to the canonical orientation.

It is easy to see that the Type is independent of the choice of the augmentation
\nu
.
Notice that for
P\in SL_{+}(2;\mathbb{R})
, we know
P.\Phi
is of the same Type as
\Phi
. But if
P\in SL_{-}(2;\mathbb{R})
, then in general
P.\Phi
may not be of the same Type as
\Phi
. Given a sequence of harmonic maps
\Phi^{k}
that converge to a limit harmonic map
\Phi^{\infty}
, it is easy to see that if
\Phi^{k}
are all Type
\I
then
\Phi^{\infty}
is also Type
\I
, and if
\Phi^{k}
are all Type
\II
then
\Phi^{\infty}
can only be Type
\II
or Type
\I
.

In the case of Type
\I
and Type
\II
, for integrable toric Ricci-flat metrics, the axisymmetric harmonic maps further simplify to axisymmetric harmonic functions and one can carry out explicit studies. We will explain this below.

7.1.1.
Type
\I
case

In this case, we will explain that the axisymmetric harmonic map reduces to an axisymmetric harmonic function, using the
Gibbons-Hawking ansatz
.

Consider the local situation where we have a hyperkähler metric
g
. Denote by
\mathbb{S}
the 2-sphere of compatible parallel orthogonal complex structures. Notice that
\mathbb{S}
is endowed with a natural round metric (it naturally sits inside
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as the unit sphere). An isometry
\psi
of
g
induces an action on
\mathbb{S}
via
\psi.I=\psi^{*}I
. One can see the latter is an isometric action. In this way a Killing field
X
for
g
generates a Killing vector field
V_{X}
on
\mathbb{S}
. So, if nonzero, then
V_{X}
is an infinitesimal rotation. We may assume it vanishes at one complex structure (say
I
) and rotates the plane generated by
J
and
K
.

Next we assume further that
g
is toric and integrable. Then we have two commuting Killing fields
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, so
V_{X_{1}}
and
V_{X_{2}}
also commute, thus they must be proportional. Up to a linear combination we may assume that
V_{X_{1}}=0
. This implies that
X_{1}
is tri-holomorphic, i.e., its flow preserves all the hyperkähler complex structures. Hence
g
is locally given by the Gibbons-Hawking construction applied to a positive harmonic function
V
on some domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. It is easy to see that, up to an additive constant,
V^{-1}
is exactly the Ernst potential
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. By the integrability assumption we have
\Theta_{1}=\Theta_{2}=0
, it follows that
V
is invariant under
X_{2}
. As we are interested in gravitational instantons where the toric action has a fixed point, the Killing field
X_{2}
globally has zero, and it follows that
X_{2}
must be a rotation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz. Hence
V
is locally an axisymmetric harmonic function.

To connect to axisymmetric harmonic maps, we write the flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as
d\rho^{2}+dz^{2}+\rho^{2}d\phi_{2}^{2}
. For an axisymmetric harmonic function
V
such that
\frac{1}{2\pi}[*dV]
defines an integral cohomology class, we can write down the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz as

(7.1)

g=V(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2}+\rho^{2}d\phi_{2}^{2})+V^{-1}(d\phi_{1}+Fd\phi_{2})^{2}

with

dF=\rho V_{\rho}dz-\rho V_{z}d\rho.

The latter admits a solution locally since
V
is axisymmetric harmonic. Here (
7.1
) is already in the Weyl-Papapetrou coordinates. The corresponding augmented harmonic map is explicitly given by

(7.2)

\Phi=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}V^{-1}&V^{-1}F\\
V^{-1}F&\rho^{2}V+V^{-1}F^{2}\end{matrix}\right),

(7.3)

\nu=\frac{1}{2}\log V.

Particularly, when we take
V=1+\frac{1}{2r}
and
F=\frac{z}{2r}
we get the Taub-NUT space, discussed in Example
3.16
.

More generally, given
k+1
distinct points
p_{1},\cdots,p_{k+1}\in\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, if we take

V=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k+1}\frac{1}{2|x-p_{\alpha}|},

then we obtain a
multi-Eguchi-Hanson space
, which is an
\ALE
-
A_{k}
hyperkähler gravitational instanton. If we instead take for
T>0

V=T+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k+1}\frac{1}{2|x-p_{\alpha}|},

then we obtain a
multi-Taub-NUT space
, which is (up to scaling according to our Definition
4.30
) an
\ALF
-
A_{k}
hyperkähler gravitational instanton. In both cases, the underlying 4-manifold is diffeomorphic to the minimal resolution of
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{k}
.

7.1.2.
Type
\II
case

In this case we again reduce the axisymmetric harmonic map to an axisymmetric harmonic function, using instead the
LeBrun-Tod ansatz
.

Given a Type
\II
Ricci-flat metric
g
, we define a conformal metric

g_{K}=(2\sqrt{6}|W^{+}|_{g})^{2/3}g.

Then by
[
Der83
]

g_{K}
is a locally Kähler metric, which is moreover
extremal
in the sense of Calabi. That is, the scalar curvature function
|s_{g_{K}}|=(2\sqrt{6}|W^{+}|_{g})^{1/3}
is not a constant, and

X_{1}:=J\nabla_{g_{K}}s_{g_{K}}

is a Hamiltonian Killing field. The LeBrun-Tod ansatz for Kähler metrics with a Hamiltonian Killing field now gives that locally,

(7.4)

g=V(d\xi^{2}+e^{u}(dx_{2}^{2}+dx_{3}^{2}))+V^{-1}\eta^{2},

where
(u,V,\eta)
satisfy the following system of equations

(7.5)

e^{u}_{\xi\xi}+u_{x_{2}x_{2}}+u_{x_{3}x_{3}}=0,

(7.6)

V=-12\xi+6\xi^{2}u_{\xi},

(7.7)

d\eta=-\xi^{2}(\xi^{-2}e^{u}V)_{\xi}dx_{2}dx_{3}-V_{x_{2}}dx_{3}d\xi-V_{x_{3}}d\xi dx_{2}.

Here
x_{2}+ix_{3}
is a local holomorphic coordinate over the Kähler quotient and
\xi
is the moment map for
X_{1}
. See Equations (28)-(31) in
[
BG23
]
. To be consistent with
[
BG23
]
, one can instead work with the scaled Killing field
\sqrt{6k}X_{1}
, where the only difference for (
7.4
)-(
7.7
) is (
7.6
) becomes

(7.8)

V=\frac{1}{k}(-2\xi+6\xi^{2}u_{\xi}).

Later we will see that
k
has the significance of ensuring
V
tends to
1
at infinity in the global setting.

When we have an additional Killing field
X_{2}
, it preserves
|W^{+}|_{g}
hence the conformal factor
s_{g_{K}}
, so
X_{2}
is also a Killing field for
g_{K}
. The complex structure
J
is determined by the non-repeated eigen-two-form of
W^{+}
and
g_{K}
, hence is also preserved by
X_{2}
, which implies that
X_{2}
is holomorphic. Since
X_{1}=J\nabla_{g_{K}}s_{g_{K}}
,
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
commute automatically. Therefore, by rotating the complex coordinate
x_{2}+ix_{3}
by some
e^{i\theta}
one can arrange that

X_{1}=\partial_{t},X_{2}=\partial_{x_{3}},\eta=dt-Fdx_{3}

for functions
u,V,F
independent of
x_{3}
. The Equation (
7.5
) becomes

(7.9)

e^{u}_{\xi\xi}+u_{x_{2}x_{2}}=0.

Performing Ward’s Bäcklund transformation
[
War90
]
one can show that the reduced equation is equivalent to the axisymmetric Laplace’s equation. More precisely, given an axisymmetric harmonic function
U
, we define

\xi=\frac{1}{2}\rho U_{\rho}

and

x_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}U_{z}.

Restricting to the domain where
\frac{\rho}{4}(U_{zz}^{2}+U_{\rho z}^{2})>0
,
(\xi,x_{2})
gives rise to local coordinates and
u=\log\rho^{2}
is a solution to (
7.9
). Conversely, any solution to (
7.9
) locally arises in this way. See Proposition 3.5 in
[
BG23
]
for details.

Combining the previous Ward ansatz, using the second Killing field
X_{2}
, we can further reduce (
7.4
), (
7.5
), (
7.8
), and (
7.7
) to the following

(7.10)

g=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+V\rho^{2}dx_{3}^{2}+V^{-1}(dt-Fdx_{3})^{2},

(7.11)

e^{2\nu}=\frac{1}{4}V\rho^{2}(U_{\rho z}^{2}+U_{zz}^{2}),

(7.12)

V=-\frac{1}{k}\left(\rho U_{\rho}+\frac{U_{\rho}^{2}U_{zz}}{U_{\rho z}^{2}+U_{zz}^{2}}\right),

(7.13)

F=-\frac{1}{k}\left(-\frac{\rho U_{\rho}^{2}U_{\rho z}}{U_{\rho z}^{2}+U_{zz}^{2}}+\rho^{2}U_{z}+2H\right).

Here the function
H
is the harmonic conjugate of
U
and the formulae make sense over the domain where
V>0
and
U_{\rho z}^{2}+U_{zz}^{2}>0
. This is Corollary 3.6 in
[
BG23
]
. The metric
g
in (
7.10
) is already in the Weyl-Papapetrou coordinates and one can easily write down the corresponding harmonic map. With
\phi_{1}=x_{3}
and
\phi_{2}=-t
, the harmonic map is explicitly given by

(7.14)

\Phi=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}V\rho^{2}+V^{-1}F^{2}&V^{-1}F\\
V^{-1}F&V^{-1}\end{matrix}\right).

Taking
U=\frac{1}{2}\log\rho^{2}+2r-z\log\frac{r+z}{r-z}
with
k=1
, one gets
V=1+\frac{1}{2r}
and
F=\frac{z}{2r}
, and we obtain the anti-Taub-NUT space, discussed in Example
3.17
. As explained in
[
BG23
]
, such construction can give rise to the Kerr, Taub-Bolt, anti-Taub-Bolt, and Chen-Teo spaces.

In the next subsections we will deal with the global situation.

Definition 7.2
.

We define the Type of a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
to be the Type of the unique harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
.

Remark 7.3
.

This notion of
Type
should not be confused with the notion of
Asymptotic Type
introduced in Definition
3.1
.

Clearly from the definition the Type of
\mathfrak{R}

is only determined after solving the global harmonic map equation, which is in general difficult to determine. Nevertheless, we will prove the following theorem in the next two subsections. It gives a complete characterization of the Type in terms of the degree (see Definition
3.11
).

Theorem 7.4
.

Given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
, we have

•

\mathfrak{R}
is Type
\I
if and only if it has degree
d(\mathfrak{R})=0
.

•

\mathfrak{R}
is Type
\II
if and only if it has degree
d(\mathfrak{R})=1
.

•

\mathfrak{R}
is Type
\III
if and only if it has degree
d(\mathfrak{R})\geq 2
.

Together with the discussion at the end of Section
3.1
we see that when the number of turning of points is at most 2, either
\mathfrak{R}
or
\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
is of Type
\I
or Type
\II
, i.e., the associated harmonic map has special geometry.

Since Type
\I
and Type
\II
harmonic maps are explicitly understood using axisymmetric harmonic functions by the discussion above, Theorem
7.4
gives an interesting PDE result, i.e., an explicit classification of axisymmetric harmonic maps when the rod structure has degree at most
1
, in terms of axisymmetric harmonic functions.

Together with the classification of hyperkähler and Hermitian gravitational instantons, we have

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
(M,g)
be a simply-connected toric gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\ALF/\AFa
, such that the associated rod structure has degree at most
1
. Then it is isometric to one in the following list

(1)

(Hyperkähler case):

•

A multi-Taub-NUT space diffeomorphic to the crepant resolution of
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{k}
.

(2)

(Hermitian, non-Kähler case):

•

An anti-Taub-NUT space diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{4}
;

•

A Kerr space diffeomorphic to
S^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
(including the Schwarzschild space);

•

A Taub-Bolt space diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{4}\sharp\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
;

•

An anti-Taub-Bolt space diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{4}\sharp\overline{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}}

•

A Chen-Teo space diffeomorphic to
S^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\sharp\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

In particular,

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose
(M,g)
is a simply-connected toric gravitational instanton of Type
\ALE/\ALF/\AFa
and let
\mathfrak{R}
be the associated rod structure. If
\mathfrak{R}
has more than 2 turning points, then
d(\mathfrak{R})>1
.

7.2.
Degree 0 = Type
\I

In this subsection we prove the first part of Theorem
7.4
.

Theorem 7.7
.

A rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
is Type
\I
if and only if
d(\mathfrak{R})=0
. In this case
\mathfrak{R}
must have Asymptotic Type
\Ae
or
\ALFp
, unless
\mathfrak{R}
has no turning points and is of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
for some
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi
be a harmonic map which is strongly tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
.
If
\mathfrak{R}
has no finite rods, then by definition it has to be of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
for some
\beta\geq 0
. By the uniqueness result in Theorem
4.24
we know
\Phi
must be equal to
\Phi^{\AFa}_{\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}}
for some
\beta
. The associated toric Ricci flat metric is flat, so by definition it is of Type
\I
. It is also obvious that
d(\mathfrak{R})=0
. In the following we assume that there is at least 1 finite rod.

(\Rightarrow)
: Suppose
\Phi
is Type
\I
. Recall that from our discussion in Section
7.1
we know
\Phi
is locally given by (
7.1
) for an axisymmetric harmonic function
V
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
.
Due to the strong tameness we know that
V
needs to stay bounded in a neighborhood of a point in the interior of a rod and blow up at the rate
r^{-1}
near each turning point, and
V
is uniformly bounded at infinity. It is then easy to see that
V
must be of the form

V=T+\sum_{j}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{(\rho^{2}+(z-z_{j})^{2})^{1/2}}

for some
T\geq 0
, where
\lambda_{j}>0
and
z_{1}<\ldots<z_{n}
are the turning points of the rod structure. Since there is at least 1 finite rod,
V
can not be a constant. If
T=0
, then
\mathfrak{R}
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
; if
T>0
, then we must take
T=1
(since it has decaying distance to
\Phi^{\TNap}
by definition) and
\mathfrak{R}
is of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
. It is easy to see that in both cases
d(\mathfrak{R})=0
.

(\Leftarrow)
: Suppose
d(\mathfrak{R})=0
. By definition,
\mathfrak{R}
has to be of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
or
\ALFp
(since in all the other cases we have
d(\mathfrak{R})\geq 1
).

We focus on the case
\sharp=\Ae
; the other case can be argued similarly. Since
d(\mathfrak{R})=0
, by applying a suitable element in
SO(2;\mathbb{R})
, one can always assume the rod vectors are of the form

(7.15)

\mathfrak{v}_{j}=[(-\sum_{l\leq j}\lambda_{j}+\sum_{l>j}\lambda_{j},1)^{\top}]

for some
\lambda_{j}>0
.
Then there is a harmonic map
\Phi^{\prime}
strongly tamed by this rod structure, which is explicitly given by the
(\Rightarrow)
part of the proof, which is Type
\I
. From the uniqueness result in Theorem
4.24
, it follows that
\Phi=\Phi^{\prime}
, hence
\Phi
is Type
\I
.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

It is easy to see, using the explicit formulae (
7.2
), (
7.3
), that the rod vectors
\mathfrak{v}_{j}
in (
7.15
) are indeed normalized. Notice that they do not depend on the rod lengths. In particular, if we are given an enhancement and an augmentation, then the cone angle along each rod can be seen explicitly, and it does not depend on the rod length. This gives a complication when one applies the strategy of tuning the cone angles by varying the rod lengths – the proof of Theorem
1.2
would not work if one instead tries to construct Type I gravitational instantons (which we already know do not exist with the
\AFa
Asymptotic Types anyway).

7.3.
Degree 1 = Type
\II

The main result of this subsection is

Theorem 7.9
.

A rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
is Type
\II
if and only if
d(\mathfrak{R})=1
.

In the following we will first prove Theorem
7.9
for the
\ALF^{\pm}/\AFa
case and then deal with the
\Ae
case in Section
7.3.2
. Harmonic maps strongly tamed by a rod structure of Asymptotic Type
\ALF^{\pm}/\AFa
have already been classified by Biquard-Gauduchon
[
BG23
]
, which we now recall.

For each positive convex piece-wise affine function

(7.16)

f(z)=A+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}|z-z_{j}|

with
A>0
,
z_{1}<\cdots<z_{n}
and
-1=a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}=1
, one can assign a canonical axisymmetric harmonic function

(7.17)

U(\rho,z)=A\log\rho^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}U_{0}(\rho,z-z_{j}),

where

U_{0}(\rho,z)=2r-z\log\frac{r+z}{r-z}.

We write
f_{j}=f(z_{j})
and denote by
f_{j}^{\prime}
the slope of
f
over
(z_{j},z_{j+1})
. In particular, we have

a_{j}=\frac{1}{2}(f_{j}^{\prime}-f_{j-1}^{\prime}).

\bullet
f_{1}
\bullet
f_{2}
\bullet
f_{3}
\bullet
f_{4}
f_{0}^{\prime}=-1
f_{1}^{\prime}
f_{2}^{\prime}
f_{3}^{\prime}
f_{4}^{\prime}=1

Figure 14.
A piece-wise affine function
f
with four turning points.

Then with the ansatz in (
7.10
)-(
7.13
) and choosing
k=2A
(this ensures
V\to 1
at infinity), we get an augmented harmonic map
(\Phi,\nu)
which is strongly tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
with
n
turning points. The rods are given by
\mathfrak{I}_{j}=(z_{j},z_{j+1})
and the normalized rod vector along
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
is

(7.18)

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}=\begin{cases}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}=f_{j}^{\prime}(\partial_{x_{3}}+F_{j}\partial_{t}),&\text{if}\ \ \ \ f_{j}^{\prime}\neq 0;\\
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{j}=\frac{1}{A}f_{j}^{2}\partial_{t},&\text{if}\ \ \ \ f_{j}^{\prime}=0.\end{cases}

Here the constants
F_{j}
are determined by the following conditions

•

We have

F_{0}=-\frac{1}{A}(A+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}z_{j})^{2}+\frac{1}{A}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}z_{j}^{2};

•

if
f_{j}^{\prime}\neq 0
then

(7.19)

F_{j}-F_{j-1}=\frac{1}{A}f_{j}^{2}(\frac{1}{f_{j}^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{f_{j-1}^{\prime}});

•

if
f_{j}^{\prime}=0
then

(7.20)

F_{j+1}-F_{j-1}=\frac{1}{A}(f_{j}^{2}(\frac{1}{f_{j+1}^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{f_{j-1}^{\prime}})-2(z_{j+1}-z_{j})f_{j}).

Notice that these imply

F_{n}=\frac{1}{A}(A-\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}z_{j})^{2}-\frac{1}{A}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}z_{j}^{2}.

For the convenience of discussion, in the ansatz (
7.10
)-(
7.13
), we take
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
such that

\partial_{\phi_{1}}=\partial_{x_{3}}+\frac{F_{0}+F_{n}}{2}\partial_{t},\quad\partial_{\phi_{2}}=-\partial_{t}.

Then the harmonic map
\Phi
is given by

(7.21)

\Phi=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}V\rho^{2}+V^{-1}(F-\frac{F_{0}+F_{n}}{2})^{2}&V^{-1}(F-\frac{F_{0}+F_{n}}{2})\\
V^{-1}(F-\frac{F_{0}+F_{n}}{2})&V^{-1}\end{matrix}\right).

The Asymptotic Type of
\mathfrak{R}
is
\AF_{0}
when
F_{n}-F_{0}=0
, is
\ALFm
if
F_{n}-F_{0}>0
and is
\ALFp
when
F_{n}-F_{0}<0
.

By construction we know that the harmonic map given by (
7.21
) is of Type
\II
. Conversely, for any Type
\II
harmonic map strongly tamed by an
\ALF^{\pm}/\AFa
rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
, by
[
BG23
]
, up to conjugation by a suitable element in
SL(2;\mathbb{R})
(in particular by
U_{\beta}
we can make the Asymptotic Type change from
\AFa
to
\AF_{0}
), it always takes the form (
7.21
).

Remark 7.10
.

In this setting, the normalized rod vectors are explicitly given by (
7.18
), so as in the Type
\I
case if we are given an enhancement and an augmentation, then the cone angle along each rod can be seen explicitly. But unlike the Type
\I
case they do depend on the rod lengths. This is the philosophy which makes it possible to apply the strategy of tuning the cone angles by varying rod lengths in our proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 7.11
.

The above rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
has degree
1
.

Proof.

First we observe that

•

if
f_{j}^{\prime}<0
, then
F_{j}-F_{j-1}<0
;

•

if
f_{j}^{\prime}>0
, then
F_{j+1}-F_{j}<0
.

If
f_{j}^{\prime}=0
, we can formally view
F_{j}
as
-\infty
.

We only consider the
\ALFm
case, as the other cases are similar. Thus, we assume
F_{n}-F_{0}>0
.
We begin with the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and choose

\partial_{\phi_{1}}+\frac{F_{n}-F_{0}}{2}\partial_{\phi_{2}}=\partial_{x_{3}}+F_{0}\partial_{t}

as the representative vector
v_{0}
.

From the above observation, if we assign the following representative vectors
v_{j}
for
\mathfrak{I}_{j}
,

(7.22)

v_{j}=\begin{cases}\partial_{\phi_{1}}+\left(\frac{F_{0}+F_{n}}{2}-F_{j}\right)\partial_{\phi_{2}}=\partial_{x_{3}}+F_{j}\partial_{t},&\text{if}\quad f_{j}^{\prime}<0,\\
\partial_{\phi_{2}}=-\partial_{t},&\text{if}\quad f_{j}^{\prime}=0,\\
-\left(\partial_{\phi_{1}}+\left(\frac{F_{0}+F_{n}}{2}-F_{j}\right)\partial_{\phi_{2}}\right)=-(\partial_{x_{3}}+F_{j}\partial_{t}),&\text{if}\quad f_{j}^{\prime}>0,\end{cases}

then
v_{j}
rotates in a counterclockwise direction. It is then straightforward to see that
d(\mathfrak{R})=1
.
∎

To finish the proof of Theorem
7.9
in the
\ALF^{\pm}/\AFa
case, we fix the positions of the turning points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
and
\sharp\in\{\ALFm,\ALFp,\AFa(\beta\in\mathbb{R})\}
. Denote by
\mathcal{S}_{1,\sharp}\subset\mathcal{S}(\sharp|z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
the subset consisting of rod structures of degree
1
. By Theorem
3.12
we know that
\mathcal{S}_{1,\sharp}
is connected. Denote by
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}
the subset of
\mathcal{S}_{1,\sharp}
which consists of rod structures
\mathfrak{R}
with
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{n}=\alpha
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}
those with
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{n}=\alpha
that are of Type
\II
. Note that if
\sharp=\AFa
then
\alpha=0
and if
\sharp=\ALF^{\pm}
then
\alpha>0
. Similar to the proof of Theorem
3.12
we also have that each
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}
is connected. From the Biquard-Gauduchon construction we know each
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}
is nonempty. All we need to show is that for each
\alpha
,
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}
. This implies that
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{1}
, which finishes the proof.

As in the discussion in Section
5.3
if we have the convergence of rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{i}\rightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
, then the associated harmonic maps
\Phi_{i}
converges to
\Phi_{\infty}
smoothly away from the rods. In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}
is closed. It remains to prove that
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}
is open. One possible strategy to show this is by using the implicit function theorem and the explicit knowledge above on the rod structures of elements in
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}
. The naive count of parameters matches, but it does not seem obvious to see the infinitesimal openness (i.e., non-vanishing of the associated derivative map). We will instead use the approach motivated to prove related result on gravitational instantons by Biquard-Gauduchon-LeBrun
[
BGL24
]
. The latter is a generalization of the surprising openness of Type
\II
Einstein metrics on compact 4-manifolds, discovered by Wu
[
Wu19
]
and LeBrun
[
LeB21
]
. The advantage of this approach is that one obtains the openness without proving infinitesimal openness.

7.3.1.
Openness of
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}

In this section we prove

Proposition 7.12
.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}
is open.

Given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
in
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}^{\prime}
we let
\Phi
be the associated harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
. Suppose the rod vectors of
\mathfrak{R}
are given by
\mathfrak{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{n}
. Without loss of generality we may assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\alpha/2}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}=[\bv_{-\alpha/2}]
. Fix
\epsilon>0
small. Then an open neighborhood of
\mathfrak{R}
in
\mathcal{S}_{1,\alpha,\sharp}
consists of rod structures with the same positions of the turning points but with rod vectors given by
\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}
where for
j=0,\ldots,n
,

\text{the angle between }\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}\text{ and }\mathfrak{v}_{j}<\epsilon.

It suffices to show that for
\epsilon>0
small any harmonic map
\Phi_{\epsilon}
strongly tamed by such a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}
is also of Type
\II
. Without loss of generality, by applying a suitable
P\in PSO(2)
on
\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}
we can again assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}^{\epsilon}=[\bv_{\alpha/2}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}^{\epsilon}=[\bv_{-\alpha/2}]
.
We first prove

Lemma 7.13
.

There is a constant
C(\epsilon)>0
with
C(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, such that over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{N}(0)
for a fixed large
N
,
d(\Phi_{\epsilon},\Phi)<C(\epsilon)/r
.

Proof.

We modify
\Phi
to a model map
\Psi_{\epsilon}
which is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}
and has controlled hyperbolic distance to
\Phi_{\epsilon}
. For
\delta>0
, we divide
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
into a union of regions

\displaystyle U_{\infty,\delta}=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus N_{\delta}((z_{1},z_{n})),

\displaystyle U_{j,1,\delta}=N_{2\delta}((z_{j},\frac{z_{j}+z_{j+1}}{2})),

\displaystyle U_{j,2,\delta}=N_{2\delta}((\frac{z_{j}+z_{j+1}}{2},z_{j+1})).

Here
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
. Fix
\delta_{0}
to be a small positive constant. Define

\alpha_{j}:=\mathfrak{v}_{j-1}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{j},\quad\alpha_{j}^{\epsilon}:=\mathfrak{v}_{j-1}^{\epsilon}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}.

Moreover, set
P_{j,\epsilon}\in PSL(2;\mathbb{R})
to be such that

P_{j,\epsilon}.[\bv_{\pm\frac{\alpha_{j}}{2}}]=[\bv_{\pm\frac{\alpha_{j}^{\epsilon}}{2}}].

Define the map
\Psi_{j,1}
over
U_{j,1,3\delta_{0}}
as

\Psi_{j,1}:=P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j-1}^{\epsilon},\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}}^{-1}P_{j,\epsilon}P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j-1},\mathfrak{v}_{j}}.\Phi

and
\Psi_{j,2}
over
U_{j,2,3\delta_{0}}
as

\Psi_{j,2}:=P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon},\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}^{\epsilon}}^{-1}P_{j+1,\epsilon}P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j},\mathfrak{v}_{j+1}}.\Phi.

Next, we glue the above maps together to obtain the map
\Psi_{\epsilon}
. We first glue the maps
\{\Psi_{j,1},\Psi_{j,2}\}_{j=1,\ldots,n-1}
. On each overlap
U_{j,1,3\delta_{0}}\cap U_{j,2,3\delta_{0}}
, we can write
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}}.\Psi_{j,1}
and
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}}.\Psi_{j,2}
as

P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}}.\Psi_{j,1}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u_{1}}+e^{-u_{1}}v_{1}^{2}&e^{-u_{1}}v_{1}\\
e^{-u_{1}}v_{1}&e^{-u_{1}}\end{matrix}\right),\quad P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}}.\Psi_{j,2}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u_{2}}+e^{-u_{2}}v_{2}^{2}&e^{-u_{2}}v_{2}\\
e^{-u_{2}}v_{2}&e^{-u_{2}}\end{matrix}\right).

Pick a cut-off function
0\leq\chi_{j}\leq 1
which equals one over
U_{j,1,2\delta_{0}}
and has support in
U_{j,1,3\delta_{0}}
. Set

u:=\chi_{j}u_{1}+(1-\chi_{j})u_{2},\quad v:=\chi_{j}v_{1}+(1-\chi_{j})v_{2}

and define

\Psi^{\prime}_{j}:=\begin{cases}\Psi_{1},\text{ over }U_{j,1,\delta_{0}},\\
\frac{1}{\rho}P_{\mathfrak{v}_{j}^{\epsilon}}^{-1}.\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u}+e^{-u}v^{2}&e^{-u}v\\
e^{-u}v&e^{-u}\end{matrix}\right),\text{ over }U_{j,1,\delta_{0}}\cap U_{j,2,\delta_{0}},\\
\Psi_{2},\text{ over }U_{j,2,\delta_{0}}.\end{cases}

This way we have obtained the glued map
\Psi_{j}^{\prime}
over
U_{j,1,2\delta_{0}}\cup U_{j,2,2\delta_{0}}
. Similarly we can glue all the
\Psi_{j}^{\prime}
on the overlaps of
U_{j,1,2\delta_{0}}\cup U_{j,2,2\delta_{0}}
to obtain a glued map
\Psi^{\prime}
over
N_{2\delta_{0}}((z_{1},z_{n}))
.

Finally, on the overlap
N_{2\delta_{0}}((z_{1},z_{n}))\cap U_{\infty,\delta_{0}/2}
, we can write
\Psi^{\prime}
and
\Phi
as

\Psi^{\prime}=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{u^{\prime}}+e^{-u^{\prime}}v^{\prime 2}&e^{-u^{\prime}}v^{\prime}\\
e^{-u^{\prime}}v^{\prime}&e^{-u^{\prime}}\end{matrix}\right),\quad\Phi=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{u}+e^{-u}v^{2}&e^{-u}v\\
e^{-u}v&e^{-u}\end{matrix}\right).

Pick a cut-off function
0\leq\chi\leq 1
which equals one over
U_{\infty,\delta_{0}}
and supports in
U_{\infty,\delta_{0}/2}
. Set
\bar{u}:=\chi u+(1-\chi)u^{\prime},\bar{v}:=\chi v+(1-\chi)v^{\prime}
and define

\Psi_{\epsilon}:=\begin{cases}\Phi,\text{ over }U_{\infty,\delta_{0}},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}e^{\bar{u}}+e^{-\bar{u}}\bar{v}^{2}&e^{-\bar{u}}\bar{v}\\
e^{-\bar{u}}\bar{v}&e^{-\bar{u}}\end{matrix}\right),\text{ over }U_{\infty,\delta_{0}/2}\cap N_{\delta_{0}}((z_{1},z_{n})),\\
\Psi^{\prime},\text{ over }N_{\delta_{0}}((z_{1},z_{n}))\setminus U_{\infty,\delta_{0}/2}.\end{cases}

From the construction and our local regularity result in Section
4.2
, there is a constant
C(\epsilon)
which depends on
\epsilon
and goes to zero as
\epsilon
tends to zero, such that
|\Delta\Psi_{\epsilon}|<C(\epsilon)
. The map
\Psi_{\epsilon}
is globally tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}
, and moreover, an important feature is that the map
\Psi_{\epsilon}
coincides with
\Phi
over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{N}(0)
for large
N
.

Now as in the proof of Theorem
4.24
, setting

F_{\epsilon}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|x-y|^{-1}|\Delta\Psi_{\epsilon}|(y)dy,

we have

d(\Phi_{\epsilon},\Psi_{\epsilon})\leq F_{\epsilon},

which particularly gives

d(\Phi_{\epsilon},\Psi_{\epsilon})<C(\epsilon)/r

when
r
is large. This implies that

d(\Phi_{\epsilon},\Phi)<C(\epsilon)/r

when
r
is large.
∎

When
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, for the normalized augmentation
\nu_{\epsilon}
, we have the metric

g_{\epsilon}:=e^{2\nu_{\epsilon}}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+\rho\Phi_{\epsilon}.

Denote by
g
the corresponding metric associated to
\Phi
. From the lemma above, by the local regularity estimate in Proposition
4.12
we know that on the end the difference between the Weyl curvatures of
g_{\epsilon}
and
g
is controlled as

|W^{+}_{g_{\epsilon}}-W^{+}_{g}|_{g}<C(\epsilon)r^{-3}.

Moreover, since
g
is Type
\II
, we know that
|W^{+}_{g}|
is exactly comparable to
r^{-3}
(see
[
BG23
]
for example). Hence when
\epsilon
small, it satisfies that
\det(W^{+}_{g_{\epsilon}})>0
. Therefore, by
[
BGL24
]
, setting
\alpha_{\epsilon}
to be the unique positive eigenvalue of
W^{+}_{g_{\epsilon}}
, which is a positive function over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
, we define

f_{\epsilon}:=\alpha_{\epsilon}^{-1/3}

and

g_{K,\epsilon}:=f_{\epsilon}^{2}g_{\epsilon}.

Moreover, let
\omega_{\epsilon}
be an eigen-two-form of
W^{+}_{g_{\epsilon}}
such that
|\omega_{\epsilon}|^{2}_{g_{K,\epsilon}}=2
. Over the manifold
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
there is a pointwise inequality

(7.23)

3d(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon})\geq*(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\omega_{\epsilon}|^{2}+3|d\omega_{\epsilon}^{2}|).

We also have

(7.24)

2(2\sqrt{6}|W^{+}|+|s|)^{1/2}\geq|\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}|.

All the norms, Hodge stars, etc. in the above two inequalities are taken under
g_{K,\epsilon}
and
s
denotes the scalar curvature of
g_{K,\epsilon}
. For the derivation of these two inequalities, see
[
BGL24
]
.

Lemma 7.14
.

We have

(7.25)

\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}d(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon})=d(\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon})).

Proof.

Since the form
\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}
is invariant under
\partial_{\phi_{1}},\partial_{\phi_{2}}
and the two Killing fields commute, we have

\displaystyle d(\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}(\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}))-\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}(d(\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}))

\displaystyle=-\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon})-\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}(d(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}))\right)

\displaystyle=\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}d(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}).

∎

Denote

\gamma:=\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}(\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}),

which descends to a 1-form on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. Denote

\overline{\mathrm{dvol}}:=\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{2}}}\iota_{\partial_{\phi_{1}}}\dvol_{g_{K,\epsilon}},

which descends to a 2-form on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. Then (
7.23
) implies that over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}

(7.26)

3d\gamma\geq(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\omega_{\epsilon}|^{2}+3|d\omega_{\epsilon}^{2}|)\overline{\dvol}.

For any small
\tau>0
and large
N>0
, define
V_{\tau,N}:=B_{N}(0)\cap\{\rho>\tau\}
, whose boundary consists of the union of
\partial V_{\tau,N}^{1}:=\partial V_{\tau,N}\cap\{\rho=\tau\}
and
\partial V_{\tau,N}^{2}:=\partial B_{N}(0)\cap\{\rho>\tau\}
. By integrating by parts,

3\int_{\partial V_{\tau,N}^{1}}\gamma+3\int_{\partial V_{\tau,N}^{2}}\gamma\geq\int_{V_{\tau,N}}(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\omega_{\epsilon}|^{2}+3|d\omega_{\epsilon}^{2}|)\overline{\dvol}.

Lemma 7.15
.

We have

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\int_{\partial V_{\tau,N}^{1}}\gamma\to 0.

Proof.

One only needs to compute
\int_{\partial V_{\tau,N}^{1}}\gamma
locally. Near the interior of each rod or near each turning point, we can always pick the correct lattice to make the quotient of
g_{\epsilon}
smooth. It is then easy to see the behavior of
\gamma
and conclude the lemma.
∎

By setting
\tau\to 0
, we have

3\int_{\partial V_{0,N}^{2}}\gamma\geq\int_{V_{0,N}}(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\omega_{\epsilon}|^{2}+3|d\omega_{\epsilon}^{2}|)\overline{\dvol}.

Next we take the canonical lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}}
as given in Definition
4.34
, which is the same as
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
. The lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
gives rise to smooth Ricci-flat ends
(E_{\epsilon},g_{\epsilon,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
and
(E,g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
, which are obtained by taking the quotient of
g_{\epsilon}
and
g
respectively. By Proposition
A.2
we know that there is a diffeomorphism
\varpi:E\to E_{\epsilon}
such that

|\nabla_{g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}}^{k}(\varpi^{*}g_{\epsilon,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}-g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})|_{g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}}<C_{k}(\epsilon)/\mathfrak{r}^{k+1}.

Here
C_{k}(\epsilon)
depends on
k
and
C_{k}(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, and
\mathfrak{r}
denotes the distance function under
g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
.

Over the smooth end we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial V_{0,N}^{2}}\gamma

\displaystyle=C\int_{\pi^{-1}(\partial V_{0,N}^{2})}\omega_{\epsilon}\wedge*d\omega_{\epsilon}

(7.27)

\displaystyle\leq 2C\left(\int_{\pi^{-1}(\partial V_{0,N}^{2})}\dvol_{g_{K,\epsilon}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\pi^{-1}(\partial V_{0,N}^{2})}(2\sqrt{6}|W^{+}|+|s|)\dvol_{g_{K,\epsilon}}\right)^{1/2},

where here
\pi:E\to\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
is the projection map from the smooth end into the base
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
and the constant
C
depends on the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Now the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem A in
[
BGL24
]
, but performed over the smooth end
E
, shows that both integrals in (
7.27
) are going to 0 as
N\to\infty
. This shows
\nabla\omega_{\epsilon}\equiv 0
and
d\omega_{\epsilon}\equiv 0
, which implies that the conformal metric
g_{K,\epsilon}
is Kähler. Therefore
g_{\epsilon}
is Type
\II
and this finishes the proof of Proposition
7.12
.

7.3.2.
The
\Ae
case

Here we prove Theorem
7.9
in the case of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
. It is possible to argue as in the other cases as above, but there is a simpler argument which we present below.

We first show that a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
with Asymptotic Type
\Ae
and degree
d(\mathfrak{R})=1
is of Type
\II
. To see this, for
\delta>0
we set
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\delta}
to be the rod structure with Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
and turning points
\delta z_{j}
and with the same rod vectors as
\mathfrak{R}
. Then
d(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\delta})=1
. Hence the associated harmonic map
\Phi_{\delta}
is of Type
\II
. Letting
\delta\to 0
, by our analysis in Section
5.3
and
5.4
, the rescaled harmonic map
\varphi_{1/\delta}^{*}\Phi_{\delta}
converges to a limit harmonic map
\Phi_{\infty}
, which is globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
. Since
\Phi_{\delta}
is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}_{\delta}^{\prime}
, by Proposition
4.38
it follows that
\Phi_{\infty}
is strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
. Notice that being the limit of Type
\II
harmonic maps,
\Phi_{\infty}
must be of Type
\I
or Type
\II
, but we have
d(\mathfrak{R})=1
so by Theorem
7.7
,
\Phi_{\infty}
can not be of Type
\I
. Thus
\mathfrak{R}
is of Type
\II
.

Next we show that a Type
\II
rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
must have degree
1
. Choosing the normalized augmentation
\nu
, we obtain a Type
\II
Ricci-flat metric

g=e^{2\nu}(d\rho^{2}+dz^{2})+\rho\Phi

over
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. As in Section
7.3.1
,
g
is conformal to a Bach-flat Kähler metric
g_{K}=f^{2}g
. Using the canonical lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
given in Definition
4.34
, we obtain a smooth toric Ricci-flat end
(E,g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
of Asymptotic Type
\ALE
. Indeed, it is asymptotic to the flat
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and
g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
is conformal to a Bach-flat Kähler metric
g_{K,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
. As is proved in
[
Li23b
]
, the Kähler metric
g_{K,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
is incomplete at the infinity of the end
E
, and the metric completion is given by adding exactly one point
\{p_{\infty}\}
. Moreover, by a singularity removal theorem for
g_{K,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
, it follows that the resulting metric is smooth, which we denote by
(\overline{E},\overline{g}_{K,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
. The torus action naturally extends to
\overline{E}
, fixing
p_{\infty}
.

Notice that in general
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
may not be an enhancement for
\mathfrak{R}
and we do not have a global quotient by
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Nevertheless, we can work directly with the metric
g_{K}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with a natural
\mathbb{R}^{2}
action. It is easy to see that this action admits a moment map, which descends to a map from
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, whose image
\Delta
is bounded. We claim that the closure
\overline{\Delta}
(given by adding one point to
\Delta
, which corresponds to the point at infinity
p_{\infty}
) is a convex polygon, with the normal vector of each edge agreeing with the corresponding rod vector of
\mathfrak{R}
. From this, it is easy to see that
d(\mathfrak{R})=1
.

The claim follows from the Atiyah-Guillemin-Sternberg’s convexity theorem. We are not in the exact situation to apply the convexity theorem, but its proof can be carried over without essential difficulty. The point is that, locally with a suitable change of the quotient lattice we may obtain a smooth toric quotient. More precisely, near each turning point, the interior of each rod, and the infinity of
\mathbb{H}
, by picking the correct lattice, the quotient of
g_{K}
locally extends as a smooth Kähler metric, with stabilizers described by the rod vectors. This implies that
\overline{\Delta}
is a locally convex polygon, hence it is a convex polygon.

8.
Discussion

8.1.
Riemannian black hole uniqueness

In Theorem
1.1
we require
\beta\in(0,1)
. Notice that when
n=1
, by Proposition
6.1
we may allow
\beta
to be
0
, which then gives rise to the Schwarzschild space, but when
n\geq 2
it is easy to see that the arguments in Section
6.2
does not go through. Similarly the arguments fail to work for all
n\geq 1
when
\beta=1
. There is a deeper reason behind these technical constraints.
In fact, in these cases it can be shown that one can not obtain gravitational instantons with the desired Asymptotic Type. In other words, conical singularities must appear. This follows from the uniqueness result of Mars-Simon
[
MS99
]
, with a mild extension. See Appendix
B
.

Motivated by this and Theorem
1.1
, it seems that the following version of the Riemannian black hole conjecture is likely to hold

Conjecture 8.1

(Uniqueness conjecture I)
.

The Schwarzschild space is the unique
\AF_{0}
gravitational instanton up to isometry and scaling.

It is a natural question to investigate the geometric behavior of the
\AFa
gravitational instanton
g_{\beta}
constructed in Theorem
6.4
when
\beta\rightarrow 0
or
\beta\rightarrow 1
