. In the case
n=1
from Example
3.20
we know that as
\beta\rightarrow 1
the Kerr spaces split into the union of a Taub-NUT space and an anti-Taub-NUT space. In general we have a similar picture. Below we assume
n\neq 2
and only consider the case
\beta\rightarrow 0
. One can similarly deal with the case
\beta\rightarrow 1
, which we leave for the interested readers.

We first prove a technical result, which generalizes Proposition
4.38
to the
\ALF^{\pm}
and
\AFa
case.

Proposition 8.2
.

Suppose
\Phi
is a harmonic map globally tamed by a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\ALFm
,
\ALFp
, or
\AFa
. Then there is a
Q\in SL(2;\mathbb{R})
such that
Q.\mathfrak{v}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}_{0}
,
Q.\mathfrak{v}_{n}=\mathfrak{v}_{n}
, and
Q.\Phi
is the unique harmonic map which is strongly tamed by
Q.\mathfrak{R}
(as produced in Theorem
4.24
). Here we make
Q.\mathfrak{R}
into a rod structure by assigning its Asymptotic Type to be
\ALFm
,
\ALFp
, or
\AFa
accordingly.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
and
\mathfrak{v}_{n}=[\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}]
for some
\alpha>0
when the Asymptotic Type is
\ALFm
or
\ALFp
and
\alpha=0
when the Asymptotic Type is
\AFa
. Note that by Theorem
4.24
there is the unique harmonic map
\Psi
strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
, and by the global tameness of
\Phi
we have
d(\Phi,\Psi)<C
.

Next we first prove the proposition in the
\AFa
case. For simplicity, by applying a
U_{\beta}
action we may assume the Asymptotic Type is
\AF_{0}
. So on the end we have
d(\Phi,\Phi^{\AF_{0}})<C
. For each
R\gg 1
, consider the scaling down

\Phi_{R}:=D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi,

and notice that

\Phi^{\AF_{0}}=D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi^{\AF_{0}}.

Hence for any
\epsilon>0
and
R
large enough depending on
\epsilon
, on the annulus
A_{\epsilon,\infty}\subset\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
we also have
d(\Phi_{R},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})<C
. By the uniform estimate from Section
4.2
, up to passing to a subsequence we can take the limit of
\Phi_{R}
, which we denote by
\Phi_{\infty}
defined on
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
. The limit of the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{R}:=D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\mathfrak{R}
is a weak rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}
with only two infinite rods, sharing the same rod vector
\bv_{0}
. After merging the two infinite rods we obtain the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{R}^{\AF_{0}}
assigned with Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
, and
\Phi_{\infty}
is globally tamed by the rod structure
\mathfrak{R}^{\AF_{0}}
. As a consequence, we know that by choosing a suitable augmentation
\nu_{\infty}
and a lattice
\Lambda_{\infty}
,
(\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty})
together with
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}_{\infty},\Lambda_{\infty})
defines a smooth complete toric Ricci-flat metric
(M_{\infty},g_{\infty})
where
M_{\infty}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{1}
. Moreover, we have
d(\Phi_{\infty},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})<C
, which together with our estimate Proposition
4.12
imply that the curvature of
g_{\infty}
decays quadratically, hence by Lemma
4.40
we know
\int_{M_{\infty}}|\Rm_{g_{\infty}}|_{g_{\infty}}^{2}\dvol_{g_{\infty}}<\infty
. Because of Lemma
4.40
, the Gauss-Bonnet formula holds and we then have
\int_{M_{\infty}}|\Rm_{g_{\infty}}|_{g_{\infty}}^{2}\dvol_{g_{\infty}}=\chi(M_{\infty})=0
, which implies that
(M_{\infty},g_{\infty})
is actually flat. Since
\Phi_{\infty}
is associated to the flat metric
(M_{\infty},g_{\infty})
and
d(\Phi_{\infty},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})<C
, it follows that there is a positive constant
a>0
such that
\Phi_{\infty}=\mathrm{diag}(a\rho,1/(a\rho))
. Hence, if we take
P=D_{\sqrt{a}}
and consider the rod structure
P.\mathfrak{R}
assigned with Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
, the harmonic map
P.\Phi
satisfies
d(P.\Phi,\Phi^{\AF_{0}})\to 0
as we approach the infinity of
\mathbb{H}
. By the uniqueness part of proof of Theorem
4.24
, this already implies that
P.\Phi
is strongly tamed by
P.\mathfrak{R}
.

Now we prove the proposition for the case of
\ALFm
, where the case of
\ALFp
is similar. We can again perform the scaling down

\Phi_{R}:=D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi.

But this time notice that

D_{\sqrt{R}}.\varphi_{R^{-1}}^{*}\Phi^{\TNam}=\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha/R}}.

We still have
d(\Phi_{R},\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha/R}})<C
and since
\Phi^{\TN^{-}_{\alpha/R}}
converges to
\Phi^{\AF_{0}}
, the uniform estimate shows that
\Phi_{R}
converges to a limit
\Phi_{\infty}
globally tamed by
\mathfrak{R}^{\AF_{0}}
as above. Taking a suitable augmentation
\nu_{\infty}
and a lattice
\Lambda_{\infty}
again we get a smooth complete toric Ricci-flat metric
g_{\infty}
on
M_{\infty}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{1}
, which has
\int_{M_{\infty}}|\Rm_{g_{\infty}}|_{g_{\infty}}^{2}\dvol_{g_{\infty}}<\infty
. It follows from the Gauss-Bonnet formula that
g_{\infty}
is flat, and
d(\Phi_{\infty},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})<C
implies that there is a positive constant
a>0
such that
\Phi_{\infty}=\mathrm{diag}(a\rho,1/(a\rho))
. So take
P=D_{\sqrt{a}}
we have
d(P.\Phi,\Phi^{\TNam})\to 0
as we approach the infinity of
\mathbb{H}
. Assign
P.\mathfrak{R}
with Asymptotic Type
\ALFm
, we then have
P.\Phi
is strongly tamed by
P.\mathfrak{R}
.
∎

Notice that in Theorem
6.4
for each
\beta
the length vector
\bm{l}(\beta)
which gives rise to an
\AFa
gravitational instanton is not a priori known to be unique. We make an arbitrary choice of such
\bm{l}(\beta)
for each
\beta
.

Proposition 8.3
.

As
\beta\rightarrow 0
, in the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff sense, without rescalings,
(X_{n},g_{\beta})
splits into the union of

•

A Taub-NUT space, an anti-Taub-Bolt space and
k-1
Schwarzschild spaces (when
n=2k\geq 2
);

•

An anti-Taub-Bolt space, a Taub-Bolt space and
k-1
Schwarzschild spaces (when
n=2k+1\geq 3
);

Remark 8.4
.

When
n=2
it is also possible to see the above directly using the explicit expression of the Chen-Teo spaces
[
CT11
]
, similar to the discussion on the Kerr spaces in Example
3.20
.

Proof.

Clearly it suffices to prove that given an arbitrary sequence
\beta_{i}\rightarrow 0
and
\bm{l}^{i}=(l^{i}_{1},\cdots,l^{i}_{n})=\bm{l}(\beta_{i})
, after passing to a subsequence we have the desired splitting.

Denote the corresponding enhanced rod structure by
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Lambda)
and the augmented harmonic map by
(\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
. By assumption we have
\vartheta_{j}(\bm{l}^{i})=2\pi
for all
i,j
. It follows that the normalized rod vector along each rod agrees with the primitive vector.
For simplicity we will call a rod
vertical
if the normalized rod vector is given by
(1,0)^{\top}
,
horizontal
if the normalized rod vector is given by
(0,1)^{\top}
and
slant
if the normalized rod vector is given by
(1,-1)^{\top}
. The key in our argument is to show that the lengths of all horizontal rods are uniformly bounded above and bounded away from zero, whereas the lengths of all slant rods are going to infinity. This will be proved using the bubbling analysis in Section
5
and the uniqueness theorem of Mars-Simon (see Proposition
B.1
).

We denote by
S_{i}
the maximal length of a slant rod and by
H_{i}
the maximal length of a horizontal rod. Passing to a subsequence we may assume that
H_{i}
is achieved by some odd
j_{0}
and
S_{i}
is achieved by some even
j_{1}
, both independent of
i
. Denote by
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
the size of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
.

Step 1.
We must have
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\rightarrow\infty
. Suppose not, then passing to a subsequence we may assume
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\leq C
uniformly. Passing to a further subsequence we may reduce to the following two cases. We will draw a contradiction in both cases.

Case (a).

\mathfrak{s}_{i}\geq C^{\prime}>0
uniformly. Then using the analysis in Section
5
we obtain a limit harmonic map
\Phi_{\infty}
tamed by an enhanced rod structure
(\mathfrak{R}_{\infty},\Lambda)
of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
and with all the cone angles
2\pi
. This leads to a toric gravitational instanton
(X,g)
of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. As in the above discussion we may apply Proposition
B.1
to conclude that
(X,g)
must be isometric to either a Schwarzschild metric or a Taub-Bolt metric. Since the Asymptotic Type of
(X,g)
is
\AF_{0}
, we conclude that it is the Schwarzschild metric. Since
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
has more than 2 turning points, there must be turning points coming together, then we arrive at a contradiction by Proposition
5.12
and Proposition
5.14
.

Case (b).

\mathfrak{s}_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
. In this case we may apply the discussion in Section
5.4.1
. We rescale the sequence by
\mathfrak{s}_{i}^{-1}
and consider the first bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime},\Phi_{\infty,p},\nu_{\infty,p})
. It is easy to see that
\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,p}^{\prime}
must be of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. Since the cone angles along all the rods are fixed to be
2\pi
, we arrive at a contradiction from Proposition
5.13
.

Step 2.
We must have
H_{i}/S_{i}\rightarrow 0
. Suppose otherwise, then consider the scaling down by
\mathfrak{s}_{i}^{-1}\sim H_{i}^{-1}\to 0
. In the limit the horizontal rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
survives. We may argue as in the proof of Proposition
5.15
to conclude that the angle
\vartheta_{j_{0}}(\bm{l}^{i})\rightarrow 0
. Contradiction.

Step 3.
From
Step 2
we know that
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\sim S_{i}
. We now consider the twisted scaling-down

(8.1)

(\mathfrak{R}_{0,i},\Phi_{0,i},\nu_{0,i}):=(D_{\sqrt{S_{i}}}U_{\beta_{i}}.\varphi_{S_{i}^{-1}}^{*}\mathfrak{R}_{i},D_{\sqrt{S_{i}}}U_{\beta_{i}}.\varphi_{S_{i}^{-1}}^{*}\Phi_{i},\varphi_{S_{i}^{-1}}^{*}\nu_{i}),

which converges to the first bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R},\Phi,\nu)
where the slant rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{1}}
survives. As in Section
5.4.2
we can analyze the twist action on the lattice
\Lambda
due to the scaling effect. As a result we see that the normalized rod vector along a horizontal rod for
D_{\sqrt{S_{i}}}U_{\beta_{i}}.\varphi_{S_{i}^{-1}}^{*}\Phi_{i}
becomes

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0,i,H}=(\beta_{i},S_{i}^{-1})^{\top},

the normalized rod vector along a slant rod becomes

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0,i,S}=(1-\beta_{i},-S_{i}^{-1})^{\top},

and the normalized rod vector a long a vertical rod is still

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}=\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{n}=\bv_{0}=(1,0)^{\top}.

Notice that we have

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0,i,S}=\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}.

See Figure
15
for a picture illustrating the scaling from
(\mathfrak{R}_{i},\Phi_{i},\nu_{i})
to
(\mathfrak{R}_{0,i},\Phi_{0,i},\nu_{0,i})
.

(-\beta_{i},1)^{\top}
(0,1)^{\top}
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0}
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0,i,H}
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{0,i,S}
l_{1}
l_{2}
l_{4}
l_{3}
D_{\sqrt{S_{i}}}U_{\beta_{i}}.\varphi_{S_{i}^{-1}}^{*}

Figure 15.
The twisted scaling from
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
to
\mathfrak{R}_{0,i}
. In this example
l_{2}\gg l_{4}\gg l_{1}\gg l_{3}
, and
S_{i}=l_{2}\to\infty,H_{i}=l_{1}
.

Step 4.
As in Section
5
after passing to a subsequence we obtain a bubble tree convergence. More precisely, we first have the convergence of
(\mathfrak{R}_{0,i},\Phi_{0,i},\nu_{0,i})
to a weak limit
(\mathfrak{R},\Phi,\nu)
. For each cluster
\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{0,i}
with
m_{p}>1
we consider the rescaled rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}}
as in Section
5.3
, and the associated twisted rescaled augmented harmonic maps
(\Phi_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}},\nu_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}})
. Taking the limits of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}}
and
(\Phi_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}},\nu_{\mathfrak{C}_{i,p}})
, we obtain the first level of bubble limits. Repeating this process for all of the further clusters we obtain all levels of bubble limits.

In this step we consider the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
where the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
survives and the goal is to show that
C^{-1}\leq H_{i}\leq C
.
We denote by
\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}
the rod of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
that contains
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
. Notice that in general
\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}
may be bigger than
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
due to possible merging of rods. By assumption, in all bigger scales no horizontal rod can survive. We want to compute the normalized rod vector along
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
with respect to
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
. For this purpose we will compute the twist necessary in order to obtain the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
. There are three cases.

Case (A).
Both infinite rods of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
arise from a slant rod. In particular,
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is of Type
\AF_{0}
. In this case, let us label all the intermediate bubble limits between the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
and the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R},\Phi,\nu)
, in the order of decreasing scales as

(\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\prime},\Phi_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathfrak{R}_{K-1}^{\prime},\Phi_{K-1}^{\prime},\nu_{K-1}^{\prime}),

with

(\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime},\Phi_{0}^{\prime},\nu_{0}^{\prime}):=(\mathfrak{R},\Phi,\nu),\quad(\mathfrak{R}_{K}^{\prime},\Phi_{K}^{\prime},\nu_{K}^{\prime}):=(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}).

Note that the bubble
(\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime},\Phi_{0}^{\prime},\nu_{0}^{\prime})
has its infinite rods arising from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{n}
. From our specific choice of the rod vectors for
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
, we know that there is a unique
k_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, such that

•

all the bubbles
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
with
1\leq k<k_{0}
have one of their infinite rods arising from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
or
\mathfrak{I}_{n}
and the other infinite rod arising from a slant rod;

•

all the bubbles
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
with
k\geq k_{0}
have both infinite rods arising from a slant rod.

It is possible to have
k_{0}=1
, in which case all of the intermediate bubble limits have their infinite rods both arising from a slant rod. Moreover, for each bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
with
k<k_{0}
, it must have Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. For otherwise, the infinite rod vectors of
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
are different. In particular, if we denote by the twisted rescaled augmented harmonic map that gives rise to this bubble limit, and denote the corresponding normalized rod vectors along the horizontal/slant/vertical rods as

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,H},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,S},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,0}

respectively, then we always have the additive relation

(8.2)

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,H}=-\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,S}+\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,0}.

The assumption that the infinite rod vectors of
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime}_{k},\nu^{\prime}_{k})
are different implies that the naturally associated lattice, which is generated by the limit of
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,S},\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{k,i,0}
, is non-degenerate.
Since both infinite rods of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
arise from a slant rod, by passing to the first non-trivial bubble limit of the convergence
(\mathfrak{R}_{k,i},\Phi_{k,i},\nu_{k,i})\to(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
where one of the finite rod would be
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
, by Proposition
5.14
we arrive at a contradiction. Notice that this argument fails if
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
is of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
, since in this case the associated lattice for
(\mathfrak{R}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi_{k}^{\prime},\nu_{k}^{\prime})
degenerates and one can not apply Proposition
5.14
directly.

If
k_{0}=1
, from the above and the discussion in Section
5.3
we know that the normalized rod vector along
\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}
is given by the limit of

(8.3)

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}^{\prime}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}}.P_{\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}.\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H},

where
\mu_{i}
is the scale such that the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
arises, so
\mu_{i}\sim S_{i}^{-1}H_{i}
. Notice that we only need to rotate once using
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}
, since all the intermediate scales share the same infinite rod vectors, and the rest contribution to the twist all comes from diagonal matrices of the form
D_{\lambda}
. Also from the explicit formula of
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,S}
we know that
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}
is given by a counter-clockwise rotation by angle
\theta_{i}
, which is small and at the order
S_{i}^{-1}(1-\beta_{i})
. By construction we know
C^{-1}\leq|\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}^{\prime}|\leq C
for a uniform constant
C
. It is now a straightforward computation based on (
8.3
) and the explicit expression of
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}
to see that
C^{-1}\leq H_{i}\leq C
.
In this case it is also easy to see that the total twist

\sqrt{\mu_{i}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}}.P_{\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}.\sqrt{S_{i}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{S_{i}}}U_{\beta_{i}}

is uniformly non-degenerate, such that
\Lambda
induces a natural enhancement
\Lambda^{\prime}
for
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
. By assumption the cone angle along each rod in
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is
2\pi
. It follows that
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
defines a 4-dimensional complete toric Ricci-flat manifold
(X^{\prime},g^{\prime})
. A priori from Section
5
we only know
\Phi^{\prime}
is globally tame by
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
, but by Proposition
8.2
we may conclude that
(X^{\prime},g^{\prime})
is a toric gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
. An application of the uniqueness result Proposition
B.1
then gives that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
has exactly one finite rod and
(X^{\prime},g^{\prime})
must be a Schwarzschild metric.

If
k_{0}>1
, the situation is more complicated. Let us denote by
\mu_{1,i}
the scale where the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}_{k_{0}-1},\Phi_{k_{0}-1},\nu_{k_{0}-1})
arises, so
\mu_{1,i}
is comparable to the length of the finite rods in
\mathfrak{R}_{0,i}
that survive in the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}_{k_{0}-1},\Phi_{k_{0}-1},\nu_{k_{0}-1})
. Let us moreover denote by
\mu_{2,i}\mu_{1,i}
the scale where the bubble
(\mathfrak{R}_{K},\Phi_{K},\nu_{K})
arises, hence
\mu_{2,i}\mu_{1,i}
is comparable to the length of the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
in
\mathfrak{R}_{0,i}
, which is then comparable to
S_{i}^{-1}H_{i}
.
For simplicity, also set

(8.4)

\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{i,S}:=\sqrt{\mu_{1,i}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{\mu_{1,i}}}.P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}.\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,S}.

By assumption the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}_{k_{0}-1},\Phi_{k_{0}-1},\nu_{k_{0}-1})
has one of its infinite rods arising from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
or
\mathfrak{I}_{n}
, and the other infinite rod arising from a slant rod.
The vector
\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{i,S}
is precisely the normalized rod vector along the slant rod, and the corresponding rod vector is converging to
[(1,0)^{\top}]
as
i\to\infty
, since the Asymptotic Type of
\mathfrak{R}_{k_{0}-1}
is
\AF_{0}
. The rotation
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}
is counter-clockwise by angle
\theta_{1,i}
, which is small at the order
S_{i}^{-1}(1-\beta_{i})
. Simple computation gives that
S_{i}^{-1}\mu_{1,i}^{-1}\to 0
.

From the discussion in Section
5.3
we know that in the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
the normalized rod vector along
\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}
is given by the limit of

(8.5)

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}^{\prime}=\sqrt{\mu_{2,i}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{\mu_{2,i}}}.P_{\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{i,S}}.\sqrt{\mu_{{1,i}}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{\mu_{1,i}}}.P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}.\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}.

This is because in total we only need to rotate twice using
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}
and
P_{\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{i,S}}
, since all the scales before
(\mathfrak{R}_{k_{0}},\Phi_{k_{0}},\nu_{k_{0}})
share the same infinite rod vectors, and all the scales from
(\mathfrak{R}_{k_{0}},\Phi_{k_{0}},\nu_{k_{0}})
to
(\mathfrak{R}_{K},\Phi_{K},\nu_{K})
also share the same infinite rod vectors.
See Figure
16
for a picture.

\mu_{1,i}

Figure 16.
A twisted scaling-down
(\mathfrak{R}_{0,i},\Phi_{0,i},\nu_{0,i})
with four finite rods
l_{1}\ll l_{3}\ll l_{2}\ll l_{4}\sim 1
and the intermediate scales between the bubble
(\mathfrak{R},\Phi,\nu)
and
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
. In this example
k_{0}=2
, and
\mu_{1,i}\sim l_{2},\mu_{2,i}\sim l_{3}/l_{2}
.

The rotation
P_{\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{i,S}}
is counter-clockwise by angle
\theta_{2,i}
that is small at the order
S_{i}^{-1}(1-\beta_{i})\mu_{1,i}^{-1}
. A direct computation based on (
8.5
) and the above give that

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}^{\prime}\sim(\beta_{i},\frac{S_{i}^{-1}}{\mu_{1,i}\mu_{2,i}})\sim(\beta_{i},\frac{1}{H_{i}}).

Because of the convergence to the bubble
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
, we have
C^{-1}\leq|\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}^{\prime}|\leq C
, which forces
C^{-1}\leq H_{i}\leq C
. Similar to the case
k_{0}=1
, we know the total twist is controlled and
\Lambda
induces a natural enhancement
\Lambda^{\prime}
for
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
. The toric gravitational instanton
(X^{\prime},g^{\prime})
associated to
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
must be a Schwarzschild space by Proposition
B.1
.

Case (B).
Exactly one infinite rod of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
arises from the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
. By assumption the other infinite rod of
\mathfrak{R}
must come from a slant rod. It then follows that all the intermediate scales between the scale giving the bubble
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
and the scale giving the bubble
(\mathfrak{R},\Phi,\nu)
also have one infinite rod from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and the other infinite rod from a slant rod. Similar to
Case (A)
, since passing to the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
the horizontal rod
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
survives, if we denote the normalized rod vector along the horizontal/slant/vertical rods in
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
as

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,H}^{\prime},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,S}^{\prime},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,0}^{\prime},

then we also have the additive relation

(8.6)

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,H}^{\prime}=-\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,S}^{\prime}+\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,0}^{\prime}.

This forces the infinite rod vectors of
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
to be different, as otherwise by the relation above
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
would have only one rod vector (since the rod vectors of the slant/vertical rods in
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
now are all the same which with the additive relation implies the rod vectors of the horizontal rods are also the same), and in particular, up to sign,
\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,H}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,S}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{\infty,0}^{\prime}
. This violates the additive relation above.

To obtain the normalized rod vector of
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
along
\mathfrak{I}_{j_{0}}
, note that starting from
(\mathfrak{R}_{0,i},\Phi_{0,i},\nu_{0,i})
we only need to rotate once using
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}
, and the rest of the twist is solely contributed by diagonal matrices. The rotation by
P_{\mathfrak{v}_{0},\mathfrak{v}_{i,S}}
is again a counter-clockwise rotation with angle
\theta_{i}
small at the order
S_{i}^{-1}(1-\beta_{i})
.
It follows that the normalized rod vector along
\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}
is given by the limit of

\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H}^{\prime}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}^{-1}}D_{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}}.P_{\mathfrak{v}_{i,S},\mathfrak{v}_{0}}.\overline{\mathfrak{v}}_{i,H},

where
\mu_{i}
is the scale such that the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
arises hence
\mu_{i}\sim S_{i}^{-1}H_{i}
. A computation shows the two infinite rod vectors are comparable to
[(1,\pm H_{i}^{-1})^{\top}]
respectively. Since the infinite rod vectors of
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
are different, again this implies that
C^{-1}\leq H_{i}\leq C
.

In this case from the discussion in Section
5.3
we know
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type either
\Ae
or
\ALFm
. It cannot be of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
, since in
\mathfrak{R}_{0,i}
, between the rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
of
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and the slant rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{0,i,S}=[(1-\beta_{i},-S_{i}^{-1})^{\top}]
of the slant rods we have

(8.7)

\alpha_{0,S}:=\mathfrak{v}_{0}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{0,i,S}\sim S_{i}^{-1}\to 0.

Hence by our discussion of the five cases in Section
5.3
, if the infinite rod vectors of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
are non-parallel, it is of Asymptotic Type
\ALFm
or
\Ae
.
In either case as in
Case (A)
one can argue that there is a natural enhancement
\Lambda^{\prime}
in the limit and we obtain a complete toric Ricci-flat 4-manifold
(X,g)
. If
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is of Asymptotic Type
\Ae
, then by Proposition
4.38
we know
(X,g)
is an asymptotically Euclidean gravitational instanton. But then
(X,g)
has to be flat by the Bishop-Gromov volume comparison. So this case can not happen and
(X,g)
is a toric gravitational instanton of Asymptotic Type
\ALFm
. As in
Case (A)
one can then argue that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
has exactly one finite rod and
(X^{\prime},g^{\prime})
is an anti-Taub-Bolt space by Proposition
B.1
.

Case (C).
Exactly one infinite rod of
\mathfrak{R}
arises from the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{n}
of
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
. This is similar to the discussion in
Case (B)
with only one difference that the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
now is of Asymptotic Type
\ALFp
. We have
C^{-1}\leq H_{i}\leq C
and the bubble limit defines a Taub-Bolt metric by Proposition
B.1
.

Step 5.

In all the three cases in
Step 4
we have shown that the maximum length of a horizontal rod is controlled by
C^{-1}\leq H_{i}\leq C
. Examining our arguments above we see that a horizontal rod will have a similar length control if it is the first one to see when we follow the bubble tree convergence, namely, in all of its previous scales no horizontal rods survive.

Next we want to say that there is no further bubbling after this scale. As a consequence this shows that all horizontal rods are uniformly controlled. We need to divide into three cases according to
Step 4
.

Case (A).
If there is a further bubbling at some point, then we go to the first non-trivial bubble limit starting from this scale. The new bubble limit will have to be of Asymptotic Type
\AF_{0}
or
\Ae
. In either case, by Proposition
5.13
or
5.12
(together with the Bishop-Gromov volume comparison in the
\Ae
case) we end up with a contradiction. The key point is that in the bubble limit
(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
, the naturally associated lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}
is non-degenerate so these two Propositions are applicable to further deeper scales of it.

Case (B).
and
Case (C).
can be dealt with in a similar fashion.

Step 6
It follows from
Step 5
that all the slant rods have a uniform lower bound on their length. Combining with
Step 4
we know their lengths must all go to infinity, for otherwise at the scale of horizontal rods (the length of them in
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
all have uniform upper bound and lower bound away from zero by
Step 4
) we would obtain a limit rod structure with more than one finite rod, which again contradicts the uniqueness result in Proposition
B.1
.

It then follows that if we consider pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limits based on a point on a horizontal rod we always obtain a limit rod structure with exactly one finite rod. Again using Proposition
8.2
and Proposition
B.1
we know that the limit gravitational instanton is either a (anti-)Taub-NUT space, a (anti-)Taub-Bolt space, or a Schwarzschild space. More precisely:

•

If the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit is based on
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
, then since as in (
8.7
) we have
\alpha_{0,S}\to 0
, by our consideration in Section
5.3
and the structure of
\mathfrak{R}_{i,0}
, we know the limit is an anti-Taub-Bolt space.

•

If
n=2k+1
, then the rod
\mathfrak{I}_{n-1}
is a horizontal rod. Similar consideration shows that the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit based on
\mathfrak{I}_{n-1}
is a Taub-Bolt space, since in
\mathfrak{R}_{0,i}
between the slant rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{0,i,S}=[(1-\beta_{i},-S_{i}^{-1})^{\top}]
of
\mathfrak{I}_{n-2}
and the rod vector
\mathfrak{v}_{0}=[(1,0)^{\top}]
of
\mathfrak{I}_{n}
we have

\alpha_{S,0}:=\mathfrak{v}_{0,i,S}\wedge\mathfrak{v}_{0}\sim S_{i}\to\infty.

•

If
n=2k
, then we can instead consider the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit based on the fixed point corresponding to the turning point
z_{n}
, and similar consideration shows that the limit is a Taub-NUT space.

•

For all the other horizontal rods, the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit is a Schwarzschild space.

Step 7
. Using Lemma
4.40
we have

\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{X_{n}}|\Rm_{g_{\beta}}|^{2}\dvol_{g_{\beta}}=\chi(X_{n})=n+1,

and for the Taub-NUT metric
g_{\text{TN}}
, Taub-Bolt metric
g_{\text{TB}}
, and Schwarzschild metric
g_{\text{Sc}}
we have

\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{X}|\Rm_{g_{\text{TN}}}|^{2}\dvol_{g_{TN}}=1,

\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{X}|\Rm_{g_{\text{TB}}}|^{2}\dvol_{g_{\text{TB}}}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{X}|\Rm_{g_{\text{Sc}}}|^{2}\dvol_{g_{\text{Sc}}}=2.

It follows that the above pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limits have caught all the curvature energy of
g_{\beta_{i}}
under the degeneration. Any other pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit is the limit with locally uniformly bounded curvature.
∎

The above discussion suggests a possible new gluing construction of
\AFa
gravitational instantons for special range of
\beta
, which may help understand the uniqueness question:

Conjecture 8.5

(Uniqueness conjecture, II)
.

In the setting of Theorem
6.4
, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
, the length vector
\bm{l}=\bm{l}(\beta)
is unique.

A more ambitious question is to prove general black hole uniqueness theorem with fixed topology

Question 8.6

(Uniqueness conjecture, III)
.

For each
\beta\in(0,1)
, is the
\AFa
gravitational instanton on
X_{n}
discovered in Theorem
1.1
unique up to isometry?

A positive answer is known when
n=1,2
by
[
AAD+23
]
under the assumption of an
S^{1}
symmetry, where the uniqueness is proved by exploiting an identity characterising the property of being conformally Kähler.

8.2.
Explicit description

Notice that our construction in Theorem
6.4
does not yield an explicit formula for the axisymmetric harmonic map that corresponds to a toric
\AFa
gravitational instanton. As discussed in Section
7
, in the low degree case we
can further reduce to the setting of axisymmetric harmonic functions, which can be explicitly written down. On the other hand, there is a general method using integrable systems, namely the
Inverse Scattering Transform
(see for example
[
BZ78
]
), to construct both Lorentzian and Riemannian solutions to the Einstein equation with symmetry. Indeed, the Chen-Teo metrics were first discovered using the Inverse Scattering Transform. One may wonder whether the gravitational instantons constructed in Theorem
6.4
can be recovered using the inverse scattering transform or another ansatz. It is also interesting to develop a systematic study relating the harmonic map viewpoint and the Inverse Scattering Transform viewpoint.

Question 8.7
.

Is there an explicit description of axisymmetric harmonic maps which are strongly tamed by a rod structure, when the degree is bigger than 1?

A related question is

Question 8.8

(Stability)
.

Are the metrics constructed in Theorem
1.1
stable in terms of the second variation of the Einstein-Hilbert functional?

Notice that Biquard-Ozuch
[
BO23
]
showed that the Kerr metrics, Taub-Bolt metrics and Chen-Teo metrics are all unstable. It is a folklore open question that whether a gravitational instanton is stable if and only if it has special holonomy (i.e., locally Kähler up to orientation reversal).

8.3.
Classification

It is far-reaching to classify all gravitational instantons. The following is a more sensible question

Question 8.9
.

Classify all (simply-connected) toric gravitational instantons.

One expects that the similar strategy as developed in this paper may generate more examples of gravitational instantons of Asymptotic Type
\AFa
. Similarly, one may ask

Question 8.10
.

Are there toric
\ALF^{\pm}
or
\ALE
gravitational instantons which are not locally Hermitian? Equivalently, are there augmented harmonic maps which are strongly tamed by a smooth enhanced rod structure of degree bigger than 1, such that the associated cone angles along all rods are all
2\pi
?

This is related to the Riemannian black hole uniqueness question in the
\ALF^{\pm}
case and the Bando-Kasue-Nakajima conjecture
[
BKN89
]
in the
\ALE
case. There are extra difficulties to construct toric gravitational instantons in these cases: in the
\ALF^{\pm}
case the Mars-Simon uniqueness
[
MS99
]
gives constraints on the possible torus action; in the
\ALE
case due to the scaling invariance of the model map, there is one less rod length parameter than the angle parameters.

Appendix A
Gauge fixing

We first prove

Proposition A.1
.

Given a rod structure
\mathfrak{R}
of Asymptotic Type
\sharp\in\{\Ae,\ALFm,\ALFp,\AFa\}
and a harmonic map
\Phi
strongly tamed by
\mathfrak{R}
, with the normalized augmentation
\nu
and the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
, the associated smooth Ricci-flat end
(E,g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}})
also has Asymptotic Type
\sharp
with decay rate
\delta=1
.

Proof.

We prove the proposition for the
\AF_{0}
case and the other cases are similar. Over the end
E
, which is the completion of
(\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\setminus B_{N}(0))\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
, there is the model metric
g^{\AF_{0}}
. It suffices to find a diffeomorphism
\varpi:E\to E
, such that
\varpi_{*}g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
is smooth, and

|\nabla_{g^{\AF_{0}}}^{k}(\varpi_{*}g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}-g^{\AF_{0}})|_{g^{\AF_{0}}}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-k-1}).

We construct the diffeomorphism on each annuli
A_{2^{j},2^{j+2}}
with
j\in\mathbb{N}
large, then patch them together. By the modified rescaling
\Phi_{2^{j}}=D_{\sqrt{2^{j}}}.\varphi_{2^{-j}}^{*}\Phi
, we scale the harmonic map
\Phi
on
A_{2^{j-1},2^{j+3}}
to
\Phi_{2^{j}}
on
A_{1/2,8}
which satisfies
d(\Phi_{2^{j}},\Phi^{\AF_{0}})\leq C2^{-j}
. Set

\Phi_{2^{j}}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u_{j}}+e^{-u_{j}}v_{j}^{2}&e^{-u_{j}}v_{j}\\
e^{-u_{j}}v_{j}&e^{-u_{j}}\end{matrix}\right),\quad\Phi=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u}+e^{-u}v^{2}&e^{-u}v\\
e^{-u}v&e^{-u}\end{matrix}\right)

over
A_{1/2,8}
and
A_{2^{j-1},2^{j+3}}
as before, then
u_{j}=\varphi_{2^{-j}}^{*}u
and
v_{j}=2^{-j}\varphi_{2^{-j}}^{*}v
. The uniform local regularity Proposition
4.12
gives that on
A_{1/2,8}

(A.1)

\|u_{j}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C2^{-j},\quad|\nabla^{k}v_{j}|\leq C_{k}2^{-j}\rho^{2-k}

for
0<\alpha<1
and
k=0,1,2
.

Step (1). Construction of local gauge in each annulus.

Now, under the augmentation
\varphi_{2^{-j}}^{*}\nu
on
A_{1/2,8}
, with the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
, we get the Ricci-flat metric
h
associated to
\Phi_{2^{j}}
defined on the completion of
A_{1/2,8}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
(denoted by
E_{1/2,8}
). Define the sector region

Sec_{\tau}:=\{\tau<\theta<\pi-\tau\}\subset\mathbb{H}^{\circ}.

For a fixed small
\tau
, over
A_{1/2,8}\cap Sec_{\tau}
, the harmonic map equation is non-degenerate. Hence, by (
A.1
) on
A_{1/2,8}\cap Sec_{\tau}
standard elliptic estimates give the improvement

(A.2)

\|u_{j}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}2^{-j},\quad\|v_{j}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}2^{-j}

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, on the region
\pi^{-1}(A_{1/2,8}\cap Sec_{\tau})\subset E_{1/2,8}
, under the coordinate
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}):=(r\cos\phi_{1},r\sin\phi_{1},z,\phi_{2})
, we already have

|\nabla_{g^{\AF_{0}}}^{k}(h-g^{\AF_{0}})|_{g^{\AF_{0}}}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{2^{j}}.

As for the region in
A_{1,4}
near the rod, we cover them by balls
B_{t}\subset A_{1/2,8}
with fixed radius
1/4
. Then we cover the near rod region in
E_{1,4}:=\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4})
by simply-connected open sets
U_{t,s}\subset E_{1/2,8}
with smooth controlled boundary over
B_{t}
. Introduce the coordinate

x_{1}:=r\cos\phi_{1},\quad x_{2}:=r\sin\phi_{1},\quad x_{3}:=z,\quad x_{4}:=\phi_{2}

on
U_{t,s}
. On each
U_{t,s}
, we solve for the harmonic coordinates

(A.3)

{h}^{kl}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{l}}\overline{x}_{j}+{h}^{kl}{\Gamma}^{r}_{kl}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{r}}\overline{x}_{j}=0,\quad\text{with }\overline{x}_{j}=x_{j}\text{ along }\partial U_{t,s}.

Since we know the metric
h
is
C^{0,1}
under
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
by Lemma
4.14
, the harmonic coordinates are
W^{2,p}
for any large
p
, hence is
C^{1,\alpha}
. Our uniform local regularity Proposition
4.12
gives that under
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

(A.4)

|h_{kl}-g^{\AF_{0}}_{kl}|+|\Gamma^{r}_{kl}|\leq\frac{C}{2^{j}}.

The elliptic estimate based on (
A.3
) gives
\|\overline{x}_{j}-x_{j}\|_{W^{2,p}}\leq C2^{-j}
for any large
p
, which implies
\|\overline{x}_{j}-x_{j}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C2^{-j}
under
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
. The Ricci-flat metric
h
is smooth under
(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2},\overline{x}_{3},\overline{x}_{4})
. Define the following map where we use the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
for
U_{t,s}

\zeta_{t,s}:U_{t,s}\to U_{t,s},

x\mapsto\zeta_{t,s}(x):=(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2},\overline{x}_{3},\overline{x}_{4}).

Shrinking the domain for
\zeta_{t,s}
slightly, the map is a
C^{1,\alpha}
diffeomorphism into its image when
j
is large. Now the metric
(\zeta_{t,s})_{*}h
under
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
is smooth. The bound
\|\overline{x}_{j}-x_{j}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C2^{-j}
and (
A.4
) imply

\|(\zeta_{t,s})_{*}h-g^{\AF_{0}}_{kl}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\leq\frac{C}{2^{j}}.

Since the Ricci-flat equation is elliptic in harmonic coordinates, starting from the above, it is standard to show
\|(\zeta_{t,s})_{*}h-g^{\AF_{0}}_{kl}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}2^{-j}
.

Step (2). Equivariant patching to global gauge in each annulus.

In the next we need to patch local diffeomorphisms on
\pi^{-1}(A_{1/2,8}\cap Sec_{\tau})
and
U_{t,s}
together to construct a global equivariant diffeomorphism on
E_{1,4}
. Denote the identity diffeomorphism on
U_{0,0}:=\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4}\cap Sec_{\tau})
as
\zeta_{0,0}
. For the open cover of
E_{1,4}
by
U_{t,s}
, we take standard cut-off functions
0\leq\chi_{t,s}\leq 1
that

•

is smooth under the harmonic coordinate for
U_{t,s}
;

•

has support in
U_{t,s}
;

•

equals one in a slightly smaller open subset of
U_{t,s}
and
\sum\chi_{t,s}\equiv 1
.

Consider the center-of-mass function

\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}:E_{1/2,8}\times E_{1/2,8}\to\mathbb{R}_{+},

(x,y)\mapsto\sum_{t,s}\chi_{t,s}(x)\cdot d_{h}(\zeta_{t,s}(x),y)^{2}.

The function is almost invariant under the
\mathbb{T}
action, in the sense that for
a\in\mathbb{T}
,

\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}(a\cdot x,a\cdot y)-\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}(x,y)\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}2^{-j},

where the
C^{k,\alpha}
norm is measured under the metric
h
. Denote by
\mathfrak{C}_{j}
the average of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}
over the
\mathbb{T}
orbits.
Now for any
x\in E_{1,4}
, we can minimize the function
y\mapsto\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}_{j}(x,y)
. The minimizing point must take place at
y
close to
x
, hence by convexity, the minimizing point is unique. Denote the minimizing point as
\mathfrak{z}_{j}(x)
. Then the map

\mathfrak{z}_{j}:E_{1,4}\to E_{1/2,8}

is a
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant diffeomorphism into its image, and the push-forward metric satisfies

\|(\mathfrak{z}_{j})_{*}h-g^{\AF_{0}}_{kl}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{2^{j}}.

To recover the diffeomorphism on the annulus
A_{2^{k},2^{j+2}}
, we can scale
A_{1,4}
back to
A_{2^{j},2^{j+2}}
, where the metric
h
is rescaled to
2^{2j}h
. Because of the modified rescaling for
\Phi
, after further taking the quotient of
2^{2j}h
by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{j}}
action along
\phi_{2}
, we exactly recover the metric
g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
on
\pi^{-1}(A_{2^{j},2^{j+2}})
. Since
\mathfrak{z}_{j}
is
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant, it induces a
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant diffeomorphism
\overline{\mathfrak{z}}_{j}
on
\pi^{-1}(A_{2^{j},2^{j+2}})
smoothing
g_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}}
with the desired regularity.

Step (3). Equivariant patching of adjacent annuli.

Finally, we need to patch the diffeomorphisms
\overline{\mathfrak{z}}_{j}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{z}}_{j+1}
on the overlap of adjacent annuli
A_{2^{j},2^{j+2}}
and
A_{2^{j+1},2^{j+3}}
. Recall for the construction of
\mathfrak{z}_{j+1}
, we need to consider the modified rescaling
\Phi_{2^{j+1}}
on
A_{1/2,8}
and repeat the construction in
Step (1)
, which is equivalent to consider the modified rescaling
\Phi_{2^{j}}
on
A_{1,16}
. Therefore, it suffices to patch the
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant diffeomorphisms
\mathfrak{z}_{j}
on
A_{1,4}
and
\mathfrak{z}_{j+1}
on
A_{2,8}
. One just needs to perform the center-of-mass construction again. Hence we have obtained the desired global diffeomorphism
\varpi
.
∎

For any two harmonic maps
\Phi^{1},\Phi^{2}
that are strongly tamed by two rod structures
\mathfrak{R}_{1},\mathfrak{R}_{2}
with the same Asymptotic Type
\sharp\in\{\ALFm,\ALFp,\AFa\}
and the same infinite rod vectors, suppose moreover that

(A.5)

d(\Phi^{1},\Phi^{2})<\frac{\epsilon}{r}

for small
\epsilon
and
r>N
, where
N
is a fixed large constant. Then under the normalized augmentations
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
, the lattices
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}
, which we denote by
\Lambda
. These give rise to
C^{0,1}
Ricci-flat ends
(E,g_{1,\Lambda})
and
(E,g_{2,\Lambda})
. By Proposition
A.1
they are both of Asymptotic Type
\sharp
. We next prove

Proposition A.2
.

There is a constant
C(\epsilon)
depending on
\epsilon
and
k
where
C(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, such that there are diffeomorphisms
\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}:E\to E
, where on the end
(\varpi_{1})_{*}g_{1,\Lambda}
and
(\varpi_{2})_{*}g_{2,\Lambda}
are smooth with Asymptotic Type
\sharp
and decay rate
\delta=1
, and with estimates

|\nabla^{k}_{g^{\sharp}}((\varpi_{1})_{*}g_{1,\Lambda}-(\varpi_{2})_{*}g_{2,\Lambda})|_{g^{\sharp}}<\frac{C(\epsilon)}{\mathfrak{r}^{k+1}}.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Proposition
A.1
. Again we prove the proposition for the
\AF_{0}
case. The diffeomorphisms
\varpi_{1}
and
\varpi_{2}
are exactly the ones constructed in the proof of Proposition
A.1
, but here we need to control the difference between them. Consider the modified rescaling

\Phi_{2^{j}}^{1}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u_{j}^{1}}+e^{-u_{j}^{1}}(v_{j}^{1})^{2}&e^{-u_{j}^{1}}v_{j}^{1}\\
e^{-u_{j}^{1}}v_{j}^{1}&e^{-u_{j}^{1}}\end{matrix}\right),\quad\Phi_{2^{j}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\begin{matrix}\rho^{2}e^{u_{j}^{2}}+e^{-u_{j}^{2}}(v_{j}^{2})^{2}&e^{-u_{j}^{2}}v_{j}^{2}\\
e^{-u_{j}^{2}}v_{j}^{2}&e^{-u_{j}^{2}}\end{matrix}\right)

defined on
A_{1/2,8}
. Then the condition (
A.5
) gives that on
A_{1/2,8}
,

d(\Phi^{1}_{2^{j}},\Phi^{2}_{2^{j}})\leq C\epsilon 2^{-j},

which implies

(A.6)

|u_{j}^{1}-u_{j}^{2}|+\rho^{-1}|v_{j}^{1}-v_{j}^{2}|\leq C\epsilon 2^{-j}.

Step (1). Comparison of local gauge in each annulus.

Under the augmentations
\varphi_{2^{-j}}^{*}\nu_{1},\varphi_{2^{-j}}^{*}\nu_{2}
with the lattice
\Lambda
there are the Ricci-flat metrics
h_{1},h_{2}
associated to
\Phi^{1}_{2^{j}},\Phi^{2}_{2^{j}}
on
E_{1/2,8}
. Since the harmonic map equation is non-degenerate over the sector region
A_{1/2,8}\cap Sec_{\tau}
, by (
A.6
) it is straightforward to conclude that

(A.7)

\|u_{j}^{1}-u_{j}^{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}\epsilon 2^{-j},\quad\|v_{j}^{1}-v_{j}^{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}\epsilon 2^{-j}.

Therefore on the region
U_{0,0}=\pi^{-1}(A_{1/2,8}\cap Sec_{\tau})
, under the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
, we already have

|\nabla^{k}_{g^{\AF_{0}}}(h_{1}-h_{2})|_{g^{\AF_{0}}}\leq\frac{C_{k}(\epsilon)}{2^{j}}

For the near rod region in
E_{1,4}
covered by the rest of
U_{t,s}
, we need to compare the harmonic coordinates

(A.8)

{h_{1}}^{kl}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{l}}\overline{x}_{j}^{1}+{h_{1}}^{kl}{\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{r}}\overline{x}_{j}^{1}=0,\quad\text{with }\overline{x}_{j}^{1}=x_{j}\text{ along }\partial U_{t,s},

(A.9)

{h_{2}}^{kl}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{l}}\overline{x}_{j}^{2}+{h_{2}}^{kl}{\Gamma_{2}}^{r}_{kl}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{r}}\overline{x}_{j}^{2}=0,\quad\text{with }\overline{x}_{j}^{2}=x_{j}\text{ along }\partial U_{t,s},

The solutions are in
W^{2,p}
hence
C^{1,\alpha}
on
U_{t,s}
.
Proposition
4.12
gives that on
U_{t,s}

(A.10)

|{h_{1}}_{kl}-{h_{2}}_{kl}|+|{\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}-{\Gamma_{2}}^{r}_{kl}|\leq\frac{C}{2^{j}}.

Moreover, on the overlap with the sector region
U_{t,s}\cap U_{0,0}
, the above closeness improves to all the higher derivatives

(A.11)

|\nabla^{k}({h_{1}}_{kl}-{h_{2}}_{kl})|\leq\frac{C_{k}(\epsilon)}{2^{j}}.

Combining (
A.10
)-(
A.11
) it follows

(A.12)

\displaystyle{h_{1}}^{kl}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{l}}(\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2})+{h_{1}}^{kl}{\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{r}}(\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle({h_{1}}^{kl}-{h_{2}}^{kl})\cdot A+({\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}-{\Gamma_{2}}^{r}_{kl})\cdot B,\quad\text{with }\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2}=0\text{ along }\partial U_{t,s}.

Here
A,B
are functions in
L^{p}
on
U_{t,s}
. Set the Green’s function for the Dirichlet problem for
{h_{1}}^{kl}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{l}}\cdot+{h_{1}}^{kl}{\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{r}}\cdot
on
U_{t,s}
as
\mathcal{G}
, then

(A.13)

\displaystyle\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \int_{U_{t,s}}(({h_{1}}^{kl}-{h_{2}}^{kl})\cdot A+({\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}-{\Gamma_{2}}^{r}_{kl})\cdot B)(y)\cdot\mathcal{G}(x,y)dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \int_{U_{t,s}\cap U_{0,0}}(({h_{1}}^{kl}-{h_{2}}^{kl})\cdot A+({\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}-{\Gamma_{2}}^{r}_{kl})\cdot B)(y)\cdot\mathcal{G}(x,y)dy

\displaystyle+\int_{U_{t,s}\setminus U_{0,0}}(({h_{1}}^{kl}-{h_{2}}^{kl})\cdot A+({\Gamma_{1}}^{r}_{kl}-{\Gamma_{2}}^{r}_{kl})\cdot B)(y)\cdot\mathcal{G}(x,y)dy.

So (
A.10
) and (
A.11
) give

|\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2}|\leq\frac{C(\epsilon)}{2^{j}}+\frac{C(\tau)}{2^{j}}.

Here
C(\tau)\to 0
as
\tau\to 0
. Hence, by choosing
\epsilon\ll\tau
the elliptic estimate gives that on
U_{t,s}

(A.14)

\|\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq\frac{C(\tau)}{2^{j}}.

The bound improves to

(A.15)

\|\overline{x}_{j}^{1}-\overline{x}_{j}^{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq{C_{k}(\tau)}{2^{-j}}

on the overlap
U_{t,s}\cap U_{0,0}
.
Since on
U_{t,s}
we have (
A.10
), the ellipticity implies that on
U_{t,s}

\|(\zeta_{t,s}^{1})_{*}h_{1}-(\zeta_{t,s}^{2})_{*}h_{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{2^{j}}.

However, since on
U_{t,s}\cap U_{0,0}
we already have (
A.7
), on
U_{t,s}\cap U_{0,0}
we must have

\|(\zeta_{t,s}^{1})_{*}h_{1}-(\zeta_{t,s}^{2})_{*}h_{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq{C(\tau)}{2^{-j}}.

Hence by making
\tau
small and taking integration the above improves to

\|(\zeta_{t,s}^{1})_{*}h_{1}-(\zeta_{t,s}^{2})_{*}h_{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq\frac{C_{k}(\tau)}{2^{j}}.

Step (2). Comparison of equivariant patched gauge.

As in the construction performed in
Step (2)
of Proposition
A.1
, we have the center-of-mass functions
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}
. As
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}
are almost invariant and the minimizing points for
y\mapsto{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}(x,y)
and
y\mapsto{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}(x,y)
only occur when
y
is close to
x
, for simplicity we only need to consider
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}
when
x
and
y
fall in a same
U_{t,s}
. Because of the
C(\tau)2^{-j}
-closeness between the harmonic coordinates and the smoothed metrics on
U_{t,s}
, we have

\|(\zeta_{t,s}^{1})_{*}\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}-(\zeta_{t,s}^{2})_{*}\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}(\tau)2^{-j},

where the
C^{k,\alpha}
norm is measured by the standard coordinate
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
. Here we need the push-forward by
\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{1},\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{2}
since only after the push-forward
h_{1},h_{2}
are smooth under
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
. When
x\in U_{0,0}
, because of (
A.11
) and (
A.15
), there exists a
\sigma:=\sigma(\tau)\gg\tau
that
\sigma\to 0
as
\tau\to 0
, such that when
x\in\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4}\cap Sec_{\sigma})
, whenever
\zeta_{t,s}(x)
makes sense,
\zeta_{t,s}(x)\in U_{0,0}
. Clearly when
x\in\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4}\cap Sec_{\sigma})
, for any
a\in\mathbb{T}
,
a\cdot x\in\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4}\cap Sec_{\sigma})
as well. Hence, when we minimize
y\mapsto\mathfrak{C}_{j}^{1}(x,y)
and
y\mapsto\mathfrak{C}_{j}^{2}(x,y)
, we only need to consider
y\in U_{0,0}
.

For
a\in\mathbb{T}
,
x\in\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4}\cap Sec_{\sigma})
, and
y\in U_{0,0}
, we have

\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}(a\cdot x,a\cdot y)-\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{1}(x,y)\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}(\tau)2^{-j},\quad\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}(a\cdot x,a\cdot y)-\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{j}^{2}(x,y)\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}(\tau)2^{-j}.

Hence

\|\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{j}(x,y)-\mathfrak{C}^{2}_{j}(x,y)\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}(\tau)2^{-j}.

This implies that on
\pi^{-1}(A_{1,4}\cap Sec_{\sigma})

\|(\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{1})_{*}h_{1}-(\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{2})_{*}h_{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C_{k}(\tau)2^{-j}.

Finally, since we obviously have

\|(\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{1})_{*}h_{1}-(\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{2})_{*}h_{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C2_{k}^{-j}

on the entire
E_{1,4}
, by taking integration we end with

\|(\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{1})_{*}h_{1}-(\mathfrak{z}_{j}^{2})_{*}h_{2}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}}\leq C(\tau)2^{-j}

on the entire
E_{1,4}
. One can similarly control the difference between gauges on adjacent annuli. Hence after scaling back we finished the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Mars-Simon uniqueness via positive mass theorem

Mars-Simon
[
MS99
]
proved a uniqueness theorem for certain gravitational instantons with an
\mathbb{R}
action without isolated fixed points. The proof uses the positive mass theorem and is based on the method of Bunting and Masood-ul-Alam in general relativity.
In this appendix we explain how to extend
[
MS99
]
to our setting in Section
8
of toric gravitational instantons, where in general isolated fixed points are allowed, by using a version of positive mass theorem with singularities.

Let
(M,g)
be a toric gravitational instanton that arises from an augmented harmonic map
(\Phi,\nu)
strongly tamed by an enhanced rod structure
(\Phi,\Lambda)
, where the Asymptotic Type of
\mathfrak{R}
is assumed to be
\AF_{0}/\ALFp/\ALFm
and the augmentation
\nu
is normalized. Without loss of generality, we assume the infinite rod vectors of
\mathfrak{R}
are
\bv_{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
and
\bv_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha\geq 0
.

Proposition B.1
.

Suppose the
S^{1}
action induced by
(0,1)^{\top}\in\Lie(\mathbb{T}^{2})
on
(M,g)
is semi-free (i.e., the action is free away from its fixed points), then either
(M,g)
is Type
\I
up to reversing the orientation, or
(M,g)
is isometric to a Taub-Bolt metric or a Schwarzschild metric.

Proof.

We follow closely the proof of
[
MS99
]
.
Denote the quotient of
(M,g)
by the
S^{1}
action as
(N,h)
. The latter is a manifold with smooth boundaries and isolated singularities modeled on cones. More precisely, a local consideration shows that each fixed
2
-sphere of the
S^{1}
action gives rise to a component of the boundary of
(N,h)
, and each isolated fixed point of the
S^{1}
action gives rise to a singularity modeled on
dr^{2}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}g_{S^{2}}
, with
g_{S^{2}}
being the round metric. The metric
h
is smooth away from the boundary and the isolated singularities.

Denote by
K
the Killing field associated to the
S^{1}
action chosen suitably such that
V:=|K|_{g}\to 1
at infinity. By the discussion in Section
2.1
there is an Ernst potential
1
1

1

In
[
MS99
]
the Ernst potential is denoted as
\omega
and the Killing field
K
is denoted as
\xi
.

\mathcal{E}
such that

d\mathcal{E}=*(\eta\wedge d\eta)

with
\eta=K^{\flat}
. From the assumption on the
S^{1}
action and the asymptotic model, we know that
(N,h)
is asymptotically flat at infinity, in the sense that

h=g_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+O(r^{-\delta}),

where
\mathfrak{r}
is the distance to a base point and
\delta>0
.

A computation shows (see (11)-(13) of
[
MS99
]
) that on
(N,h)

(B.1)

\displaystyle\Ric_{ij}

\displaystyle=V^{-1}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}V-\frac{1}{2}V^{-4}(\mathcal{E}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{j}-h_{ij}h^{kl}\mathcal{E}_{k}\mathcal{E}_{l}),

(B.2)

\displaystyle\Delta V

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}V^{-3}h^{ij}\mathcal{E}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{j},

(B.3)

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=3V^{-1}h^{ij}\mathcal{E}_{i}V_{j},

and the scalar curvature of
(N,h)
is

(B.4)

s=\frac{3}{2}V^{-4}g^{ij}\mathcal{E}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{j}\geq 0.

Since near infinity,
(N,h)
is clearly asymptotically flat, we know that by adjusting
\mathcal{E}
with a suitable constant we can assume that

\mathcal{E}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-\epsilon}).

Then we consider the conformal change
\gamma:=V^{2}h

and let

(B.5)

\displaystyle v_{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1-V^{2}\mp\mathcal{E}}{1+V^{2}\pm\mathcal{E}},

(B.6)

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle:=1-v_{-}v_{+}=\frac{4V^{2}}{(1+V^{2}+\mathcal{E})(1+V^{2}-\mathcal{E})}.

Another computation (see
[
MS99
]
Lemma 3-5) shows that
\gamma
is again asymptotically flat at infinity. Let

(B.7)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{\Theta}}{2\sqrt{\Theta}},

(B.8)

\displaystyle g^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\Omega_{\pm}^{2}\gamma=\frac{1}{16}(\sqrt{(1+V^{2})^{2}-\mathcal{E}^{2}}\pm 2V)^{2}h.

Define

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}:=V^{2}\pm\mathcal{E}.

Then a computation shows that

(B.9)

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}\mathcal{E}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=V^{-2}|\nabla_{g}\mathcal{E}_{\pm}|_{g}^{2}\geq 0.

Combining with
\mathcal{E}=O(\mathfrak{r}^{-\epsilon})
and by applying the maximum principle on
M
, know that either
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}<1
or
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}\equiv 1
. Moreover, notice the identity
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}=2V^{2}-\mathcal{E}_{\mp}
, we conclude that
-1<\mathcal{E}_{\pm}\leq 1
and

(1)

either at least one of
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
satisfies that
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}\equiv 1
or

(2)

both
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
satisfies
-1<\mathcal{E}_{\pm}<1
.

This also justifies that

\Theta=\frac{4V^{2}}{(1+\mathcal{E}_{\pm})(1+\mathcal{E}_{\mp})}\leq 1,

and the equality holds only when case (1) occurs. By considering the asymptotic expansion at infinity, one can show that
(N,g^{+})
is still asymptotically flat but with vanishing mass at infinity. For
(N,g^{-})
one can show that the metric
g^{-}
is incomplete at infinity, but it can be compactified by adding one point at infinity to a
C^{2}
metric, with resulting manifold denoted as
(\widetilde{N},\widetilde{g^{-}})
. Both
g^{+}
and
\widetilde{g^{-}}
have non-negative scalar curvature.

Now, if we are in case (1), as in the first part of the proof of Theorem 1 in
[
MS99
]
, then
\gamma
is locally flat and
(X,g)
is precisely locally given by the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz. We then know it is Type
\I
and there is an explicit description from Section
7
.

If we are in case (2), as in the second part of the proof of Theorem 1 in
[
MS99
]
, by local consideration near the boundary of
N
, we can patch
(N,g^{+})
and
(\widetilde{N},\widetilde{g^{-}})
together, with the resulting manifold denoted as
(\mathcal{N},g_{0})
. The key is that by computation along boundary of
(N,g^{\pm})
the second fundamental form matches, which implies that
g_{0}
is
C^{1,1}
near the patched boundary. It may also have isolated singularities modeled on the cone
dr^{2}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}g_{S^{2}}
, and is
C^{1,1}
at the added point in
\widetilde{N}
. The space
(\mathcal{N},g_{0})
is smooth elsewhere, with only one asymptotically flat end and zero mass at infinity. A computation gives that
(\mathcal{N},g_{0})
also has non-negative scalar curvature (see (38) in
[
MS99
]
). Hence by the positive mass theorem with isolated singularities (see for example Theorem 1.1 in
[
DSW24
]
), we know
(N,g^{\pm})
has to be flat. Starting from this, a more detailed computation gives that
(X,g)
must be Taub-Bolt or Schwarzschild (this is the last paragraph of the proof to Theorem 1 in
[
MS99
]
).
∎
